# Halo: Combat Evolved on Bevy (Solari + DLSS) — Roadmap

Goal: run Halo: CE — from the [OpenCE](opence) decompilation (Xbox build 2342) and the retail
Xbox maps — with Bevy as the renderer, lit by Solari real-time ray tracing and DLSS Ray
Reconstruction.

## Starting point (what OpenCE is)

| Fact | Consequence |
| --- | --- |
| ~500k lines of decompiled C (AI 79k, rasterizer 44k, interface 42k, bitmaps 37k, game 32k, hs 28k, units 25k, …) plus a 95k-line port layer (`port/linux`). | A line-by-line Rust rewrite is years of work, so the port has to be incremental. |
| A **32-bit x86** program. Tag data is loaded at the fixed Xbox address `0x803A6000`, and every tag struct holds 32-bit pointers. | The game code can't be linked into a 64-bit process without rewriting every tag struct. The DLSS SDK only ships 64-bit libraries. |
| The port renders by **emulating Direct3D 8 + NV2A shaders on OpenGL** (`d3d8_gl.c`, `nv2a_psh.c`, `nv2a_vsh.c`). | That layer only sees triangles and textures, not a scene. Ray tracing needs scene data, so Bevy has to hook in *above* the rasterizer: game state → Bevy entities. |
| Retail maps are zlib-compressed Xbox cache files (`head` … `foot`, version 5). | The map format is well defined by OpenCE's headers. A native Rust loader is practical. |
| OpenCE's own code is CC0. The game data is Microsoft's. | We never ship or commit game data. The user supplies `maps/` (OpenCE extracts it from a disc image). |

## Architecture

```
            ┌──────────────────────── 64-bit Bevy process ────────────────────────┐
 maps/*.map │ halo_map (Rust)          halo_rt (Bevy app)                         │
 ─────────► │  cache file ─► tags ─►   BSP / models / bitmaps / shaders ─► meshes, │
            │  (layout generated       StandardMaterial, lights ─► Solari + DLSS   │
            │   from OpenCE headers)                                               │
            │                          ▲ per-frame game state (Phase 4)            │
            └──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                       │ shared memory: camera, objects, node
                                       │ matrices, effects   ◄── input
            ┌──────────────────────────┴───── 32-bit OpenCE process ──────────────┐
            │ OpenCE simulation (AI, physics, scripts, network) with rasterizer   │
            │ calls replaced by a render-state exporter                           │
            └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

- **`crates/halo_map`**: a pure-Rust, Bevy-free reader for Xbox cache files.
  - Struct offsets are **generated from OpenCE's own C headers** by `tools/tag_layout` (clang, i686 MSVC ABI), so they are the compiler's numbers rather than hand-counted ones.
  - The generated file is committed, so OpenCE isn't needed to build.
- **`halo_rt`**: the Bevy app. It converts tags into Bevy assets and entities and owns rendering, the camera and the UI.
- **The simulation bridge** (Phase 4): OpenCE runs headless in its own 32-bit process and streams render state to Bevy. This gets the whole game playable without first rewriting 400k lines of game logic. Individual subsystems can still move to Rust later (Phase 7) behind the same interface.

Coordinates: Halo is Z-up and measured in world units (1 wu = 10 ft = 3.048 m). Bevy is Y-up and measured in metres. The mapping is `bevy = (x, z, -y) * 3.048`.

---

## Phase 1: Map loader + static level viewer ✅ (done)

Deliverable: `cargo run --release --features dlss -- b30` flies a free camera through The Silent Cartographer's level geometry, textured and lit by Solari with DLSS-RR.

- [x] Workspace layout: `halo_map` library, `halo_rt` app; the Solari demo moved to `examples/`
- [x] `tools/tag_layout`: generates struct offsets/sizes from OpenCE headers → `crates/halo_map/src/layout.rs`
- [x] Cache file: header check, zlib decompression, tag index, address → file-offset translation per image (tag data and each BSP)
- [x] Tag access: by datum, group or name; `tag_block` / `tag_reference` / `tag_data` readers with bounds checks
- [x] Scenario → structure BSP references → BSP header → `structure_bsp`
- [x] BSP geometry: lightmaps → materials → compressed vertices (11:11:10 normals) + surfaces, winding fixed for Bevy
- [x] Bitmaps: Xbox swizzle, DXT1/3/5 passthrough as BC1/2/3 with mips, A8R8G8B8 / X8R8G8B8 / R5G6B5 / A1R5G5B5 / A4R4G4B4 / A8 / Y8 / AY8 / A8Y8 → RGBA8 (P8 bump, cube and 3D textures moved to phase 3)
- [x] Shaders: base maps → `StandardMaterial`; shaders with radiosity emission → Solari emissive lights
- [x] Sky: the sun → directional light; the hemisphere sky light → environment light + skybox (a stand-in for skies without one)
- [x] Bevy viewer: meshes + `RaytracingMesh3d`, free camera at the player start, Solari, DLSS-RR, map/BSP selection, live light tuning, `--screenshot`
- [x] `halo_map` CLI: info / tags / bsp (stats + winding check) / shaders / sky / bitmaps (decode-all) / bitmap → PNG

Checked on b30 (2 BSPs, 58k triangles), a10 (9 BSPs, 364k triangles) and bloodgulch. All of their 2D bitmaps decode. Frames take about 2 ms of GPU time at 1280×720 with DLSS-RR on an RTX 5080.

### What Phase 1 taught us (format notes)

- Built maps have the `_bitmap_cached_bit` set, which means `pixels_offset` is already absolute. Adding the group's `pixel_data.file_offset` again (as `texture_cache_bitmap_new` does at build time) breaks every texture.
- BSP triangles are clockwise when seen from the front (100% of triangles in every BSP checked), so the viewer swaps two indices per triangle.
- The decompiled `sky_light` leaves colour, power and direction in `unused30`. They sit at the Halo Editing Kit offsets 0x50, 0x5C and 0x68.
- The Xbox build has no `shader_transparent_chicago_extended`, so the shader types run: environment 3, model 4, generic 5, chicago 6, water 7, glass 8, meter 9, plasma 10.
- Halo's light fixtures are mostly transparent-generic (`sotr`) shaders with radiosity emission. Drawn opaque and emissive, they light interiors on their own.

## Phase 2: World content and real lighting ✅ (done)

Deliverable: levels populated with their scenery, vehicles, weapons and devices; Halo's own sky behind them; light fixtures and self-illuminated surfaces lighting the scene.

- [x] Scenario placements for scenery, bipeds, vehicles, equipment, weapons, machines, controls and light fixtures, filtered as the game does (not-automatic flag, `on_bsp_flags`; multiplayer vehicles with no BSP flags are shown)
- [x] Models (`mode`): Xbox compressed vertices read through the tag header's D3D vertex buffers, triangle strips unrolled, regions/permutations (the scenario's choice), highest LOD, nodes; meshes made once per (model, permutation) and instanced
- [x] Object placement: the game's yaw/pitch/roll matrix (`matrix4x3_rotation_from_angles`) converted to Bevy
- [x] `shader_model` base map with the model's and shader's UV scales
- [x] Self-illumination: model shaders (multipurpose green mask × base map × colour) and environment shaders (self-illumination map R/G/B × primary/secondary/plasma colours × base map) baked into emissive textures; radiosity emitters keep their radiosity power
- [x] The sky model as camera-centred, raster-only, unlit, alpha-blended layers in Halo's order (each layer's main map for now)
- [x] Light fixtures (scenario colour × intensity) as invisible emissive spheres in the ray-traced scene, since Solari has no point lights
- [x] Tooling: `--camera` / P to reproduce views, `--no-objects`, `--no-fixture-lights`, `--show-light-proxies`; CLI `objects [list]` decodes every placed model
- [ ] Moved to later phases: sky layers captured into the environment cubemap (phase 3); `ligh` lights on objects and effects (phase 4/5, where they move with objects); the "baked" lightmap comparison mode (optional, phase 3); physically calibrated light units (phase 3)

Checked on b30 (916 objects, 170k triangles), a10 (466 objects, 245k triangles) and bloodgulch. Every placed model decodes, normals are unit length and 99.97% of triangles wind clockwise like the BSPs. GPU time is about 2 ms per frame with DLSS-RR.

### What Phase 2 taught us

- Xbox model parts leave their tag blocks empty. The geometry is reached through `vertex_buffer.hardware_format`, a D3D resource `{Common, Data, Lock}` in the tag header's array whose `Data` is a tag-space address (the game registers it with base `NULL`). `triangle_buffer.base_address` is the strip itself.
- Model vertices are in model space at the default pose (`render_model` multiplies node matrices by `runtime_default_inverse_matrix`), so static objects need only their object transform.
- On Xbox the multipurpose map's channels are R specular, G self-illumination, B colour change, A auxiliary. Halo PC moves them, and the port swizzles Custom Edition maps to match.
- Environment self-illumination adds to the lightmap, so it lights the base map rather than replacing it.
- Many light fixtures and glowing panels emit radiosity *and* self-illuminate. The radiosity power is their real light output; using the self-illumination colour instead made a10 go dark.

## Phase 3: Material fidelity ✅ (done)

Deliverable: Halo's materials as the game draws them. Solari lights the surfaces; the camera sees Halo's own shading layers.

The architecture: Solari traces rays against `StandardMaterial` only, but takes each pixel's material from the G-buffer the rasterizer writes. So a surface with a Halo material is two entities. A raster-only one carries a material extension or custom material (what the camera sees). A ray-traced-only one carries the plain `StandardMaterial` (what bounces, shadows and reflections see).

- [x] `shader_environment` detail layers: the Xbox pixel shader reproduced in WGSL (primary/secondary detail mixed by secondary or base alpha, the three detail functions, micro detail, gamma-space combine, rescaled detail maps)
- [x] Bump maps: palettized (P8) bump maps decoded through the renderer's vector palette into tangent-space normal maps, used at their own scale on the camera's surfaces and on the ray-traced ones where the UVs line up
- [x] Alpha test: environment shaders cut out by the bump map's alpha (the palette's zero-alpha entries), as the Xbox alpha-tests the bump pass
- [x] Specular and reflection: reflection brightness and specular masks (environment base alpha, model multipurpose red) become roughness and metalness, baked into a metallic-roughness texture where masked (`--matte` turns this off)
- [x] Transparent chicago and generic shaders: the Xbox preprocessors ported to build each shader's NV2A register combiner program; a WGSL interpreter evaluates it with the port's semantics. Covers holograms, glows, shields, decal-like surfaces, waves and the sky
- [x] Glass and water: blended, smooth surfaces with their tint and base maps that reflect the sky
- [x] The sky: its layers composited in order with Halo's combiners, and baked on the CPU (a Rust combiner evaluator plus ray casts) into the environment cubemap that lights the level
- [ ] Moved later: texture animation (UV scrolling, chicago map animation); reflection cube maps on transparent and environment shaders; meter and plasma shaders; detail maps on model shaders; light-unit calibration and a baked-lightmap comparison mode; 3D textures

On b30, every transparent surface is drawn except one meter and one plasma shader; on a10, all of them are. GPU time is still about 2 ms per frame with DLSS-RR.

### What Phase 3 taught us

- The environment shader's diffuse is `f(base, mix(secondary, primary, w))`, then the micro detail, with `f` one of `2ab`, `ab` or `a + 2b - 1`. A missing detail map is the game's 50% grey default, which is neutral for the "biased" functions.
- Halo's alpha test isn't the base map's alpha. The Xbox alpha-tests the pass where texture 0 is the bump map, and the P8 vector palette has seven zero-alpha entries for the cut-outs.
- The combiner programs are small and fully data-driven. Porting the two preprocessors and interpreting NV2A combiners covers every chicago and generic shader at once.
- Bevy sorts transparent meshes by the centres of their bounds, not their origins. Sky layers need a large negative depth bias (so they draw before the level's transparents) plus a step per layer bigger than the sky.
- A custom `Material` culls back faces by default. Sky layers are seen from inside, so they must be double-sided.

## Phase 4: Simulation bridge (playable) ✅ (done)

Deliverable: `cargo run --release --features dlss -- b30 --opence <OpenCE>\build\windows\halo.exe` starts the game hidden and plays it in the Bevy window, ray traced.

OpenCE side, branch `bevy-bridge` (now the subtree `opence/`):
- [x] `port/linux/game/bevy_bridge.c`: with `game.bevy_bridge` on, `render_frame` writes the first player's camera and every object's interpolated node matrices, definition, region permutations and visibility to shared memory (`bevy_bridge_protocol.h`; two frame buffers with sequence locks; a named file mapping on Windows, POSIX shm elsewhere)
- [x] The first-person weapon and hands, recorded as `first_person_weapon_draw` poses them
- [x] Input from the renderer: `bevy_bridge_input.c` turns the forwarded keyboard and mouse into SDL events before the platform layer polls, so bindings, menus and aiming work unchanged
- [x] `game.bevy_bridge_map`: a level to load at start-up

Viewer side:
- [x] `crates/halo_bridge`: the protocol mirrored in Rust, snapshot reading and input writing; `bridge_probe` (with `walk`) checks it end to end without a window
- [x] `--bridge`: the game's objects, spawned as they appear, split by node and posed every frame at `node matrix × default inverse` (the tag's baked `runtime_default_inverse_matrix`), hidden as the game hides them, despawned when deleted; the camera follows the game's (F8 flies free)
- [x] Playing from the viewer: click to grab the mouse, F12 to let go; the viewer's hotkeys pause while playing
- [x] `--opence <halo.exe>`: starts the game hidden on the map's scenario and stops it with the viewer; `--show-game` keeps its window
- [x] Frame interpolation comes from the game (`render_interpolation.c`): it exports at display rate
- [ ] Moved to phase 5: true two-node skinning (bipeds use a rigid per-node split now, with small seams at joints); change colours; shader animation from object functions (the rifle's ammo digits); effects, decals and dynamic lights; showing only the active BSP

Checked: b30's opening cutscene (Pelicans, the troop bay landing), first-person gameplay with the assault rifle, a10's opening. A scripted walk through the bridge moves the player. Bridged starts are stable: 8 of 8 a10 starts clean with DLSS-RR running.

### What Phase 4 taught us

- OpenCE already blends node matrices and the camera between ticks for display-rate frames. Exporting from `render_frame` gets those blended poses for free.
- Halo's node default inverse matrices are baked into the cache file, so the renderer needs no skeleton maths of its own.
- Injecting SDL events is the narrowest input seam. Nothing in the game or the port's input code had to change.
- DLSS creates its feature with GPU work that wgpu doesn't track. If that happens during the window's first surface configure, wgpu's idle wait fails and Bevy quits. Starting the camera 30 frames in avoids the race. Adding DLSS to a camera that's already rendering doesn't work: it stays off.

## Phase 5: Effects, HUD and presentation ✅ (done)

The approach: the game draws everything that isn't opaque world, and the viewer composites it. With `game.bevy_bridge_overlay` (OpenCE branch `bevy-bridge-overlay`), `render_window` draws the opaque world only into the depth buffer. It draws the rest over a clear screen, keeping coverage in alpha: alpha blends add coverage, additive blends leave it. `Present` reads the picture back into the bridge (protocol 4). The viewer draws it with premultiplied blending, paired with the world frame it was drawn for. Effects are depth-tested against the game's own copy of the world, so they're occluded correctly by the geometry the viewer ray-traces.

- [x] The overlay: protocol 4, the depth-only world, coverage alpha, the readback; `src/overlay.rs` composites it and pairs frames; the viewer skips its own transparent surfaces; `--no-overlay` turns it off
- [x] Through the overlay: the HUD, menus and text; particles, contrails, decals, lens flares, weather; fog and water; transparent surfaces; cinematic letterboxing
- [x] Fixed on the way: the camera's field of view in cinematics. The game's frustum is wider than its camera's FOV there, because the window bounds sit inside the viewport. The bridge now shares the frustum's FOV, and cutscene objects no longer look 18% too close.
Carried over, then done in the rendering pass (branch `rendering-pass`, OpenCE `bevy-bridge-rendering`, protocol 9):

- [x] The rifle's ammo digits: they are transparent chicago shaders (numbers, compass, icons), drawn with the first-person weapon inside `render_objects`, so the overlay's depth-only world swallowed them. Its blended draws now go to the overlay. Change colours were done in phase 6. Opaque shaders animated by object functions (pulsing Covenant lights) are still static.
- [x] Two-node skinning: each model part is one skinned mesh (Halo's two nodes and weights per vertex), with the node entities as joints. Solari traces no skinned meshes, so the rays keep the rigid pieces, 2 cm inside the skinned surface to avoid self-shadowing blotches. The jagged shards under the first-person rifle are gone.
- [x] Only the active BSP is shown, along with its placed light fixtures. Solari traces every `RaytracingMesh3d` whether visible or not, so hidden meshes now leave the ray-traced scene (`TracedMesh`).
- [x] Active camouflage: drawn as a faint glassy shell, out of the ray-traced scene (no shadow). Under the overlay the game neither draws its shimmer, which refracts a picture it no longer has, nor the camouflaged model.
- [x] Dynamic lights: up to 64 a frame from the game's light system (muzzle flashes, plasma, explosions, the flashlight), each lighting an emissive proxy the rays see: a sphere, or a disc for a spotlight.
- [x] Opaque-world animation the overlay can't carry, for environment shaders: their self-illumination (primary and secondary colours between "off" and "on"; the plasma band sweeping through the map's alpha) and texture scrolling, by the game's periodic functions (`halo_environment.wesl`). The tables are rebuilt as the game builds them (identical, all 12,288 bytes) and the plasma band comes from the game's own combiner program.
- [x] Objects' self-illumination (model shaders): their colour between the lower and upper bounds by the shader's periodic function, at each object's own phase (seeded by its object index, as the game seeds it), times its change colour when the shader says; each bridged object gets its own copy of such materials
- [x] Model shaders' detail maps: masked by a multipurpose channel and combined with the base map by the shader's function, in the camera's material (the environment material's model mode)
- [ ] Model shaders' texture animation, whose sources can be object functions (not shared by the bridge); transparent meter and plasma shaders and 3D textures in the map-only viewer (in play the game's overlay draws them). Reflection cube maps are left out on purpose: the ray tracer reflects the scene itself
- [x] Multiply-blended effects (protocol 31): the game draws them to a picture of their own over the same depth buffer, cleared to 0.5, on which its own blends compute half their factor; the viewer multiplies its world by twice it under the overlay (scorch marks and other darkening or brightening decals)

Checked: b30's opening cutscene (letterbox, engine glows, water, fog) lined up with the ray-traced Pelicans; b30 gameplay with the full HUD; a10's opening with the Pillar of Autumn's engine glows. 4 of 4 a10 overlay starts were clean.

### What Phase 5 taught us

- Letting the game draw its own effects over a depth-only world is far cheaper than porting each effect system, and it stays faithful: the effects are the game's own.
- Coverage alpha only works for blends that replace or add to what's under them. Multiplying blends need the world's colour, which the game no longer has.
- Compare the two renderers directly: a half-strength blend of the game's full picture over the viewer's found the cinematic field-of-view bug in one screenshot.

## Phase 6: Audio and multiplayer parity ✅ (done)

OpenCE branch `bevy-bridge-multiplayer` (protocol 6).

- [x] Sound: the hidden game already plays it (OpenCE's mixer: Xbox ADPCM, 3D positioning, occlusion, I3DL2 reverb). The bridge's input block now carries a listener, so with the viewer flying free (F8) the game hears from the viewer's camera.
- [x] Level changes: the viewer follows the game's level (the menus, the next campaign level, each multiplayer game). It unloads the old level (everything sits under one root entity) and loads the new map, with its sky and sun. `--menu` starts the game at its menus, which the overlay shows and the forwarded input drives.
- [x] A bridge per game (`game.bevy_bridge_name`): each viewer's game shares under its own name and writes its own log, so several viewers run side by side
- [x] System link through the bridge. Checked with OpenCE's network test harness on one machine: one viewer's game hosted Blood Gulch and the other's joined. Both games logged the same player positions every tick, and each viewer showed its own player's view, with the other player drawn.
- [x] Change colours (from phase 5): the bridge shares each object's four change colours, and the first-person arms' and weapon's. Model shaders tint their base maps where the multipurpose map's alpha says, as the Xbox shader does, so armour, team colours and Blood Gulch's autumn trees are right.
- [x] Options UI (F10): DLSS Ray Reconstruction and quality, ReSTIR, light levels, exposure and the stats text, saved between runs. (A run that found no DLSS libraries, such as a build run in place, saved Ray Reconstruction off for every later run; every run now finds them, and one without DLSS keeps the saved choice.)
- [ ] Internet play (OpenCE's invite links) through a viewer: untested

### What Phase 6 taught us

- The hidden game already did most of the work: its own mixer plays positioned sound, and its own menus drive multiplayer through the overlay. The viewer's job was to follow it: reloading levels, a bridge per game, and the camera as the listener.
- OpenCE's network test harness (`HALO_NETWORK_TEST`, loopback addresses per game) makes a two-machine system link check scriptable on one PC.

## Phase 7 (long term): Native Rust subsystems  ← *in progress*

Move subsystems out of the 32-bit process one at a time behind the bridge interface, ordered by payoff:

1. Camera and input ✅ (OpenCE branch `bevy-bridge-native-input`, protocol 7)
   - [x] Input is the viewer's: keyboard and mouse (phase 4), and now gamepads. Bevy reads the controllers and the bridge forwards up to four in SDL's layout. While the viewer owns them, the game's XInput layer takes them instead of physical pads, which a hidden window never receives. The game's rumble comes back and plays on the controller.
   - [x] Camera: measured, and kept in the game. The game's shared camera follows the mouse within one frame (median 7.5 ms, at most 9.1 ms, at 144 frames a second; `bridge_probe latency`). A Rust camera predicting the aim could save only that. It would also put the game's world-anchored overlay (water, effects, decals, drawn with the game's camera) out of step with the scene on fast turns.
2. Objects and physics ✅ (branch `phase-7-collision`, OpenCE `bevy-bridge-collision`, protocol 10)

   Each piece is a port of the game's own code, checked against the running game through the bridge before the next one is built on it.
   - [x] Collision BSP and the ray test (`halo_map::collision`, `collision_bsp_test_vector`): 483,000 rays through b30 and a10 agree with the game bit for bit (`bridge_probe <map file>` with `HALO_BEVY_BRIDGE_RAY_TESTS=1`)
   - [x] Sphere and pill tests (`collision_bsp_test_sphere`, `collision_bsp_test_pill`): the surfaces, edges and vertices near a point, and where a sweeping sphere first touches the structure, as movement uses them. The same fan of rays checks them: 414,000 spheres (34 million features touched) and 483,000 sweeps in b30 and a10 match the game exactly. Feature lists match in order; sweeps match bit for bit in t, surface and normal. One detail mattered: C's `fabs` is the double one, so the game computes a sweep's plane contact time in double.
   - [x] Object collision models (`collision_model_test_vector`): a ray against an object's collision BSPs, one per node and region permutation, in each node's space (the game's own `matrix4x3_inverse` and transforms). 141,000 rays that hit Pelicans, Marines, crew, doors and cryo tubes in b30 and a10 match the game bit for bit: object, node, BSP, surface and t.
   - [x] The game's whole ray test (`collision_test_vector`) with its object broad phase (`collision::World`). The structure ray's recorded leaves give the clusters to search. Each connected object with a collision model is registered in the clusters its bounding sphere reaches through portals (`structure_clusters_in_sphere`, `sphere_intersects_cluster_portal`, `convex_hull2d_test_circle`). Objects are tested once each, children inside their parent's sphere, nearest hit winning. 483,000 whole tests in b30 and a10 match the game bit for bit (134,000 of them object hits). The objects' bounding spheres and location clusters still come from the game (protocol 14), until the object simulation moves.
   - [x] Water in the whole ray test: each cluster's fog plane, moved by its fog's plane distance, as `collision_cluster_fog_plane` finds it; and the test's full result (what it hit, the plane facing the ray, the point, the location)
   - [x] Vehicles' physics shapes in the ray test (`halo_map::vehicle_physics`, `physics_instance_new` and `physics_test_vector`, through `collision::World` with `test_flags::USE_VEHICLE_PHYSICS`). A vehicle's physics tag's mass points, placed by its origin and axes and moved back by the centre of mass, are tested as spheres instead of its collision model. The game itself sets the bit only for debugging (`collision_debug_flag_use_vehicle_physics`), so the bridge tests rays from the camera at each vehicle with it and records the vehicles' placement (protocol 26). 378,600 such rays in b30 and a10 agree with the game bit for bit in what they hit, t and plane, 47,682 of them on b30's vehicles' mass points.
   - [x] Point physics (`halo_map::physics`, `point_physics_update`): the first simulation in Rust, which moves particles, contrails, antennas and flags. It covers gravity and buoyancy in air or water, friction towards the wind, and up to three bounces a step off the structure and water. Each frame the game launches 16 points from the camera, and Rust runs the same 90 steps. 552,000 points through 9.9 million bounces in b30 and a10 (11,000 of them into b30's water) end on exactly the game's position and velocity, bit for bit. Wind is an input; its runtime state is still the game's.
   - [x] Projectile flight (`halo_map::projectile`, `projectile_update`): timers, deceleration, gravity and range, flight through the structure and water, and each material's response (disappear, detonate, reflect, overpenetrate, attach) with its noise and friction, using the game's random numbers. The game records every projectile update along with the random seeds it began with, and Rust replays them from the same state. 6,700 updates of 1,400 projectiles in b30 (790 collisions) leave exactly the game's state, bit for bit.
   - [x] Projectiles against objects and guided projectiles (`halo_map::projectile`). Projectiles fly through objects, through `collision::World` with the objects their ray tests visited, as the game records them. Object hits get their material type from the collision model's damage materials (`collision_model_get_material_type`). An object a projectile damages reports back a material type, which the projectile's response uses. Responses can be limited to units. A projectile can pass through an object, ignoring it afterwards, or stick to one in the hit node's space, as a child (`object_attach_to_node`). Needles count those already stuck in the object for a super-combining explosion. Guided projectiles steer toward the target's centre of mass, wandering about it until close (the game's wander table, `periodic_function_evaluate`), scaled by the difficulty against players. The game records each update's objects, what damage reported and the random seed it left, and each guided step's inputs (protocol 27). 7,983 updates in b30, a30, d40 and c10 agree bit for bit, including 676 touching an object, 700 damaging one, 2 sticking to one and 520 steering. Not exercised: super-combining.
   - [x] Damage (`halo_map::damage`, `object_cause_damage` with `object_damage_shield` and `object_damage_body`). The roll from the effect's range (the game's random numbers), scaled by the attacker's AI, the gametype or the difficulty; the objects it reaches (the object, the parents it rides in, its umbrella shield) and a vehicle's riders, through the passthrough penalty and child damage fraction; friendly fire, the unsuspecting, and killing instantly; then each object's collision model's resistance (read from the tags: materials, regions, the node's damage part), its shield (leak, the failure curve, over-charging, depletion, stun) and its body (friendly resistance, headshots, region damage and destruction, death, the destroyed threshold), and the material effect it records. What damage does to the rest of the game goes through a `DamageHost`: the network's authority, teams, difficulty, players' screen effects, depleting and destroying, damage effects and the aftermath. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Area damage (`halo_map::area_damage`, `area_of_effect_cause_damage` with `area_of_effect_cause_damage_to_object`). The objects within the effect's cutoff radius (`objects_in_sphere`: the clusters the sphere reaches through portals, each one's collideable then noncollideable objects, each once, then their bounding spheres). A unit, when the effect has a core radius, is reached if any of four rays spread about the line to it gets through; another object if the line itself does (the structure alone: the game's flags leave out the objects bit). The owner and friends may be spared, and infection forms popped (the difficulty's toughness, from the game's random numbers). Each object reached gets the damage's direction and its falloff scale, and a collision model whose children take area damage passes it to them and their siblings. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Damage over time (`halo_map::damage_update`, `object_damage_update`, each tick for each object with a collision model). An act of god's death (the game's falling damage, killing instantly), the shield's over-charge (rising to three times its vitality, or draining above full in a gametype), its recharge once its stun runs out (the collision model's recharge velocity, scaled by the difficulty for its team, with the recharging effect when it was depleted), and the decay of the current and recent body and shield damage. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Device updates (`halo_map::devices`, devices.c's `device_update` and device_machines.c's `machine_update`; controls' and light fixtures' own updates do nothing). A device's power and position move toward their device groups' values (`accelerate_to_position`), the position waiting out the definition's delay from closed and making its start, stop and delay effects. A platform turns round at its velocity. A door opens for the bipeds near it every fourth tick (`objects_in_sphere` over the collideable partition, shared with area damage; a closed one-sided door only from behind for the player's side) and closes once it has stood open long enough. An elevator carries the bipeds riding it by its elevator node's motion, and a machine that moved is translated. The definitions' motion, effects and machine fields come from new layout constants. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Item updates (`halo_map::items`, items.c's `item_update`, every item's: weapons', equipment's and garbage's, before their own). A dropped item, free in the world: kept upright if its definition says so; falling under gravity (unless antigravity) along its line through the world (the whole ray test with the items' flags); bouncing off what it hits, or coming to rest on a gentle enough slope of the structure, scenery or a device, aligned there by its "ground point" marker (`item_align_to_normal_and_point`); at rest, riding the object it lies on, or falling again when its surface breaks or its object goes; spinning by its angular velocity (`rotate_vector_about_axis`); and counting down to its detonation. New with it: model markers (`halo_map::markers`: `model_find_marker`'s binary search with `_stricmp`, `model_get_marker_by_name`, `object_get_marker_by_name`, `object_align_marker_to_matrix`), and the point test (`collision_test_point`: the structure's solid, objects' collision models in their nodes' space, vehicles' mass points). Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Weapon updates (`halo_map::weapons`, weapons.c's `weapon_update` with `weapon_trigger_can_fire_again`). Each tick, for each weapon: its animation (`animation_update_internal`, now shared from `halo_map::unit_animation`), whose key frames and ends move its state on; detonating when dropped if it does; its integrated light dimming unless its unit controls it; its heat (overheating, cooling slowed by age, recovering); which triggers are down (the controls, the secondary trigger's mode, analog, sticky and toggling triggers); reloading; each magazine's recharge and its reload and chamber timers; and each trigger's state machine (idle, spewing, overloading, charging, charged, recovering, tracking, locked), its rate of fire with its blurred permutation, and its error. A charged trigger with no magazine reads the slot just before the magazines, as the C does: the low half of the second trigger's charging effect. The weapon's helpers (firing, reloading, chambering, charging, effects, messages) remain the game's. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] The object update loop (`halo_map::objects_update`, objects.c's `objects_update` and `object_update`), with garbage's and scenery's own updates (`garbage_update`, `scenery_update`; equipment has none). Each tick: the players' view activates and deactivates objects; every active top-level object is updated (only players' units on a double-speed game's odd ticks); then each object's do-not-update flag is cleared, objects created this tick get their first update, and those deleted are deleted; and garbage is collected. An object's update counts garbage, steps its animation's interpolation, runs its type's update and its damage over time, exports and computes its function values, computes its node matrices (unless told not to), change colours and lights, then updates its children and (a child's) next sibling, and postprocesses its node matrices. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Function values and change colours (`halo_map::object_functions`, objects.c's `object_compute_function_values` and `object_compute_change_colors`, with `periodic_function_evaluate`, `transition_function_evaluate` and `rgb_colors_interpolate`). Each of an object's functions (four at most) is a periodic function of the time at the object's own phase, its period and value scaled by other functions, inverted, wobbled, squared off, stepped, sawtoothed, added to and scaled, then mapped through a transition function and its bounds, with whether it is active. The C tests the function's "additive" bit through a bounds mode's name, and the port keeps that bit. Each change colour (four at most), when the definition scales them, lies between its bounds by a function (in RGB, or HSV either way round) and is darkened by another. The periodic and transition tables are the game's own, built when it starts (some from its random numbers). Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Each object type's export of its function values (`halo_map::object_exports`, object_types.c's `object_type_export_function_values` over the type's parts: `object_`, `unit_`, `biped_`, `vehicle_`, `weapon_`, `projectile_` and `device_export_function_values`). The definitions say what each of an object's four incoming values reads:
     - an object's: its vitality and recent damage, a random constant (from the game's random numbers), whether it is alive, a compass, its regions' damage;
     - a unit's: its seats' power, aiming, mouth, light, blinking, shield sapping (which reads the animation's index where the frame was meant, as the January executable does);
     - a biped's: its flying speed;
     - a vehicle's: speed, slide, turn, velocity, treads, wheels, sideslip, hover, thrust, boost (single or double precision, case by case, as the C's `fabs` makes it);
     - a weapon's: heat, ammunition, ejection ports, rate of fire, charge, illumination (which it keeps), age, light, written into its first visible ancestor's values;
     - a projectile's: range and time remaining, tracer;
     - a device's: power, position, their change, lock, delay.
     Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] A unit's inverse kinematics (`halo_map::unit_ik`, units.c's `unit_postprocess_node_matrices`, the only type's postprocess of its node matrices, with `object_inverse_kinematics` and `inverse_kinematics_adjust_matrices`), and an object's lights (`object_connect_lights`' choice). A unit that isn't a simple creature, in a seat, puts its hands on its vehicle's markers the seat's points name (unless its animation lets go, or a grenade throw releases it) and on its weapon's markers its weapon class's points name (unless its animation lets go). Each point moves the hand's node to where the grip's marker puts it, bending the shoulder and elbow to reach (98% of the arm's length at most); a later point sees the earlier ones' pose. The graph's IK points come from new layout constants. Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] The weapon's helpers (`halo_map::weapons`), ported with the update.
     - **Firing** (`weapon_trigger_begin_firing`, `weapon_trigger_fire`): charging and overloading, the magazine's rounds, firing effects chosen in turn or at random, misfires by age, heat and age, recoil, a firing effect's damage to its owner, and overheated detonation.
     - **Projectiles** (`trigger_create_projectiles`, `projectile_distribute`): from the trigger's markers, aimed by the unit, the player or the actor, spread in the error cone (the game's random directions) and across the distribution, tracers counted.
     - **The trigger's other states:** releasing a charge, overcharging, recovering, locking, tracking.
     - **The magazine:** reloading (the first person animation's time, a shotgun's reload type) and chambering.
     - **The weapon's state** (`weapon_set_state`, its animation from the graph's weapon animations), key frames, ejection ports, and detonating.
     Checked in the running game (see the handoff below).
   - [x] Detonation effects: settled by the hand-off. A detonating projectile's effect and area damage are made through `effects` and `area_damage`, both Rust's. Which needles a super-combining hit sets off, how many, and whether it will super-explode are Rust's decisions in `projectiles`; only each other needle's two timers are written through the game's callback, as every port writes another datum.
   - [x] Biped movement physics (`halo_map::biped`, `biped_update_physics`, on `halo_map::features`, Halo's collision features and `collision_move_pill`): walking along the ground with its uphill and downhill speeds, falling, flying, sliding along up to three planes, being held to ledges, finding the ground, and the crouching player's stand test, with breakable surfaces as the game has them. The game records each update's physics, and Rust replays it. 131,000 updates of 95 bipeds in b30, a10, c10 and c20 (2,700 of them flying, 48 held to a ledge) leave exactly the game's physics, byte for byte.
   - [x] Bipeds against objects (`halo_map::features`, `object_get_features_in_sphere`): other bipeds' pills, and vehicles', scenery's, machines' and controls' collision models in their nodes' space, plus what a biped bumped, the elevator it rides, and volatile collisions. The game records each object its broad phase visited during an update, as that object was then; Rust works out the object's features and replays the whole update. 213,000 updates in b30 (two runs), c20, b40 and a30 agree byte for byte, 4,900 of them bumping an object; in the three runs that broke the bumps down, 627 were bipeds, 17 vehicles, 3,244 scenery and 3 machines. Not yet checked: riding an elevator, and vehicles' physics shapes (no biped test asks for them).
   - [x] Biped movement control (`halo_map::biped_moving`, `biped_update_moving`). It turns the throttle, crouch, the animation's root motion (read from the map's animation graphs, `halo_map::animation`) and the definition into the physics' input, through player speeds, animation-driven movement or flying. Afterwards it applies the result: the biped moves and finds its location, keeps what it stands on, starts landings, jumps and counts bumps. It also covers snap facing and turning by the animation. The game records each update's movement state and what it asked of the rest of the game (AI jump aim and leaping, difficulty, cinematics, aiming). 159,586 updates in b30 and c20 leave exactly the game's state, byte for byte, and every physics input Rust builds is the game's. That includes 5,367 with player physics, 2,671 flying, 2,027 turned by the animation, 130 landings and 4 jumps. Falling and melee damage, effects and the AI's notices remain the game's.
   - [x] The biped update up to its animation (`halo_map::biped_update`, `biped_update`). It covers snap facing, the airborne and slipping counters, turning (on the ground, climbing, and flying with banking through `unit_euler_aiming_update`'s acceleration planner), the movement, and the dead, airborne (tumbling, from the game's random numbers) and landing updates. 160,660 updates in b30 and c20 leave exactly the game's state, byte for byte: 23,193 dead, 3,487 airborne, 480 landing, 2,672 flying turns, 27,389 moving turns and 227 tumbles.
   - [x] Unit animation state (`halo_map::unit_animation`, `unit_update_animation` and `unit_animation_set_state`, with the graph's seats, weapon classes and weapon types read from the map). It picks the stance from the AI's state and the magic seats, finds animations by seat and weapon label, and advances the base, soft ping, action and overlay animations (key frames, loops, restarts on a permutation drawn from the game's random numbers). It enters the state asked for when the current one may be interrupted, with its aiming and looking screens and interpolation. Grenade releases, melee damage, destruction, limp bodies and seat changes are reported, not done. 171,934 updates of bipeds in b30 and c20 agree with the game, including 1,884 states entered, 356 seat or weapon changes, 3,135 restarts and 22,639 with side effects.
   - [x] Object poses (`halo_map::pose`). An object's node orientations come from its animation's frame (`animation_get_node_orientations`, the plain 8-byte quaternion frames and the keyframe-compressed ones with 6-byte quaternions) or its model's default pose, then its scale. Its node matrices come from the interpolation from the previous pose and the node hierarchy, placed by its position, axes, mirroring, centre of mass, origin offset and parent node (`object_compute_node_matrices`). The game records a sample of poses. 115,333 in b30, a10 and d40, of every kind of object (bipeds, vehicles, weapons, equipment, projectiles, scenery, machines, light fixtures), have exactly the game's orientations and node matrices, bit for bit, including 117 from compressed animations, 541 interpolating and 9,171 hanging from a parent.
   - [x] A unit's preprocess of its pose (`halo_map::unit_pose`, `unit_preprocess_node_orientations`, with the animation applies in `halo_map::pose`: replacement, overlay, scaled, continuous and aiming screens). Over the base animation go the action, the overlay action and the soft ping, the seat's emotion, mouth and acceleration overlays, the weapon class's aiming screen at the aim relative to the body, and the seat's looking screen at the look relative to the aim, setting the unit's euler screens and their bounds. The port's grenade throw on the move comes first: the legs kept in the walk (blended by speed and direction, eased a step a tick), floating in the air, crouched, or seated on a rider. The game records each sampled biped's inputs to it (protocol 23). 30,342 bipeds' preprocessed poses in b30 (twice, once throwing grenades on the move), a10 and d40 have exactly the game's orientations, euler screens and bounds, bit for bit, including 22,752 aiming, 22,187 looking, 172 actions, 1,500 overlay actions, 248 soft pings, 1,211 emotion, mouth or acceleration overlays and 22 grenade throws on the move (4 in the air); crouched throws and riders' throws were not exercised.
   - [x] Objects' overlay animations and the other types' preprocesses. An object's graph's overlays, driven by its outgoing function values, play a frame the value picks or a frame the time picks, scaled by the value (`halo_map::pose::object_overlays_apply`). A vehicle's preprocess, after the unit's, applies its steering screen at its turn, overlays for its sideways velocity, speed, forward velocity and wheel rotation, and one per suspension (`halo_map::vehicle_pose`). A device's applies its position overlay, interpolated or not, looping or not, reversed or not, and its power overlay (`halo_map::device_pose`). A weapon's does nothing. The game records the vehicle's and device's state with each sampled pose (protocol 24). In b30, a10 and d40, all 250,151 sampled poses' orientations agree with the game bit for bit, 1,950 of them with object overlays. Every type's preprocess agrees too: 15,771 bipeds, 6,434 vehicles (1,629 in a10 with wheels turning and suspensions compressed), 20,543 weapons, 6,038 devices (3,106 changed by position or power) and 201,365 others.
   - [x] A player's melee (`halo_map::biped_update::player_melee`, with `unit_animation_start_action` in `halo_map::unit_animation`). A player asking for one, with a weapon that allows it (not overcharged) or with none outside a seat, not zoomed, starts one. It lasts the weapon's first-person melee animation's frames with the hit at its key frame, both sped up by a quarter (`weapon_get_first_person_animation_time`). The unit's melee action plays a permutation of its weapon type's melee animation, drawn from the game's random numbers, and the damage lands at its tick. The game records each player's melee step (protocol 25). 6,156 steps in b30 agree with the game, including 60 melees started with an assault rifle and their 60 hits, with the action, its animation and the random seed after. Not exercised: melees with no weapon (a port-only gametype loadout). Not ported: the PAL maps' frame counts.
   - [x] Vehicles' physics (`halo_map::vehicle_physics`, `physics_update`). Each mass point gets its ground plane, the deepest feature it is inside among the features around it (`collision_features_test_point`, new in `halo_map::features`), and its water depth, as `scenario_location_water_depth` finds it through the clusters' water fogs. Then come its ground friction (scaled by the ground's material), its powered friction against the ground, water and air, its water pressure, lift, thrust and antigravity. Most vehicles take the old physics (a physics tag with a radius, `physics_update_old`): the object's own frame, a moment of inertia from the mass points' distances from the torque's axis, ice an eighth as grippy, and a move straight to the new position. The others take the new physics (`physics_update_new`): the inverse inertia matrix, the freeze that stops mass points sweeping into the world, and the collision force other vehicles push them with. The game records each update's state, what the vehicle control code gave it (the powered mass points, magic force and torque), its mass points, the objects its tests visited, and the vehicles it might collide with (protocol 28). 9,443 updates in a30, b30, b40, a50 and d40 agree with the game byte for byte, state and every mass point, including 32 of d40's Ghosts antigraving. The frame now records up to 128 vehicles' placement (protocol 29), as b40 has more than 32. Not exercised: water, the freeze against the world, collisions between vehicles.
   - [x] Vehicle control (`halo_map::vehicle_update`, `vehicle_update` up to its animation). It covers righting an upended vehicle, the speed, slide and turn seeking the driver's throttle and facing (`physics_variable_speed_update_seek`, `physics_variable_position_update_seek`), and each type's control code that hands the physics its powered mass points and magic force and torque: the tank's treads, the jeep's wheels and steering, the boat's rudder and roll, the Pelican's lift and attitude, the Ghost's hover, the Banshee's flight (old and new physics) and the turret. Then the suspensions follow the ground, it counts airborne and on-ground ticks, and planes and fighters are held between the structure's floor and ceiling. A vehicle at rest coasts to a stop instead. The game records each update's state, the driver's controls and the physics record it made (protocol 30). Fields the control code leaves unset in the stack array the game passes in come from that record (physics reads them only where flags allow). 44,310 updates in a30, b30, b40, a50, c10 and d40 agree byte for byte, along with the controls handed to their physics: 3,574 jeeps, 14,008 Pelicans, 1,117 Banshees and dropships, 25,611 turrets. Not exercised: the tank, the boat, the Ghost, righting, riding a parent.
   - [x] The object simulation handing off from the game to Rust. The ports run inside the game first, one system at a time (`crates/halo_sim`: a 32-bit DLL OpenCE loads, `port/linux/game/halo_sim.c`). The game calls the Rust port in place of its own C, with the same order and the same tick, so results stay bit for bit. `game.rust_simulation` (`HALO_RUST_SIMULATION`) names the systems that run in Rust. A system followed by `:check` runs both, keeps the game's result, and logs where the Rust differs. The DLL reads the level from the game's own map file and keeps only each structure BSP's collision and clusters. The ports move out to the 64-bit viewer later, when AI and scripts follow.
     - [x] Point physics (particles, contrails, antennas, flags): Rust is authoritative with `point_physics`. In check mode, all 8,257,536 updates in a 3-minute b30 run agree with the game bit for bit. The handoff found one difference the probe never could: the game's ray test with no environment flags skips the structure and takes the end point's location, which `collision::World::test` now does too. With Rust in charge, the game plays as before (145 frames a second), and every probe check still agrees.
     - [x] Tags read where the game holds them. `halo_sim_set_tag_cache` gives the DLL the game's tag cache in its own memory (0x803A6000, the tag data and the structure BSP loaded after it), and `CacheFile::from_memory` reads it as a map file is read, without a copy. Models, animations and object definitions are read once each per level.
     - [x] Poses (`object_compute_node_matrices`): Rust is authoritative with `poses`. Two calls run in Rust: the orientations (the animation's frame, or a frame of the time for an object that animates by itself, or the model's pose; the object's overlays; its scale), and the interpolation from the previous pose plus the node matrices. The object types' preprocess between them is still the game's. In check mode, all 1,458,176 poses in a 2-minute b30 run agree in both stages. With poses and point physics both in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees.
     - [x] The object types' preprocess of a pose, under `poses`. A unit's covers its actions, overlays, aiming and looking screens, and the grenade throw on the move; a vehicle's follows the unit's, and devices have their own. The game hands over the unit's, vehicle's or device's state, and takes back the orientations, the unit's euler screens and their bounds, and the grenade throw's leg weights (`unit_grenade_throw_set_leg_weights`). In check mode, all 393,216 preprocessed poses in a 3-minute b30 run with grenade throws agree with the game, euler screens and leg weights included. With the whole pose and point physics in Rust, the game plays at 145 frames a second, and every probe check agrees, including 18 throws on the move (4 crouched).
     - [x] The live object world, through the host's callbacks (`halo_sim_set_host`): the objects in a cluster's collideable partition, an object's state when it is read (type, flags, bounding sphere, regions, node matrices, a vehicle's placement), and its children. `collision::World` takes them as an `ObjectSource`. The first user is the game's whole ray test, `collision_test_vector`, under `collision` (check mode only). All 68 million ray tests in four 1-minute runs (b30, b40, a30, c10, firing and throwing grenades) agree with the game. That includes 6.5 million structure hits, 1.8 million media hits and 619,260 object hits, 96,051 of them on vehicles' physics.
     - [x] Projectiles (`projectile_update`): Rust is authoritative with `projectiles`. Rust runs the timers, the flight, the ray tests on the live world and each collision's response. It calls the game back, at the moments the C does, for what a projectile does to the world: impact damage (taking back the material type), breakable surfaces, effects, the AI's impact noise, its moves (flyby sound, orientation, `object_translate`, contrail), sticking to what it hit, and the timers of needles a super-combining hit resets. Each callback writes the update's state and random seed into the game first and reads them back after. `halo_map::projectile` calls a `ProjectileHost` for all of this; the probe's recorded replay is one host and the game another. The C's side effects became functions that the C and the callbacks share. Ray-test results now carry the region, material index, plane designator and breakable surface index (and the `collision` check compares them). Breakable surfaces are read again after each callback, since a surface can break between two ray tests of one update. In check mode the game's update records each call (its inputs, results, and the random seed before and after), and Rust replays the update from those records. All 9,984 updates in five 1-minute runs (b30 firing and throwing grenades, b40, a30, c10) agree with the game, as do all 14,015 calls. With point physics, poses and projectiles all in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees: 8,403 of 8,403 projectile updates on b40, and 587 of 587 on c10. On b30 one update of 3,917 differs in the probe only: it damaged the same object twice, and the probe replays objects as first seen in an update.
     - [x] Biped movement physics (`biped_update_physics`): Rust is authoritative with `biped_physics`. It uses neither random numbers nor side effects, so check mode runs Rust on a copy, as for point physics. Collision features now come from the live objects too: `features::Objects::Live` runs the game's broad phase (`collision_get_features_in_sphere`: the collideable objects of the clusters the sphere reaches, each once, and the children of those it reaches) through a `FeatureObjectSource`. The host now also reports what features read of an object: whether it is dead, its velocity, whether it is an elevator, and a biped's pill, player and seat. In check mode, every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, c20, b40, a10 and a30 (at least 92,160; the log reports at powers of two). Those runs include flying sentinels, bipeds held to ledges, and bumps. With point physics, poses, projectiles and biped physics in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second. Every probe check agrees, including 42,777 of 42,777 biped physics updates on b30 and 80,378 of 80,378 on c20, since the host records the feature gatherings and the objects they visit for the bridge as the game's broad phase does.
     - [x] Vehicles' physics (`physics_update`, old and new): Rust is authoritative with `vehicle_physics`. Rust computes the forces at each mass point, with ground planes from the live objects' collision features, then the integration and the freeze against penetrating the world. Through a `VehiclePhysicsHost`, the game moves the vehicle (`object_set_position`, or `object_translate` along the line), and its unit collisions push the bipeds and vehicles it hits (the game's `physics_compute_unit_collisions`, called back). Rust writes the mass points into the game's array. In check mode the game's update runs first. Rust then replays it as the probe does: from the state it began with, on the live world, with the bounding sphere centre the game's move left and the other vehicles its unit collisions considered. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, b40, a50, c40 and d40 (at least 4,640). That covers the old physics, and the new physics' driven Ghosts and Banshees with antigravity; no two vehicles collided in these runs. With point physics, poses, projectiles, biped physics and vehicle physics in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees, including 2,126 of 2,126 vehicle physics updates on b30 and 589 of 589 on d40.
     - [x] Vehicle control (`vehicle_update`, up to its falling damage): Rust is authoritative with `vehicle_update`. Rust runs the steering, righting, speed, slide and turn, each type's control code, the physics (live, as above), the suspensions and airborne ticks, and the floor and ceiling. Through a `VehicleUpdateHost`, the game makes the slipping, crashing and thruster effects, the suspension sound (whose playing decides the crash effects, as the C's `update_suspension` result does), and moves a vehicle coasting to a stop. Falling damage to what a vehicle carries stays the game's, after Rust returns. The powered mass points start from zeros, where the game's start from its uninitialized stack; the physics reads only what its flags allow, and in check mode Rust takes the game's. In check mode Rust replays the game's update on the live world, with the bounding sphere centre it left and the powered mass points and other vehicles its physics met. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, c40 and d40 (at least 6,656). With all seven systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second. Every probe check agrees, including 7,911 of 7,911 vehicle updates and 2,126 of 2,126 vehicle physics updates on b30. That works because Rust's physics calls the bridge's recording through its host, and the host's object callback says what each object is read for (a ray test or a feature gathering), so the bridge records the visits as the game's own code paths do.
     - [x] Biped movement control (`biped_update_moving`): Rust is authoritative with `biped_moving`. Rust works out what the physics wants (the animation's root motion, read from the game's tags; player speeds; flying), runs the physics on the live objects, then what follows it: where it stands, landing, jumping and bumps. Through a `MovingHost`, the game aborts the animation, moves the biped, answers the AI's jump aim, makes the jump's footsteps, does a melee attack's impact (its own function now, which the C shares), notes the AI's bump and does falling damage. In check mode Rust replays the game's update on the live world, with the AI's answer to its jump aim. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, c20 and a30 (at least 102,400), including turns that abort the animation and AI jumps. With eight systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees, including 58,417 of 58,417 movement updates and 43,462 of 43,462 physics updates on b30. Rust's physics records itself for the bridge through the host.
     - [x] The biped update up to its player melee (`biped_update`: snap facing, turning, flying banking, the movement as above, and the dead, airborne and landing updates): Rust is authoritative with `biped_update`. Through an `UpdateHost`, the game relaxes a limp body onto the ground, makes the landing's and slipping's footsteps, and lends its random seed to a tumble (the seed is the game's as the tumble draws, and Rust hands back what it left). The host also brackets the movement for the bridge, and writes the update's own state (throttle, facing, state, slipping) to the biped before the movement's callbacks read it. In check mode Rust replays the game's update on the live world, with the AI's jump answer and the seed the airborne update began with. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), c20 and a30 (at least 102,400), including 14,177 dead bipeds and 2,647 airborne updates. With nine systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees, including 58,451 of 58,451 biped updates on b30 and 79,253 of 79,253 on c20.
     - [x] Unit animation (`unit_update_animation`, every unit's: bipeds, vehicles, riders): Rust is authoritative with `unit_animation`. Rust picks the stance, advances the base, soft ping, action and overlay animations, and enters the state asked for, drawing permutations from the game's random seed. An `UnitAnimationHost` does each side effect as it happens, with the seed synced around it: a grenade's release, melee damage, destroying a dead unit, a limp body, and leaving or entering a seat. Each runs the C's own block (`unit_rust_animation_effect`); leaving a seat adds the animation's exit velocity, as the C does. Destroying a unit deletes it, so the callbacks stop and nothing is written back. In check mode Rust replays the game's update from the state and seed it began with. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), c20 and a30 (at least 122,880; 14,769 with side effects), vehicles' included. With ten systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees, including 74,930 of 74,930 unit animation updates on b30.
     - [x] A player's melee step (`biped_update`'s): Rust is authoritative with `player_melee`. Rust decides when one starts, times it by the weapon's first-person melee animation (from the game's tags), and plays the unit's melee action with its own `unit_animation_start_action`, drawing from the game's random seed. The game stops the weapon's reload, tells the first-person weapon, and does the damage when it lands. In check mode every step agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (10 melees started, 11 hits landed). With all eleven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees, including 1,656 of 1,656 melee steps on b30 (23 started, 23 landed).
     - [x] The unit update (`unit_update`, every unit's; new in `halo_map::unit_update`): Rust is authoritative with `unit_update`. It covers the controls (a blind run's, from the game's random seed; a driver's and gunner's), timers (persistent controls, camouflage, stun, weapon drop, feigning death, delayed damage, flaming death), readying weapons and grenades, zoom, aiming and looking (the euler screens' acceleration planner, now in a unit's orientation, `angular_accelerate_to_position`, clipping to the screens), the grenade throw's states, the weapon's controls, seat power and acceleration, the mouth, the integrated light and night vision, and the time-sliced unit timer. Every call of the game goes through units.c's `unit_update_call`, which the C and Rust's `UnitUpdateHost` share: weapons, grenades, death, zoom sounds, seats, AI damage, melee, dialogue, illumination, effects. The unit definition's aiming, looking and powered-seat fields come from the game's tags (new layout constants). In check mode `unit_update_call` records each call with the unit before and after; Rust's replay compares the unit before every call and at the end, so each stretch of Rust between calls is checked. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), c20 and d40 (at least 115,200, with 425,027 calls of the game). A planted off-by-one in the unit timer was caught at once. With all twelve systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees.
     - [x] The rest of the biped update (new in `halo_map::biped_rest`): Rust is authoritative with `biped_rest`. It covers a biped riding a vehicle (its speech, leaving the seat when asked, thrown out of a vehicle lying upturned on the ground) or another biped (the state it asks for), and after an unparented biped's update its footsteps (timed by the animation's foot frames, read from the game's tags), the AI's notice of a dangerous fall (from the ground below it and the falling damage that kills), and erasing a biped fallen out of the world. Its calls of the game go through bipeds.c's `biped_rest_call`, shared by the C and Rust's `RestHost`, which records each with the biped before and after in check mode. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, c20 and d40 (at least 98,560, 11,104 of them riding). A planted bug in the stop counter was caught at once. With all thirteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees. The whole biped and unit update now runs in Rust, the game called back for what it does to the world.
     - [x] Damage (`object_cause_damage`, wherever the game deals it: projectiles, melee, falling, area damage, scripts): Rust is authoritative with `damage`. The network's authority over the outermost call stays the game's, ahead of Rust. Every call of the rest of the game goes through damage.c's `damage_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, which writes the damage data and the objects Rust has read into the game first and reads them back after (an object deleted by its aftermath is left alone). Damage dealt from within one of those calls is its own, Rust's again. In check mode the game notes the objects damage may read (the object, its parents, its children and their umbrella shields) and the random seed, and records each call with the damage data and the call's object before and after; Rust's replay compares every call's arguments and what came before it, and the game compares the damage data Rust leaves. Every damage agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), c20, a30 and d40 (at least 1,152, with 2,841 calls of the game). A planted error of a tenth of a percent in the roll was caught at its first use. With all fourteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, c20, a30 and d40 (on a30, 5,396 dead bipeds' updates and 121 projectiles damaging an object).
     - [x] Area damage (`area_of_effect_cause_damage`: grenades, explosions, projectiles' detonations, scripts): Rust is authoritative with `area_damage`. Rust gathers the objects, runs its rays with its own ray test on the live structure, and works out each object's falloff. damage.c's `area_damage_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, deals the damage (`object_cause_damage`, itself Rust's under `damage`), and answers teams, the infection forms' difficulty and breakable surfaces. In check mode the game notes the clusters the sphere reaches, each one's objects, and those objects with their children and parents. It records each call, rays included, with the damage data before and after and the objects a dealing of damage changed (a killed unit drops its weapon). Rust's replay compares every call's arguments and the damage before it, and the clusters it finds. Every detonation agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20, a30, d40, c40 and 90 seconds of d20, all throwing grenades: at least 10,296 detonations, with 9,150 rays and 7,706 objects damaged. A planted error of a tenth of a percent in the falloff was caught at the first object it damaged. Not exercised: popping infection forms. With all fifteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, d20, c20 and a30. On d40, one biped update of about 960 differs in the probe at the level's opening fall. It differs the same way with only point physics in Rust, so it's a gap in the probe's replay, not the handoff.
     - [x] Damage over time (`object_damage_update`): Rust is authoritative with `damage_update`. Rust runs the shield's over-charge, drain and recharge, the stun and the decay timers. damage.c's `damage_update_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, does an act of god's death, the HUD's shield tick, the recharge difficulty and the recharging effect with the shield regions' permutations. In check mode it records each call with the object before and after, and the game compares the object Rust leaves. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 and 90 seconds of a30, d40 and d20, all throwing grenades: at least 991,232 updates, 277 with the shield changing and 8,983 decaying. A planted error in the body damage's decay was caught at once. Not exercised: over-charging, an act of god's death. With all sixteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, d20, c20 and a30 (d40 has the probe's one known gap at its opening fall).
     - [x] Device updates (`device_update`, `machine_update`): Rust is authoritative with `devices`. Rust moves power and position, runs platforms, doors and elevators, and finds the bipeds near a machine with its own sphere query on the live clusters. devices.c's `device_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, makes effects, sets device groups' actual and desired values, answers teams and translates objects; the device is written to the game before each call and read back after, since a group's change can move its devices. In check mode it records each call with the device before and after, and each sphere's bipeds; Rust's replay compares every call and the game compares the device Rust leaves. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of a10, b40, c20 and d20 and 90 seconds of b40, c20, d40 and a30: at least 352,256 updates, including 34,391 moving and 1,209 doors opened or closed. A planted error in `accelerate_to_position` was caught at once. Not exercised: an elevator carrying a rider (the probe's player rides none). With all seventeen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b40, c20, d20, a10 and b30, the devices' poses included.
     - [x] Item updates (`item_update`): Rust is authoritative with `items`. Rust runs the fall, the bounces, coming to rest, riding an object and the spin, with its own ray and point tests on the live world and the item's markers from the game's tags. items.c's `item_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, makes the impact's material effect and sound, marks garbage, translates the item, accelerates it (`item_accelerate`, which explosions call too) and detonates it. In check mode it records each call with the item before and after, and Rust's replay compares every call and the game the item Rust leaves. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), a30, d20, b40 and c20: at least 135,168 updates, with 88 impacts, 80 items coming to rest and 722 spinning. A planted error in the bounce's reflection was caught at its first bounce. With all eighteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. The check found three functions halo_sim.c and devices.c called undeclared, so their one-byte booleans were read as ints with garbage above: `game_engine_running` (the biped rest's, damage's, the damage update's and items' inputs), `actor_aim_jump` (a jump's result) and `game_team_is_enemy` (one-sided doors). It also found the poses' world-relative result written as an int into a boolean. All are fixed, and every system those fed agrees again.
     - [x] Weapon updates (`weapon_update`): Rust is authoritative with `weapons`. Rust runs the animation (from the game's random seed), heat, triggers, magazines and their timers. weapons.c's `weapon_update_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host, runs each of the weapon's helpers at the moment the C does, with the weapon and random seed synced around it: state changes at key frames, detonating, overheating's message and looping effect, stopping effects, reloading and chambering, and each trigger's firing, recovering, overloading, charging and locking. In check mode it records each call with the weapon and seed before and after; Rust's replay compares every call and the game compares the weapon Rust leaves. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and c20: at least 49,152 updates, with 693 triggers fired and 609 updates of hot weapons. A planted error in the heat loss was caught at once. Not confirmed: charging, overheating and tracking triggers (there are no counts for them). With all nineteen systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. Counting the unit definition's flags for this found area damage's infection-form test reading bit 12 for `_unit_is_inconsequential_bit`, which is bit 19; it is fixed (the infection-form path was never exercised).
     - [x] The weapon's helpers, under `weapons`: Rust's update calls its own helpers now. What they do to the rest of the game goes through weapons.c's `weapon_game_call`, shared by the C and Rust's host:
       - effects and messages;
       - whether the weapon is underwater;
       - the first person animation's time;
