       - creating each projectile (forced inside the structure from a player's camera, its target, its tracer).
       The C helpers make the same calls, so whoever calls them (the unit's code too) is unchanged. Rust reads the owning unit and the effect object's markers live. In check mode every call is recorded with the weapon and random seed before and after. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, a30, d20 and c20: at least 49,152 updates, with 421 projectiles made and 4,143 calls of the game. A planted error in the error cone was caught at the first projectile. Not confirmed: charging, overloading, spewing, alternate ammunition, misfires, tracking and detonation (no counts for them). With all twenty-four systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40.
     - [x] The object update loop (`objects_update`): Rust is authoritative with `objects`, and drives each tick's object updates. Every step of the loop goes through objects.c's `objects_update_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, and so does every read of an object header or object. Each step is the game's, or a system already handed over behind it: each object's type update reaches Rust's bipeds, vehicles, units, devices, items and weapons that way. Garbage's lifetime and scenery's animation (from the game's random seed) run in Rust under the same system. In check mode the game records every call with what it read; Rust's replay must make the same calls in the same order, and garbage's and scenery's updates are compared. Every tick agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), d20, c20 and a30: at least 4,096 ticks, with 1,095,168 object updates, 22,648,885 calls and 765,968 scenery updates. A planted off-by-one in the interpolation was caught at once. Not exercised: garbage (none expired in these runs). With all twenty systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. In check mode d20 shows two biped updates the probe can't replay (a Flood form attached mid-update); the original C loop shows the same two.
     - [x] Function values and change colours: Rust is authoritative with `object_functions`. Neither calls the rest of the game: Rust computes them from the object's values, its definition (from the game's tags) and the game's function tables (`transition_function_table_get` is new, beside the periodic tables' getter). In check mode Rust's, from the values the game's began with, is compared with what the game's left. Everything agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), d20, c20 and b40: at least 1,245,184 function value updates (153,393 changing something) and as many change colour updates (37,187 changing). A planted error in the time scale was caught at once. With all twenty-one systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40.
     - [x] Function exports: Rust is authoritative with `object_exports`. Rust exports from the object's state as the game hands it over, its definitions (from the game's tags) and the random seed; it calls nothing of the game. In check mode Rust's, from the state the game's began with, is compared with what the game's left (the values, a weapon's ancestor's values and triggers' illumination, the seed). Every export agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), d20, c20, b40 and a30: at least 1,507,328 exports, 30,868 changing something (bipeds, vehicles, weapons, scenery, machines and controls among them). A planted error in weapons' exports was caught at once. Not exercised: projectiles' and equipment's (none changed). With all twenty-two systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. The implicit-declaration check found `game_engine_vehicle_placement_allowed`, a boolean, called undeclared from object_types.c (older than this work): it and `game_engine_vehicle_placement_begin` are declared in game_engine.h now.
     - [x] Inverse kinematics and lights: Rust is authoritative with `unit_ik` and `lights`. Rust adjusts the unit's node matrices in place, from its tags (its graph's points, its model's markers and nodes) and the grip objects' markers and poses as the game holds them. Whether a grenade throw releases a vehicle's grip stays the game's (it keeps its own record across ticks), asked through a callback. Rust chooses the lights to disconnect and reconnect, and the game reconnects them. In check mode the game's grenade answer is recorded; Rust's adjustment of the matrices the game's began with is compared with the game's, and so are the lights. Everything agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 (throwing grenades), a30, d20, c20 and b40: at least 139,264 units' postprocesses (15,933 adjusting the pose, 69,103 with the weapon's grip, and on b30 10,381 with the vehicle's), and 442,368 objects' lights (325,475 lights). A planted error in the arm's reach was caught at once. With all twenty-four systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. Every step of an object's update now runs in Rust: the loop, its type's update, damage over time, function exports and values, node matrices, change colours, lights and the postprocess. What they call of the rest of the game (effects, sounds, AI, the network, firing) remains the game's.
     - [x] The game's tick (`game_tick`; new in `halo_map::game_tick`): Rust is authoritative with `game_tick`, and drives the tick: the order the subsystems update in, and the questions that choose some of them (a distributed client drives its units from the host's actors and runs no scripts in co-op; double speed halves the effects' step). Each step is game.c's `game_tick_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls), and reaches the systems handed over behind it. In check mode the game's tick notes each step and answer, and Rust's replay must make the same steps in the same order: 1,024 ticks agree on b30, and a planted swap of two steps was caught at the first tick. With all thirty systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40 at 144 frames a second.
3. Effects ✅ (same branches)
   - [x] Effect updates (`effect_update`, every tick's and each new effect's first; new in `halo_map::effects`): Rust is authoritative with `effects`. Rust follows the object an effect is attached to (its location, velocity, function values and change colour), decides its visibility, runs its events' delays, skips and durations from the game's two random seeds (the global one for deterministic effects, the local one otherwise), and works out each part and particle: where its location's instances put it, its velocity in the cone, its scale, radius, rotation and tint. effects.c's `effect_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, does what an effect does to the rest of the game: reading its object, visibility, water and node matrices, its impulse field, making particle systems, sounds, objects, decals, damage, lights and particles, and deleting it. Each call writes the effect and both seeds into the game first and reads them back after. The location instances are handed over with the update. In check mode the game records each call with the effect and both seeds before and after; Rust's replay compares every call, and the game compares the effect (or that it is gone) and the seeds Rust leaves. An effect updated within one of those calls is part of that call. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 405,504 updates, 15,564 parts and 111,964 particles made, 1.47 million calls of the game. The check found one difference first: c20's lightning ring asks for a particle count beyond a `long`, which x86 truncates to 0x80000000 where Rust's `as` saturates; `c_long` does as x86 does. A planted error in particles' radius was caught at the first particle. Not counted separately: impulse fields, first person locations, effects deleted when inactive and looping effects stopping. With all twenty-five systems in Rust, the game plays at 144 frames a second, and every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40.
   - [x] Creating effects (`effect_new_looping`, `_from_object`, `_attached_from_markers`, `_unattached_from_markers`), under `effects`: Rust runs each creation and its first update. It decides the effect's flags (a corpse's effects are nonviolent), scales, colour, attachment and first event (from the random seeds), works out a marker list's locations (the markers in their node's space, with an up vector perpendicular to the forward), and builds each location's instances, which it keeps for the update that follows. `effect_call` now also does what creation takes of the game: the definition's test and the effect's datum (`effect_allocate`, evicting a non-deterministic effect for a deterministic one), its impulse field, the corpse test, the first person weapon's local player, a point's location, an object's or first person weapon's markers, each location instance's datum, and a node matrix. The C creations are restructured to make the same calls. In check mode a creation is recorded before its effect exists, and the game compares the effect Rust made, its state and the seeds. A call nested in a recorded call is part of it: the check found that a nested creation closed its outer call's record, so only the call that opened a record closes it now. Every creation agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 15,104 creations (312 looping, 620 from an object, 461 attached and 13,711 unattached from markers) and 39,172 location instances built. A planted error in the perpendicular up vector was caught at the first location. With all twenty-five systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Particle updates (`particles_update`, each particle's every frame; new in `halo_map::particles`): Rust is authoritative with `particles`. Rust ages each particle, deletes one gone undrawn too long (the frames drawn at the last ticks are handed over), kills one at the end of its life, runs its animation's frames and its initial, looping and final sequences (from the local random seed and its bitmap's sequences, read from the game's tags), and its physics: attached particles' friction and movement, and for the rest a collision's effect, material effect and death, settling and coming to rest. particles.c's `particle_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, deletes it, makes its effects and sounds, its impact's material effect (if visible), tells whether its object is still there, and runs its point physics (the game's `point_physics_update`, itself Rust's under `point_physics`). In check mode every call is recorded with the particle and the local seed before and after. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 11,272,192 updates, 10.3 million of them with point physics, 94,477 attached, 20,452 effects made. A planted error in particles' radius was caught at the first physics call. With all twenty-six systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Contrail updates (`contrails_update`, each contrail's every frame; new in `halo_map::contrails`): Rust is authoritative with `contrails`. A contrail's points are datums of their own, linked from it, so the game hands Rust the contrail with all its points and writes back what Rust leaves. Rust follows the object's density function, adds points at the attachment's markers when they are due (on the first frame after each tick, as the native builds emit them), spreading them in a cone from the local random seed and between the last point and the marker, runs the animation and texture scroll, steps each point through its states, trims expired points and deletes a contrail left with none. contrails.c's `contrail_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, answers what reads the world or allocates: the function value, the markers, the object's velocity, a point's location (from a point or along a line), its point physics, its datum, and deleting the contrail. In check mode each call is recorded with the seed before and after, and the game compares the contrail and every point Rust leaves with its own. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 119,040 updates, 4,941 points made and 554,621 compared, 398,186 point physics steps. A planted error in a state's transition time was caught (the first plants, in the inherited velocity and the state duration, were not exercised: these levels' contrails inherit no velocity and have no state durations). With all twenty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Particle system updates (`particle_system_update`, each system's every frame; new in `halo_map::particle_systems`): Rust is authoritative with `particle_systems`. As with contrails, the game hands Rust the system with all its particles (linked from each of its up to four types) and writes back what Rust leaves. Rust follows the object it is attached to (active while connected and its function has a value), runs the system's point physics, steps each type through its states and interpolates their variables (scaled and tinted as the type's flags say), makes new particles at the attachment's markers with the default, explosion or jet creation physics (from the local random seed), and steps each particle through its states, randomizing their variables, deleting the dead and running the rest's (interpolated) point physics. It follows the lists through shorts as the game does. particle_systems.c's `particle_system_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, answers the object, the point physics (interpolating a transition's), the markers, the object's location, a point's location, the particles' datums and deleting the system. In check mode each call is recorded with the seed before and after, and the game compares the system and every particle Rust leaves with its own. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30 and a10: at least 21,504 updates (17,567 attached), 388 particles made, 187,898 compared after their point physics (d20, b40 and c10 ran no particle systems). A planted error in particles' rotation was caught at the first particle. With all twenty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40 at 144 frames a second.
   - Decals have no update to port: nothing calls `decals_update`, so a decal the game makes is only drawn.
   - [x] Making particles (`particle_new`), under `particles`: Rust places the particle (in its node's space for an attached one), finds its location and tests its visibility, then sets its flags, lifespan, frame rate, impulse and lighting and starts its first sequence (from the local random seed). New `particle_call`s do what reads the world or allocates: the node's transform, the location, the visibility test, the datum and `light_particle`. The location now starts from zeros: the C's was the stack's leftover, in a word only rendering keeps. In check mode a creation is recorded before its particle exists. Every creation and update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 53,248 creations on b30, d20 and c20 (47,184 particles made). A planted error in the lifespan was caught at the next call. The first runs counted far fewer updates than the day before: a Rust panic (a copy into a slice too short after the call grew) had stopped the game early, and the counts were all that showed it. The probe script now reports a panic in the game's log.
   - [x] Making contrails and particle systems (`contrail_new`, `particle_system_new_unattached` and `_attached`), under `contrails` and `particle_systems`: Rust sets up the contrail (its first frame from the local random seed, its density and first point) or the system (its colour, position, velocity, lighting and scale; then `particle_system_initialize`: its location, each type's first state, and its first update), with the update ports' code. New calls answer the datum, the attachment's function (and a system's change colour and the object's velocity), `light_particle` and a datum's deletion when a type has no states. One of the C's stack leftovers becomes zeros: an attached system's position when its object has no such marker. Every creation agrees in 1-minute runs of b30, a30, a10, d20, c20 and b40: at least 640 contrails and 14 particle systems (attached ones on a30 and a10). Planted errors in a contrail's first texture offset and a system's first state length were caught. The first Rust run hung b40 while loading: the contrail store wrote only what an update changes, so a contrail Rust made was left with no definition or object; the stores now write what a creation sets as well. With all twenty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Player effects (`player_effect_start` from damage, `player_effect_screen_flash`, `player_telefrag_effect_start`, `player_effect_add_continuous_effect`, and each frame's `player_effect_get_camera_effect_matrix` and `player_effect_get_screen_flash`; new in `halo_map::player_effects`): Rust is authoritative with `player_effects`. It runs a local player's screen flash, camera impulse (with the permanent turn it gives the player's aim), camera shake (from a seed made of the tick, as the native builds shake once a tick), continuous shake, damage indicators, screen fade and scripted effect, with the game's periodic and transition function tables. player_effects.c's `player_effect_call`, shared by the C (restructured) and Rust's host, answers the player's facing and takes its permanent impulse, the rumble, the damage effect's sound, and where the attacker is from the player's camera. The state handed over is the local player's datum with the shared fade, scripted effect and flags. In check mode each operation's calls are recorded with the state before and after, and the game compares the state and what the operation gives back (the camera matrix, the screen flash). Every operation that ran agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: 504 starts from damage, about 40,600 camera matrices and as many screen flash reads. The first run differed on b30's opening fade: the cosine transition is the sixth function, not the third. Not exercised: the game's screen flash, telefrag and continuous shake. A planted error in the camera shake's duration was caught at the next call. With all twenty-nine systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - Left for a presentation pass with the renderer: decals (made in the game's rendering geometry and only drawn), weather particle systems (updated as they are drawn) and material effects (an effect and a sound at a nudged point, reached through calls the ports already make). They don't touch the simulation, and they belong with how the viewer draws effects.
4. Scripts (`hs`)
   - [x] The script runtime's update (`hs_runtime_update`, every tick's; new in `halo_map::hs_runtime`): Rust is authoritative with `hs`. Rust runs every thread on the game's own script memory, in place: the scenario's startup, dormant, continuous and static scripts and the console's, their stacks (push, allocate, pop and the overflow), evaluation of each node, the casts, globals (the scenario's and the game's external ones, read and written back through their widths), and the built-in functions: `begin`, `begin_random` (from the global seed), `if`, `set`, `and`, `or`, arithmetic, comparisons, `sleep`, `sleep_until`, `wake`, `inspect` and the object casts. The library's 390-odd functions stay the game's: hs_runtime.c's `hs_runtime_call`, shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls) and Rust's host, evaluates one in its thread, and answers what the runtime asks of the rest of the game: whether a script is disabled, whether the game is in progress, an object from its name, the object lists (from an AI, of an object, their references and garbage), the object's type, the messages, `inspect`'s printing, the syntax garbage and a console thread's deletion. The C left the upper bytes of a cast to a boolean or a short, and of a comparison's or `and`'s result, to whatever the compiler did; they are set now as the January build leaves them. The game's cast table sits two types early against `enum hs_type` past the integers (its real casts are from types 30 to 33, the object list's from 16, 34 to 38 and 39 to 44); Rust mirrors it as it is. In check mode the game snapshots the thread array, the globals, the global seed and the runtime's flags, runs its own update recording each call (a library function's with its thread's datum before and after, where its value returns to and that value), puts the snapshot back, runs Rust's replay, compares the memory Rust leaves with its own, and keeps its own. A tick whose library calls change the thread array (as a library function's level start) is left out. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40: at least 1,024 updates on each, 23,888 library calls. A planted extra tick in `sleep_until` was caught at the first update. With all thirty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The runtime's level start and console commands (`hs_runtime_initialize_for_new_map`: the thread array emptied, each of the scenario's globals made and initialized in an internal thread, a thread for each script that runs; `hs_runtime_evaluate`: a command's expression in a thread of its own, its value or left running), under `hs`. Rust now makes and deletes the threads and the globals' datums itself, with the game's `datum_new`, `datum_new_at_index`, `datum_delete` and `data_make_valid` on its arrays, so a finished console command's thread is deleted in the update by Rust too, and the check no longer leaves that tick out. New `hs_runtime_call`s answer whether a global's initializer is disabled and give the level start's and a command's messages. In check mode each operation is compared as the update is, with the command's value. A command for testing (`HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND`, run at the 300th tick) exercises a command that sleeps, calls the library and returns. Every level start, command and update agrees on b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40. Planted errors in a dormant script's first sleep and a command thread's script index were caught at the fields they wrote. With all thirty-one systems in Rust and the test command, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second, except when the command draws from the global seed (`random_range`): then one biped update on d20 differs in the probe, the same with the scripts in C, because the probe's replay doesn't expect a draw partway through the tick.
   - [ ] The library (398 functions behind `hs_runtime_call`, hs.c's definitions; plan of 24 October), in batches by subsystem, each Rust's in Rust's mode and compared with the game's in check mode as the runtime was:
     - [x] The arguments (25 October): a library function its macro parses (all 398) has its arguments evaluated by the runtime, Rust's as the game's (`arguments_evaluate`), then its body through a new call (`call::LIBRARY`, the arguments' address in `c`; hs_macro_function_evaluate takes them as they are); one without parameters goes straight to its body. Every update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, c20, a30, d20 and b40 (2,048 each, 1,900 to 12,200 library calls). A planted error in the arguments' address was caught at once (2 of 512).
     - [x] The first bodies in Rust (25 October), for queries whose values the check compares in the threads: `unit_get_health`, `unit_get_shield`, `unit_get_total_grenade_count` and `game_difficulty_get` (hs_runtime.c's `hs_runtime_rust_bodies`, which the game's runtime then ran inline too, so the two made the same calls; Rust reads the bits and the object and object list headers from the context). Every update agrees on the five levels, but each runs only once or twice a minute there. `list_count` was held back then: in check mode the lists it counts had been collected before the replay.
     - [x] The check, made robust (25 October). The replay stops at the first call that isn't the game's (`HsHost::stopped`), so a wrong body is reported rather than freezing the game; the planted `game_difficulty_get` error is now reported on a30, c20, d20 and b40 (1023 of 1024 agree). Each Rust body is checked as the game's runs it instead of in the replay (`halo_sim_hs_body_check`): Rust's runs first on the memory as it is, its writes held aside (read back by the body), then the game's runs and each byte Rust wrote is compared with what it left; the replay makes these bodies calls, as recorded. So setters, which read what they change, can be checked, and a difference names the function ("library body list_get differs"). A body no longer runs inline in the game's runtime; it goes through `hs_runtime_call` like the rest. In check mode the update's object list collection waits until after the check, so a body reads the lists the update made. What the check can't see: a byte the game's body writes and Rust's doesn't write at all. `HALO_HS_LIBRARY_COUNTS` logs the functions run most.

5. AI
   - [x] The AI's update (`ai_update`, `actors_update` and `actor_update`; new in `halo_map::ai_update`): Rust is authoritative with `ai_update`, and drives the AI's tick: what runs (the conversations, the encounters and every active actor; or the actors moved at random; or, with the AI switched off, the actors frozen once), the walk over the active actors (each active encounter's list in the encounter array's order, then the actors of no encounter, an actor's next read before its update), an erased actor's removal, and the order of an actor's steps, with the dormant and swarm actors' shorter updates. Each step, and each question the walk asks of the AI's state (the AI's flags, the next encounter, an actor's next and flags), is ai.c's `ai_update_call` (with actors.c's `actors_port_call`), shared by the C (restructured to make the same calls). This is the frame the AI's systems hand over into, one step at a time. In check mode the game's update records each call and answer, and Rust's replay must make the same calls with the same arguments: every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), a30, d20 and b40 (54,678 actor updates), and a planted swap of two of an actor's steps was caught at the first actor. With all thirty-two systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's emotions (`actor_emotion_update`, each actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_emotions`), the first of the AI's steps: Rust is authoritative with `actor_emotions`. Rust ends a berserk actor's rage, follows the defending it was ordered (with its shout), decides whether it is forced to charge, works out the danger it feels from its threats and how it settles, finds whether it blocks a friend's line of fire or walks into a player's (`actor_perception_aiming_vector_test_blockage`), and runs its defensive crouch and timers. An actor's steps share `halo_map::ai_actor`: the actor's datum, props and definition are the game's memory, worked on in place with layouts measured from the game's C (a new probe, `probe_ai.c`), and each step's calls go through a call function in the C. actor_perception.c's `actor_emotion_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers a friend's attack (with its aim), berserk's end, the shout, a discarded firing position, the flood's shamble and the unopposable retreat. The line of fire's vector now starts from zeros: the C's was the stack's leftover when the friend isn't ahead of the aim. In check mode the game records each call with the actor's datum before and after, puts the actor back, runs Rust's replay and compares the actor Rust leaves. Every update agrees in 1-minute runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), d20 and b40: at least 61,440 updates, 67,307 calls (a30 runs no actors in its first minute). A planted error in the danger's settling was caught on d20 (on b30 nobody felt danger in time). With all thirty-three systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's situation (`actor_situation_update`, each actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_situation`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_situation`. Rust counts the enemies the actor knows and the threats they pose (in view, facing it, aiming at it, shooting near or at it, extremely close, damaging it), and the friends near it, in view and close by, by type. It also picks its target (the heaviest prop by target weight, both the old and new target's weights recomputed), and sets that target's status and the actor's combat status and timers. actor_perception.c's `actor_situation_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers a unit's actor, its player, its active regions and a prop's target weight. The step context gained `actor_data` (a friend's type and target are read from its datum). The step writes props, so its check also saves the actor's props as the step began, puts them back for Rust's replay and compares the props Rust leaves (a step's `halo_sim_ai_step_props`). Every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), d20 and b40: at least 30,720 updates, 117,000 calls. A planted error in the target weight's write was caught at the prop's byte on b30's first actors. With all thirty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's upkeep (actors.c's `actor_get_timeslice`, `actor_clear_orders` and `actor_clear_output`, three steps of each actor's update; new in `halo_map::actor_upkeep`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_upkeep`. Rust advances the actor's service timer (three at a time while charging to melee, leap or strafe and ram in a vehicle) and decides whether it gets the tick's thinking time. It also clears its orders before its decision loop and its output (back to its input's facing, aiming and looking) before its control. The AI's service-timer globals go through actors.c's `actor_upkeep_call`, shared by the C (restructured). Every step agrees in 30-second runs of b30 and c20 (throwing grenades), d20 and b40: at least 30,720 timeslices (84,206 calls) and as many clearings. A planted error in the timeslice's threshold was caught on b30 at the call that gives the thinking time. With all thirty-five systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's input (actors.c's `actor_input_update`, each actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_input`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_input`. Rust works out whether the actor is under water, flying, or in a vehicle (driving by the vehicle's AI flags, gunning, riding) and the encounter the vehicle carries it into and back out of. It also finds whether it is burning to death or carrying a melee attacker or a projectile about to explode, whether it is in midair, and where it stands for pathfinding. It sets which way it faces, aims and looks (the looking frame's left and up) and copies its unit's vitality and recent damage. The step context gained `object_header_data`, so a step reads the game's objects in place (`ai_actor::object_get`; unit, biped and projectile layouts added to `probe_ai.c`). actors.c's `actor_input_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers a swarm's refresh, the position's sampling, the water, a vehicle's flags, a gunner's bombardment, the squads' migration, the encounter changes, the firing positions' debug check, a team's enmity and a unit's facing. Every update agrees in runs of b30, a30, d20, c20 and b40 (30 seconds) and a50 and c40 (a minute): at least 49,000 updates. a50 is the one with AI in vehicles, and a planted error in the passenger test was caught there (the five usual levels never seat one). With all thirty-six systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's general update (actors.c's `actor_general_update`, first in each actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_general`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_general`. Rust erases a swarm left without its cache, clears the path's refresh flag, and counts down the suspicion and command list timers. It also wakes the actor, or after two seconds with no reason to stay awake puts it to sleep: no transitory action, no player it sees or pursues, no path to a firing position while guarding for an encounter that follows the players, and no path to a player. actors.c's `actor_general_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers the AI profile's counts, the erasing, an encounter's forced activity and following, the dormancy, the action's class and whether there is a path. A step now returns a result (`AiStepC.result`, `halo_sim_ai_step_result`), and the check compares it with the game's (the timeslice's too). Every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 30,720 updates, 92,000 calls. A planted error in the dormancy's delay was caught on d20 (Rust slept a tick early). With all thirty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Three small steps of an actor's update (new in `halo_map::actor_attention`), each its own system: whether its looking forces how it moves (actor_looking.c's `actor_look_affect_movement`, `actor_look_movement`), its idle vocalizations (ai_communication.c's `actor_communication_update`, `actor_communication`), and its looking at whoever it is in conversation with (action_converse.c's `actor_conversation_control`, `actor_conversation`). Each step's call function is in its own C file (the helpers it needs are private there): the path and the direction's decoding; the dialogue triggers, combat, the idle timer's reset and the idle speech; and a unit's prop and head and the secondary look. The conversation's look direction now starts from zeros: the game left the rest of a prop direction's union as the stack's leftovers, and `actor_look_secondary` copies all of it into the actor. Every step agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50. b30's scripts turn dialogue triggers off, so the idle speech was checked with `HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND="(ai_dialogue_triggers 1)"` (b30 and d20, a minute each). A planted error in the look's path test was caught on b30, and one in the idle timer's reset with dialogue on. No conversation came up in any run, including b30's own conversations started through the script hook (their participants aren't there), so the conversation look is unexercised. With all forty systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's destination (actor_moving.c's `actor_destination_update`, each controlled actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_destination`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_destination`. Rust advances along the path's steps the actor has reached (within 0.15 of the step, or 0.25 past it when its movement is thwarted, or as soon as its movement completes) and works out where it moves towards: the path's step, three units ahead of (or behind) a directional flying vehicle, or nowhere. actor_moving.c's `actor_destination_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers the path's refresh in its timeslice, the destination's test, the path's clearing and the two errors. Every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 30,720 updates, 58,000 calls. A planted error in the moving-towards vector was caught on b30. One in the step radius was not: paths of more than one step didn't come up in time. With all forty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's control of its unit (actors.c's `actor_unit_control`, last in each controlled actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_unit_control`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_unit_control`. Unless a player drives the unit with input enabled, Rust thaws a frozen unit and hands it the actor's output, its animation impulse and its persistent control. actors.c's `actor_unit_control_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers the players' input and does the unit's side: the control data is built in the call from the actor's output. Every step agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50. A planted inversion of the impulse test was caught on b30. With all forty-two systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's perception (`actor_perception_update`, each actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_perception`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_perception`.
     - **What Rust does:** it acknowledges the danger zone and notices it (by chance for a vehicle or projectile), and decides whether the actor may dive from it. For each prop it keeps the timers, decides which props are in use and which is refreshed this tick (enemies every tick, friends an eighth as often, far props half as often), and runs the acknowledgement state machine: the awareness that builds by knowledge and perception, the orphans left when a prop is lost, and the orphans' inspection, lifespan and expiry. It raises the stimuli and shouts of a prop newly killed, sighted or changed in allegiance, the friendly player first seen, combat entered, and the alerts between friends. Last it finds the nearest orphan, the target's history and the props' refresh pace.
     - **The C's side:** actor_perception.c's `actor_perception_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers 29 calls. The global random seed's draws are calls too, so the check replays them. Two C fixes of the same kind as before: an allegiance shout's information starts from zeros, and an unreachable awareness speed takes no awareness.
     - **The check, extended:** perception's calls make, change and delete props, so in check mode each call also records the props it changed and the props' data array as it left them (its count drops as the top prop is freed), plus a checksum of the actor's props before it (reason 6). The step's comparison takes in the props the step made. Rust's prop iterator now ends at a freed prop where the C's would assert.
     - **Results:** every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 32,000 updates, 300,000 calls. Planted errors in the friends' refresh pace and the orphans' lifespan were caught on b30. With all forty-three systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's looking (actor_looking.c's `actor_look_update`, each controlled actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_looking`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_look`. Rust weighs the actor's primary look (its orders, or its target while it fires a burst) against its secondary look (an event it turned to) and its idle looking (a major direction and minor variations), within its definition's aiming and looking limits. It decides which way it faces, aims and looks, whether it faces exactly, its fixed stationary facing, and how fast it aims. The limits' tests (`actor_look_valid_aim_vector`, `actor_look_valid_look_vector`) are in Rust. actor_looking.c's `actor_look_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers a direction's decoding, the idle directions and timers (which draw random numbers), the weapon, the burst, the action's class, forcing a stop, the unit's business, the stimulus and the unit's exact facing. The burst's target direction now starts from zeros: the game left its union as the stack's leftovers, though a target's decoding doesn't read it. Every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 32,000 updates, 180,000 calls. A planted swap of the look limit's two sides was caught on b30. With all forty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's movement (actor_moving.c's `actor_move_update`, each controlled actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_moving`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_move`.
     - **What Rust does:** it blends a flying vehicle's avoidance rotation and turns the movement by it (`rotate_vector_about_axis`). It chooses the movement type (flaming, panicked, asleep, alert, combat) and the crouch. It works out how the vehicle holds the actor still or frees it (a hovering vehicle airborne or tipping, sideslip on the ground, a flying one stuck), and the same for its state (riding, animating, asleep, in midair, throwing a grenade, keeping out of a player's fire). It sets its facing when still, its berserk shout, and its jumps and leaps.
     - **The C's side:** actor_moving.c's `actor_move_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers the vector avoidance and the movement's calculation (both still the C's, into the actor), the vehicle's definition and stuckness, the path, the firing positions, the unit's crouch, jump and leap, the animation, and the shouts. `AiCall.output` now holds six floats.
     - **Results:** every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 32,000 updates, 170,000 calls. A planted alert-to-combat movement type was caught on b30. With all forty-five systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's combat (actor_combat.c's `actor_combat_update`, each controlled actor's every tick; new in `halo_map::actor_combat`): Rust is authoritative with `actor_combat`.
     - **What Rust does:** it keeps the firing timers and picks the target it fires at (its target prop or a manual point) and what it knows of it (range, position, line of sight, water, whether it is outside the players' view). It handles the grenade throw and special fire (overcharge or the secondary trigger, by chance after its delay). It makes the decision to fire and the reason not to, runs the fire state (holding, bursting, pausing, wild), and handles the burst's tracking and leading, its origin (camera, gun offset or head), its aim, its line of fire and the blocked shout, and the trigger's rate of fire. It reads the variant's and firing variant's definitions and the weapon definition in place (the step context gained `variant_definition`). It writes the actor's debug info (the burst's alignment and tracking, the firing decision).
     - **The C's side:** actor_combat.c's `actor_combat_call`, shared by the C (restructured), answers 30 calls: the weapon, the grenades, the difficulty, the random draws, special fire's situations, the players' visibility, the lines of sight and fire, the animation, the bursts' timing, the gun's position and the triggers. Three values the game computed and never used are left out: the special fire delay scale, the overcharge chance and the weapon's first trigger.
     - **The check, extended again:** the first 0x100 bytes of the actor's debug info (of 0x657C) are saved, replayed and compared like its datum, both per call and per step. `AiCall` gained a second result.
     - **Results:** every update agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50: at least 32,000 updates, 250,000 calls. A planted error in the burst's alignment threshold was caught on d20. With all forty-six systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The dispatching steps of an actor's update (new in `halo_map::actor_dispatch`), one system `actor_dispatch`. They are the decision loop (actors.c's `actor_decision_loop`: the type decides the action and the action performs until it settles, or the loop gives up after ten tries with its warning and the action reset), the action's update and control (actions.c), and a swarm's control (actor_types.c). With these every step of an actor's update runs in Rust. The actions and actor types they dispatch to are still the game's, through actors.c's `actor_dispatch_call`, and each will take its place there as it is ported. Every step agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, and a planted error in the loop's exit was caught on d20. With all forty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The first actions in Rust (new in `halo_map::actions`): sleep's control, and wait's, avoid's and fight's perform, update and control. Wait covers the pursuit group's coordinator, its timers and its shouts. Avoid finds a firing position away from danger. Fight handles the defensive crouch's nearby positions, the firing position's choice and time, and whether the target is out of reach.
     - **How they plug in:** the dispatching steps (`actor_dispatch`) run a ported action's function in place of the game's. Each action's calls are numbered `(action << 8) | n`, answered by its own C file's call function (`action_wait_call`, `action_avoid_call`, `action_fight_call`), and actions.c's `actions_port_call` forwards them. For the game's side of a check, actions.c's `actions_port_ported` names the same functions, and the dispatch runs them directly rather than as one call. That way the action's work is compared, not just its call.
     - **Results:** every step agrees in 30-second runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50. A planted error in fight's control was caught on d20. With all forty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels at 144 frames a second.

     - **A bug the check caught:** the handler calls' kinds were `BASE | n` with a base of 0x1040, which from the sixty-fourth folds back onto the first (0x1040 | 67 is 0x1043, the flee-always call). They're `BASE + n` now; in Rust mode those calls would have run the wrong C functions.

   - [x] Perception's refresh in Rust (`halo_map::actor_perception`): actor_perception.c's `actor_perception_refresh` and `actor_perception_refresh_test_object`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - the actor's props it no longer wants, or whose units are outside its potentially visible set, are given up (their orphans first);
       - each object in the set's clusters, and each object's children, is looked at once: a biped it wants (or its swarm's nearest unit) becomes a prop, or waits on the list if it's optional; a suicide's danger is assessed; so is a driverless vehicle's; and a dangerous projectile, if it's near enough and nearer than the projectile the actor fears now, becomes the danger zone (with its owner's hostility);
       - of the optional props, the nearest are kept: up to four enemies (the game's early exit, which gives up the entry that reached the limit, is kept), then friends up to two past the enemies (at least four props); the rest are given up.
     - **The sort:** `halo_map::msvc_qsort` is the Windows C runtime's `qsort`, ported. It isn't stable, so it must match the game's order when priorities tie. Its order matched the UCRT's over 200,000 arrays with many ties, both in C (built for 32-bit Windows) and in its own test.
     - **The C's side, through `actor_perception_call`:** the potentially visible set (kept by the game) and whether a unit is in it; walking it cluster by cluster, and each cluster's collideable and noncollideable objects; the object marker; a biped's sense position and swarm unit; a unit's status; prop creation; the suicide and vehicle dangers; a projectile's danger, sense position and owner; and the overflow warning.
     - **The check:** the step check now records up to 4,096 calls a step (256 before), because the refresh marks every object in view.
     - **Results:** the perception step agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out. A planted error in the friends' limit was caught on b30. With all forty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

     - **What Rust does:** the danger zone follows its object, and is forgotten if the object is gone. It tracks the object's position, velocity and distance from the actor, and the sphere the object may sweep in a second and a half. Then it decides whether the actor perceives it:
       - a vehicle, while it moves nearby: through its driver's prop, or else by sight or sound;
       - a projectile: if it's attached to the actor, or seen within its perception radius (its fuse is timed);
       - a suicide: through its prop (its death is timed).
       A newly perceived danger draws a look.
     - **The C's side, through `actor_perception_call`:** the object's origin and the sense position to it (kept by the game), the object's details (the encounter's blindness, a vehicle's bounding radius, a projectile's perception radius and fuse, a suicide's dying frames), a unit's active prop, the line of sight, visibility and audibility, and the look.
     - **Results:** the perception step agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out. A planted error in the danger's prediction was caught on b30. With all forty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
