   - [x] Choosing firing positions in Rust (new in `halo_map::firing_positions`): actor_firing_position.c's `actor_select_firing_position` and `actor_active_select_firing_position`, with their evaluators, `actor_get_firing_position_group`, and the discarded positions; and action_flee.c's `action_flee_find_flee_position`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - it gathers the encounter's positions in the groups the actor's squad allows, leaving out those another actor holds unless the actor is much nearer;
       - it measures each one's path from the actor and from its target, and scores it by mode (fight, panic, cover, uncover, guard, pursue, avoid) for:
         - the path's length;
         - range from the target;
         - the danger zone's clearance;
         - friends' and dangerous enemies' lines of fire;
         - the positions given up lately and the points to avoid;
         - a flying driver's heading;
         - for a pursuit, whether and when the encounter looked there;
       - it sorts the positions with sort.c's `qsort_4byte` (ported) and tests line of sight from the best down, until no position left can beat the best.
       - The fight, avoidance, guard, uncover and pursuit actions now choose in Rust, and so does the flee (setting up, and finding a new position). Rust also makes the writes when a position is given up, or the given-up list is cleared, in the actions, the emotions and the move update.
     - **The C's side, through the new `actor_firing_position_call` (call kinds `(14 << 8) | n`):**
       - the path searches (the actor's, kept for the change, and the target's) and their distances;
       - 3D paths, lines of sight, a unit's estimated head or gun, and friends' and enemies' aim;
       - the encounter's owners and pursuit records;
       - the random draw, the change of position, and the flee's approach point.
     - **The C mirror:** the original selection, with each of those game queries made through the call, is what the game runs under the check. The C flee search used to pass an uninitialized owner to the change when it found nothing (harmlessly ignored), and now passes NONE.
     - **Results:**
       - The dispatch steps (the decision loop and the actions' update and control), the emotion update and the move update agree in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out.
       - Planted errors were each caught on b30: the actor's search range, the fight's line-of-sight score, the panic flee's attractor, and the given-up list's wrap.
       - With all forty-seven systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The path search in Rust (new in `halo_map::path`, a new system `paths`): path.c's `path_state_find` and `path_state_estimated_distance`, which every path the AI plans and every firing position it weighs goes through.
     - **What Rust does:** an A* search over the structure BSP's collision surfaces, from the start's surface through each edge into a walkable (and, unless told otherwise, unbroken) neighbour. Costs are kept in tenths of a unit on a heap of 1,024 nodes found through a hash of the surfaces; an attractor makes the stretches near it dearer, and a bound limits how far the search goes. Towards a destination it enters each long edge at the point nearest it, keeps the node nearest it (within four units, the nearest point of the surface, from collision_bsp.c's `collision_surface_find_closest_point2d`, ported), and gives up when no node can come close. The estimated distance adds a reached surface's straight line to its node's path distance, with the closest approach to the attractor and the direction the path sets off in.
     - **Where it runs:** on the game's own path state and debug storage, in place, reading the BSP's surfaces, edges, vertices, planes and pathfinding flags and the breakable surfaces' flags from the game's memory. path.c calls halo_sim.c's `halo_sim_path_find` and `halo_sim_path_estimated_distance`, which run the game's or Rust's.
     - **The check:** a function-level one. The game saves the path state (82 KB) and its debug storage (117 KB), runs its own search, puts them back, runs Rust's, compares every byte and the result, and keeps its own.
     - **Results:**
       - Every search and estimated distance agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, b40 and a50, with none left out (c20 searched no paths in its first minute).
       - Planted errors in the attractor's cost and in the distance within which the nearest point of a surface is found were each caught on b30.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The paths' 2D tests, smoothing and approach point in Rust (`halo_map::path`, under `paths`): path_structure_bsp.c's `structure_test_line2d`, `structure_test_pill2d` and `structure_surface_index_from_point` (with `structure_test_ray2d` and `structure_surfaces_are_equivalent`, ported for the obstacle avoidance to come), collision_bsp.c's `collision_surface_test_line2d`, path_smoothing.c's `path_smooth`, and path.c's `path_state_approach_point`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - The line test walks the walkable surfaces from edge to edge until it reaches the far point's surface, or stops a 128th of the edge short of an unwalkable (or broken) neighbour. Where its first surface doesn't lead back to the start, it retries once from the surface's centre.
       - The pill test runs a line along each side of a swept circle and keeps the nearer blocking one (one within the radius of the end doesn't count).
       - The smoothing sweeps a 0.3 circle from the start to the furthest raw step it can reach. When a wall blocks it, it walks the unwalkable boundary both ways to the corners it must turn about, takes the one that turns least (from arccosines, the C's double `acos`), and steps to where its 0.35 tangents meet, four steps at most.
       - The approach point is the furthest node back along the search's path that the point sees in a straight line.
     - **The C's side:** each goes through a halo_sim.c hook that runs the game's or Rust's. Under the check the hook runs both and compares their results: the collision result without its padding (the game leaves the caller's bytes there), the smoothed steps, the approach point.
     - **A C fix:** the pill test's side results start from zeros. A side whose start is on no surface set only its collision flag, and the game could copy the stack's leftovers out as the result.
     - **Results:**
       - Every test agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out: 16,384 to 65,536 line tests, and 128 to 512 smoothings, on each of b30, d20, b40 and a50.
       - On b30 every line and pill test was clear, so a planted error at a blocking edge (its 128th) went unseen there; it was caught on d20, b40 and a50.
       - A planted error in the smoothing's tangent radius was caught on d20.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Building a path in Rust (`halo_map::path`, under `paths`): path.c's `path_state_build_path`, with path_obstacle_avoidance.c's `path_avoid_obstacles` (its planar search) and path_obstacles.c's discs (grouping, circle, pill and tangent tests). With it, the whole of path.c's search and build is Rust's; only the gathering of the objects' discs stays the game's.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - It takes the search's nodes back from the destination (or the node nearest it, within its radius) to the start as raw steps, up to 64, and smooths them.
       - For each smoothed step it finds a way round the objects near its start. The discs come from the objects' pathfinding spheres within four units (and the attractor's, which may be passed through); discs that touch, with the radius between, are grouped into obstacles.
       - The way round is a second A* over steps that sweep the circle towards the goal and, when they meet a disc, step past its tangents on both sides. It never wraps an obstacle both ways, prefers the step that comes nearest when the goal itself is blocked, and retries through the passable discs when there is no way.
       - It writes the result (four steps at most, and whether they finish the path) and, with debug storage, the raw, smoothed and avoided steps and each step's obstacles and search.
     - **The C's side:** the build goes through halo_sim.c's `halo_sim_path_build`, which hands Rust the game's `obstacles_get_discs_in_sphere` (the objects in a sphere, their collision models' pathfinding spheres at their nodes). Under the check, the game saves the path result and debug storage, runs its own build, puts them back, runs Rust's, compares every byte, and keeps its own.
     - **Results:**
       - Every build agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, b40 and a50 (128 to 512 on each), with none left out.
       - A planted error in the discs' tangent margin was caught on b30 and d20.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The movement's calculation in Rust (`halo_map::actor_moving`, under `actor_move`): actor_moving.c's `actor_move_calculate_movement`, with `actor_move_calculate_controlled_by_aiming` and `actor_move_calculate_free`. The move update no longer hands it to the game.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - It picks the facing: the override's (forward, back, left or right of the movement), a free facing for flyers sidestepping, the side nearest a forced aim that turns least, or straight along the movement.
       - It lets the actor move only when it faces close enough to the way it goes. The margin tightens to 0.95 when a 0.4 line ahead on the walkable surfaces is blocked (Rust's own `structure_test_line2d`).
       - It marks the movement complete within the destination tolerance and eases the throttle down inside the stopping distance.
       - For a vehicle with AI steering (the banshee), it clamps the turn and holds an oversteer angle until the turn drops below its lower bound. The angles use the C's double `acos`, `sin` and `cos`.
     - **The C's side:** the step context now carries the structure and the breakable surface flags. Four new calls are the game's: finding the pathfinding location, the destination tolerance, the stopping distances (the vehicle's and the flying biped's definitions) and the debug switch that turns oversteer off. The game's calculation makes the same calls in the same order. The `print_oversteer` console lines stay the game's alone.
     - **Results:**
       - Every move step agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50 (4,096 to 32,768 on each), with none left out.
       - No AI steers in the first minute of any probe level, and only the banshee's tag has AI steering (the ghost and warthog have none). A console command at tick 300 (`HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND`) seats the `beach_banshee` encounter in b30's two banshees. With them flying, every step still agrees, and planted errors in the steered angle and its clamp were caught.
       - Planted errors in the forward throttle (b30) and in choosing a side under a forced aim (b30 and d20) were caught. One in the blocked-wall facing margin (0.95 to 0.97) went unseen in a minute of b30 and d20.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An actor's path to its destination in Rust (`halo_map::actor_path`, under `actor_destination` and `actor_dispatch`): actor_moving.c's `actor_path_refresh`, `actor_move_to_point`, `actor_move_to_move_position`, `actor_move_to_firing_position`, `actor_move_to_prop`, `actor_move_halt` and `actor_move_halt_at_firing_position`, with `actor_path_clear` and `actor_test_destination`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - A move-to writes the move orders and hands them to the path when they change (another point more than 0.1 away, another position, prop or accept radius). Otherwise it refreshes the path in the actor's timeslice. It wakes a dormant actor.
       - The refresh finds the destination from the orders: a raw point, a squad's move position, an encounter's firing position, or a prop's pathfinding point (its centre of mass for a flyer). It finds a new path unless the actor is already there and the destination hasn't moved, notes the time in the actor's debug info, and clears the path when the actor is already within the prop's radius.
       - The destination update and every action that moves an actor (alert, search, guard, flee, converse, vehicle, charge, wait, fight and obey, with the charge's and vehicle's handlers) call these directly instead of asking the game.
     - **The C's side:** these functions stay the game's for the steps that aren't ported, and now make their own calls (`actor_path_call`, kinds `15 << 8`, which the dispatch step's action router and the destination step's call function pass on). Those calls cover waking the actor, a prop's pathfinding location, the flyers' test and path, the destination tolerance, and the walkers' path (its input, ignored target, the danger zone's attractor, then the search and build, both Rust's through `paths`). The firing position change keeps its cached path state, a call only the game makes. The actions' and the handler's move and halt calls are gone from both sides.
     - **Results:**
       - Every destination update, decision loop, action update and action control agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out. b30 with AI pilots in its banshees also agrees.
       - Planted errors in the prop's accept radius (d20), the original distance (b30) and the radius at which a path is dropped (d20) were caught. One in a raw point's re-order distance (0.1 to 0.3) went unseen on b30 (also with the banshees) and d20.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The flyers' vector avoidance in Rust (`halo_map::actor_avoidance`, under `actor_move`): actor_moving.c's `actor_move_vector_avoidance`, with its ray test, its direction finding, the rays of `actor_move_initialize`, and real_math.c's `pill_test_vector3d`, `sphere_test_vector3d` and `angle_between_vectors3d`. With it, an actor's whole move update is Rust's apart from the game's queries.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - Nine sense rays ahead and sixteen avoidance rays (two in each of eight directions round the vehicle) test the structure and the cylinders of the objects nearby.
       - Each ray weighs the directions: a blocked one against, and one clear for long enough for. Weights spread to the neighbouring directions, with a bias towards the direction the actor last chose and against the way the vehicle is already turning.
       - It turns the vehicle sharply towards the best direction when the movement points behind it, rotates it about its forward axis when the best direction is well off the movement, or steers away from what the sense rays meet. It also keeps the avoidance's debug record (rays, weights, choice).
     - **The C's side:** the object setup (`actor_move_avoidance_setup`: the bipeds in a sphere, their pathfinding spheres' cylinders) stays the game's. A new move call gathers it, with the vehicle's origin and axes, into the actor's debug info, where Rust reads it. The structure's rays are Rust's own, through a new `AiHost::structure_test_vector` on the simulation's copy of the collision BSP; the game passes no breakable surface flags, so every surface stands. The step context now carries the structure BSP's index.
     - **Results:**
       - Only an AI flying a vehicle runs this, so it was checked with the `beach_banshee` encounter seated in b30's banshees. Every move step agrees, with none left out.
       - Planted errors in the emergency scale, in the objects' pill test and in a structure ray's t were caught. One in the weight of the last chosen direction (0.4 to 0.41) went unseen.
       - With all forty-eight systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second, and on b30 with the banshee pilots.
   - [x] The encounters' update in Rust (`halo_map::encounters`, a new system `encounters`), checked by a new kind of check for updates over the game's arrays.
     - **What Rust does:** encounters.c's `encounters_update`, with `encounter_update_status`, `_timers`, `_respawn`, `_squads`, `_platoons`, `_follow`, `encounter_control_actors`, `encounter_test_rule` and `encounters_update_dirty_status` (and actors.c's `actor_in_combat` and `actor_is_fighting`).
       - Each active encounter in its turn (one tick in every fifteen) counts its actors, swarms and strength by squad and platoon, and whether its enemies are visible or alive.
       - It keeps its enemy timers, and stays active, stands down or begins its post-combat.
       - It respawns its squads below their minimum, and one random squad below its maximum once its delay runs out.
       - It runs out its squads' delay timers and tests its platoons' maneuvering and attacking/defending rules.
       - It moves its automatically migrating squads to the one whose firing positions lie nearest its follow target (its players, a unit, or an AI's actors).
       - It gives its actors their orders: standing down, playfighting, the post-combat cleared, defending, and the maneuver to another squad.
     - **The C's side:** these calls stay the game's: activating the encounters and encounterless actors, standing down, the post-combat, spawning an actor, the random seed, a squad's timer running out, the follow targets, an object's origin, moving an actor to another squad, and the maneuvering stimulus (encounters.c's `encounter_call`). The rules' and respawns' debug console lines stay the game's alone.
     - **The world steps:** a check for an update that isn't one actor's step (halo_sim.c's `halo_sim_world_step_*`, crates/halo_sim's `world_steps`).
       - The C names the memory the update works on: the encounters, squads, platoons, pursuits and the actors in use, the data arrays' headers, `ai_globals` and the profile's meter.
       - The game's update runs first. Each of its calls is recorded with a checksum of that memory before it and the runs of bytes it changed.
       - The game puts the memory back. Rust replays the update, compares each call's kind, arguments and the memory's checksum before it, and applies the call's changes as the game's made them.
       - The game then compares the memory with its own byte for byte, reports the first difference by region, and keeps its own.
     - **Results:**
       - Every update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, a30, d20, c20, b40 and a50 (1,024 on each), with none left out.
       - Planted errors in the strength's epsilon, the update's phase and the actors' defending order were caught. One at the boundary of the 50%-dead rule went unseen on b30 and d20.
       - With all forty-nine systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] An encounter's post-combat and standing down in Rust (`halo_map::encounters`, under `encounters`): encounters.c's `encounter_post_combat` (with its possibilities and random choice) and `encounter_stand_down`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - The post-combat weighs each actor's dead props by distance, age and the actor's player rating, and adds the actor itself as a celebration after an enemy's death.
       - It picks a behavior at random by weight (shooting a corpse, checking an enemy or a friend, celebrating), and a second one for another actor when the encounter isn't human or has beaten eight enemies.
       - It picks the best-rated actor outside the communication team to speak of the fight (wounded, alone, a massacre, triumph, unscathed). A wounded speaker's nearest friend who sees it runs to it.
       - Standing down forgets the encounter's pursuit and its actors' orphaned enemy props, except each actor's target.
       - Both keep the original's slip: a candidate that beats the first slot fills the second too.
     - **The C's side:** these calls stay the game's: the player rating, the random seed, the communication team, a unit's active prop, the actor definition's no-corpse-shooting flag, clearing the pursuit, and dropping a prop (switching from it and deleting it). The props (their data array and the ones in use) are now part of the update's checked memory, since standing down deletes them.
     - **Results:**
       - Every update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, a30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out.
       - No encounter stands down or finishes a fight in a probe level's first minute. With the console killing b30's `beach_lz` at tick 900, a planted error in standing down was caught.
       - The post-combat never ran, even with that kill at tick 2400 and a run of two and a half minutes, so it agrees without being exercised.
       - With all forty-nine systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The conversations' update in Rust (`halo_map::conversations`, a new system `conversations`, the second world step): ai_communication.c's `ai_conversation_update`, with `ai_conversation_line_begin`, `ai_conversation_line_perform` and `ai_conversation_line_end`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - Each conversation tries to begin once a second until it can or gives up.
       - It speaks its lines in turn. Each line finds its speaker, addressee (the player or another participant), sound and delay, and is unspatialized over the radio.
       - A line waits for its speaker or everyone to come near, for scripted dialogue to end and the speaker to be free, then for the line to finish, its delay to run out and, if it asks, to be told to advance.
       - The conversation finishes after its last line. Until then it points its participants' attention at the speaker or the addressee as the line's flags ask.
     - **The C's side:** these calls stay the game's (ai_communication.c's `conversation_call`): beginning a conversation (choosing its participants), finishing it, whether scripted dialogue is playing, a scripted sound and its time, a unit's speech test, and its speech. The line and participant views' offsets are now asserted in the C. The conversations' console lines stay the game's alone.
     - **Results:**
       - Every update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, a30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out. Only a30 (170 calls) and a50 (54) begin conversations early.
       - A planted error in a line's delay was caught on a30.
       - Plants in the participants' attention went unseen: the early conversations' lines have no actor speaking to them, so the attention loop agrees without being exercised.
       - With all fifty systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The encounters' activation in Rust (`halo_map::encounters`, under `encounters`): encounters.c's `encounters_test_activation`, `encounter_compute_activation_cluster_bit_vector`, `encounter_activate`, `encounter_deactivate`, `encounterless_activate` and `encounterless_deactivate`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - Once a second, each encounterless actor (or swarm) wants to sleep unless its unit's outermost parent stands in a cluster the players can see (their combined PVS). It is made active or, after its three-second timer, dormant.
       - Each encounter in the current structure gathers the clusters of its actors, of its active actors' targets, of the firing positions they fight, guard or pursue from, and of the move positions of squads under alert orders, marking each actor's wish to sleep as it goes.
       - The encounter stays active for five seconds after any of those clusters is visible, or while a linked encounter is. Otherwise its actors are made dormant and its pursuit forgotten.
     - **The C's side:** making an actor active or dormant, its activation's verification, and a cached swarm's visibility stay the game's. The step context now carries the players' combined PVS, the structure's cluster count, and whether the game is in the editor or a local game forces every actor active.
     - **Results:**
       - Every encounters' update agrees in minute-long runs of the six levels, with none left out (875 to 3,064 calls on each).
       - Planted errors in the remain-active time and an actor's cluster visibility were caught on b30.
       - With all fifty systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] A communication event in Rust (`halo_map::communication`, a new system `communication`, the third world step): ai_communication.c's `ai_communication_event`, the choice of who says what when something happens to a unit.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - It finds the subject's and the cause's actors, races and friend groups.
       - A death between allied teams may be a betrayal, observed by a friend nearby, and an allegiance incident.
       - It works out the enemy's status (never seen, dead, lost, unseen, no danger, visible) from the subject's encounter or its own targets, and the two communication teams' speech timers by priority and distance.
       - It tests each of the event type's dialogue usages (read from the game's own table) against the hostility, scripted dialogue, the enemy's status, the races and the damage. It finds the speaker (the subject, the cause, a friend or an enemy, or a reply to a player) and weighs it by the player rating, its speech and animation, how recently the line was heard and its timeout.
       - It picks one at random by weight (or among those that force themselves) and speaks it or replies with it.
     - **The C's side:** these calls stay the game's (ai_communication.c's `communication_call`): the teams' allegiance, an actor type's race, finding an actor to talk (global or in an encounter) or to reply to a player, the allegiance incident and its debug line, the player rating, the communication team, the speech and animation tests, an actor's action class and the random seed. Speaking or replying (the unit's speech, its animation turned to the recipient, the speaker's look, the speech timers) is one call on the chosen possibility, which both sides write field by field into a static the check compares. The dialogue usage's and possibility's layouts are asserted in the C. The communication's debug lines stay the game's alone.
     - **Results:**
       - Every event agrees in minute-long runs of b30 (2,048 events), d20 (512), b40 (64), a50 (16) and c20 (4), with none left out; a30 has none early.
       - Planted errors in the repeat rating and the speech timers' delay were caught on b30.
       - Planted errors in the player's additional delay and the animation's weight went unseen: no player-caused speech or animation was chosen in those minutes.
       - With all fifty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] Evasion in Rust (`halo_map::actor_moving`, under `actor_dispatch`): actor_moving.c's `actor_move_try_evasion_vector` and `actor_move_try_evasion_direction`, which the evading, diving and danger-avoiding transitions ask.
     - **What Rust does:** an evasion sweeps a circle of the actor's pathfinding radius over the walkable surfaces (Rust's own pill test) and refuses a climb, or without a ledge height a drop, of more than half the distance. With a ledge height it may instead clear the structure ahead and drop no further than that (Rust's own structure rays). A random side picks one by the random seed and tries the other if it fails.
     - **The C's side:** finding the actor's pathfinding location and the random seed are new path calls (`_actor_path_call_find_pathfinding_location`, `_seed_random`). The transitions on both sides call the evasion directly, and the actions' three evasion calls are gone.
     - **Results:**
       - Every decision loop, action update and action control agrees in minute-long runs of the levels, with none left out.
       - A planted error in the random side's threshold was caught on b30 and d20. One in the climb limit went unseen (the early evasions are over level ground).
       - With all fifty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The grenade's target and collateral damage in Rust (`halo_map::actor_transitions`, under `actor_dispatch`): actor_combat.c's `actor_combat_find_grenade_target` and `actor_combat_check_collateral_damage`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - The target is the target prop's body a little above it, if the prop is a live enemy that is acknowledged (or an uninspected orphan) within the variant's grenade range.
       - The check counts the threats within the enemy radius: a player ten, a vehicle five, an actor one or its swarm's units, and the target's encounter's other actors once each. It refuses when a friendly prop, or one of the actor's own encounter, stands within the collateral radius.
     - **The C's side:** a playfighting grenade's random miss (world collision rays and the random seed) is a new handler call. The trajectory's planning (the projectile's aim and the ballistic line of fire) stays a call, and the actions' two calls are gone. The step context now carries the encounters' data array.
     - **Results:**
       - Every decision loop agrees in minute-long runs of the levels, with none left out.
       - A planted error in the target's height was caught on b30. One in a player's threat (ten to nine) can't show, since it still clears the minimum count.
       - With all fifty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The choice of a vehicle and its seat in Rust (`halo_map::vehicle_choice`, under `actor_dispatch`): action_vehicle.c's `action_vehicle_desirable`, `action_vehicle_evaluate_seat`, `action_vehicle_find_impromptu_seat` and `action_vehicle_find_destination`, for the vehicle transition's impromptu entry and the vehicle action.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - A vehicle is worth entering unless it's dead or upturned, too far, or (for a new attempt) moving.
       - A seat must be free and allowed (noncombatants only where the definition forbids vehicle combat), and not claimed by a friend nearer it. It is weighed by distance and by the variant's gunner or passenger preference, and the best one is chosen.
       - The destination walks round the vehicle's sphere (or its AI avoidance radius) towards the seat's entrance or hint point, steps sideways when close, and drops onto the ground below (Rust's own structure ray, now giving its surface). It keeps the avoidance's debug record.
     - **The C's side:** the seats' status (filled, for noncombatants, a gunner's), their entrance and hint points, the vehicle's shape (entrances inside its sphere, its seats' count, its sphere and avoidance radius) and an object's origin are calls in a range of their own (`vehicle_choice_call`, `17 << 8`). The vehicle action and the impromptu entry call the choice directly on both sides, and five old calls are gone.
     - **Results:**
       - Every decision loop, action update and action control agrees in minute-long runs of a50, b30, d20, c20 and b40, with none left out.
       - A planted error in a seat's range was caught on a50. Two others fell in runs where no actor boarded a vehicle, which is timing-dependent there.
       - With all fifty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
   - [x] The pursuit's desires and availability in Rust (`halo_map::actor_transitions`, under `actor_dispatch`): encounters.c's `encounter_modify_pursuit_desires` and `encounter_determine_pursuit_availability`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - An encounter or squad that never searches forbids the actor's pursuit, and one that searches forever pursues tenaciously.
       - The encounter's other actors of the same coordination are counted as searching, fleeing or pursuing, against the limits (six, four, and a third of those in combat but at least three, or none, or any).
       - The actor leads a group's pursuit when enough are near it, or follows one.

   - [x] The examined pursuit positions in Rust (`halo_map::pursuits`, under `actor_dispatch`): encounters.c's `encounter_find_pursuit`, `encounter_mark_examined_pursuit_position` and `encounter_pursuit_position_already_examined`.

       - Each recorded call stores the arrays' checksum before it and its runs of changed bytes, as the world steps do.

   - [x] The encounter's and squad's state in the dispatch in Rust (`halo_map::actor_transitions` and `halo_map::encounters`, under `actor_dispatch` and `encounters`).
     - **What Rust does:**
       - It reads whether the encounter stands down and whether the actor's squad is delayed.
       - It marks the encounter's last grenade throw.
       - It expires a squad's delay timer (`encounter_squad_timer_expire`), both in transitions and in the encounters' update.
       - The magic sight that a squad's flag asks for after its timer stays the game's (`ai_scripting_magically_see_players` touches other actors), as a new call made only when the flag is set. The rules' console line is the game's alone.
     - **The check:** the dispatch steps' check also follows the squads' array (32 KB), and the step context gains `squad_array`. The grenade timer stays a call, since the difficulty's team values are the game's.
     - **Results:**
       - Every decision loop, action update, action control and encounters' update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, a30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out. The decision loop's calls fell by about a third on b30 and by half on a50.
       - A planted error in the squad timer was caught on d20 by the encounters' update. Transitions' own expiry, by an actor in combat while its squad waits, didn't happen in these runs. A planted error in the grenade mark was caught on b30.
