   - [x] The AI's senses in Rust (`halo_map::sensing`, under `actor_perception`, `actor_combat` and `actor_dispatch`): ai.c's `ai_test_line_of_sight`, actor_perception.c's `actor_visibility_at_point` (with `actor_get_vision_distances`) and `actor_audibility_at_point`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Line of sight:** the clusters' PVS from the structure in game memory, then the game's whole ray test in Rust (structure and sight-blocking objects, through a new `AiHost::world_test_vector`), widened about an enemy player. Blocked lines are classed as from cover, to cover or obstructed by distance.
       - **Visibility:** the vision range scaled by knowledge, lighting, pitch and the horizontal angle's full and partial distances, noting a player's sighting in the debug info.
       - **Audibility:** hearing scaled by facing, mode, the sound's type, deafening background sounds and line of sight, against the structure's encoded sound distances. The heard unit's constant sound comes from its definition.
       - A tag's data is now found by its index as `tag_get` finds it (the step context gains the loaded tags' table). That resolves the unit definitions and looping sounds.
       - An actor senses from its own input position. A swarm actor senses from its sampled unit's, which the game samples; the rest of that position is a new call. The sense-position call stays, because the game keeps the position for the danger assessments.
       - The firing positions' and combat's manual line of sight use the same function.
     - **Left to the game:**
       - Fog is none: `scenario_fog_at_point` is zero in this build.
       - The firing variant behind the vision range stays a call.
       - The AI profile's meters, the collision user stack and the line-of-sight debug switches are the game's alone.
       - The danger zone's sensing and the search's pursuit sighting (by an estimated unit position) stay calls.
     - **Results:**
       - Every perception, combat, decision loop and action update agrees in minute-long runs of b30, d20, c20, b40 and a50, with none left out.
       - Planted errors were caught: the cover distance on b30 and a50, the sound distance on b30, the vision angles on b30, and a swarm's sense cluster on d20 and c20. A planted error in the pitch limit went unseen: targets that far above an actor's view are rare.
       - With all fifty-one systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
6. Networking ✅
   - **The way in:** OpenCE's networking is about 35,000 lines: the game's own messages and lockstep client/server (`source/networking`), the port's co-op and distributed netcode (`port/linux/game/network_*.c`), and Bungie's transport. It moves one checkable piece at a time, starting from the bytes on the wire. A system link session on one machine (OpenCE's `HALO_NETWORK_TEST`, host and join on loopback addresses) exercises it, with each machine's per-second player positions compared.
   - [x] The packet codec in Rust (`halo_map::data_packets`, under `data_packets`): memory/data_packets.c, data_encoding.c and data_packet_groups.c. The game's messages and its key agreement go through it.
     - **What Rust does:** a packet's struct is described by its definition's fields (bytes, shorts, longs, 64-bit integers, strings, sized data, nested arrays, padding). It is encoded big-endian with its version byte first and its type's header byte last, and decoded back. This is the C's walk exactly:
       - fields' sizes as verification leaves them
       - fields a version leaves out
       - bad counts failing the packet
       - decoding's byte swap of the received buffer in place

       halo_sim reads the group's definitions where the game holds them.
     - **The check:** each encoding is compared with Rust's of the same struct: result, error, size and bytes. Each decoding is compared with Rust's of the same received bytes, from copies kept before the game's: the struct, the type, the version, the size and the swapped buffer.
     - **Found on the way:** the packet table's order doesn't match the message enum for types 8 to 10. The pregame keep-alive (a 2-byte struct) is encoded and decoded with the graceful exit's definition (one long). Its decoding writes two bytes past the struct, onto the caller's message size. The begin-game message, in turn, goes out with only its first short.
       - Rust kept this exactly. It writes the caller's size before the struct, as the game does, so the same overrun landed the same way. The check compares the size the game set.
       - **Fixed afterwards:** the table is now in the enum's order, and the network version went from 24 to 25, so builds before and after don't join each other.
         - The begin-game message now carries all of the host's time. A late joiner used to get only its low 16 bits, so a game more than about 36 minutes in started it at the wrong time until the first update.
         - A late join (the joiner 30 seconds into the game) has all 67 of its seconds within 5 cm of the host's.
     - **Results:**
       - In minute-long sessions on Blood Gulch, every encoding (2,048 on the host) and decoding (1,024) agrees on both machines.
       - A planted error in the 4-byte swap was caught at once. A planted error in a string's length bound went unseen: no string in the traffic fills its field.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With both machines' codec in Rust, the machines' player positions agree each second as they do with the C. The one difference is a millimetre on the joining player at one tick, in both cases.
         - A Rust host and a C joiner agree on all 51 seconds, with seven hits dealt across the link.
         - With all fifty-two systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] The distributed netcode's wire format in Rust (`halo_map::distributed_wire`, under `distributed_wire`): network_distributed.c's packers, writers and readers.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **16-bit packing:** vectors a part at a time, unit vectors, vitalities and angles.
       - **Unit state:** each player's unit, every tick, with only the parts that are not their defaults: riding, tilt, damage, powerups, camouflage, the killer.
       - **Relayed action:** each player's input as the host ran it, filled from the player's action, with each earlier tick's buttons only where they changed.
     - **The C's side:** each function's body stays as the game's, behind a wrapper that runs Rust in Rust's mode and compares in check mode. One generic call (`halo_sim_distributed_wire`) covers all twelve.
     - **Results:**
       - In an 80-second session with shooting, kills and a vehicle, every call of eleven of the twelve agrees on both machines: 32,768 vector packs, 16,384 vitality packs, 4,096 unit state writes and so on. The unit-vector unpack never came up.
       - **Planted errors:**
         - Caught with scripted controller play (`HALO_TEST_INPUT=bot:<seed>`): the angle's rounding on the host, and the relayed action's history bits on the joiner.
         - Caught on its own: a vitality packing error.
         - Unseen: the unit state's tilt test. Units on foot are never tilted.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With both machines' wire layer and codec in Rust and players moving, the machines' positions differ by the same amount as with the C (median 3.8 cm, largest 9 cm against 8 cm).
         - A Rust host and a C joiner play together.
         - With all fifty-three systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] The netcode's sampling and checks of the game in Rust (`halo_map::distributed_wire`, under `distributed_wire`): network_distributed.c's `distributed_state_from_player` and `distributed_axes_make_valid`, network_objects.c's `distributed_state_from_object`, `distributed_inventory_from_unit`, `distributed_transform_valid`, `distributed_vector_clamp`, `distributed_object_state_unpack` and `distributed_checksum`.
     - **What Rust does:** the host's sampling of the game, read straight from its memory (new layout probe `probe_net.c`):
       - **Each player's unit, every tick:** alive or dead, riding, placed, its shields' state and vitalities, powerups, camouflage, where its client predicted it, and who killed it.
       - **Each object's state:** at rest, dead, position, axes and velocities.
       - **Each unit's inventory:** grenades, weapons, their magazines and age.

       The receiver's checks: a transform within the world with finite velocities, axes made a rotation, a velocity held to a speed. The inventories' checksum.
     - **The C's side:** wrappers as for the wire format. Each keeps a copy of its inputs before the game's runs, because callers pass their forward and up as the valid axes. The netcode's own tables give the player's prediction and death.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, every call of the eight agrees on both machines: 8,192 object states, 4,096 player states, 2,048 inventories, 8,192 transforms tested and so on.
       - Planted errors in the object's at-rest flag and in the prediction's echo window were caught on the host.
       - Two went unseen. A weapon's age scale: nobody carries a plasma weapon, so every age is 0. The axes' squareness limit: the host's axes are always square.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With both machines' codec, wire format and sampling in Rust, the machines' positions differ as with the C: median 3.7 cm against 3.6, largest 15 against 13.
         - The items made and removed nearly match the C's.
         - With all fifty-three systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] A client applying the host's state in Rust (`halo_map::network_apply`, under `network_apply`): network_distributed.c's `distributed_apply_state`, network_objects.c's `network_objects_correct`, `network_objects_reconcile` and `distributed_object_move`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - A unit is moved toward where the host has it, when further than the tolerance, with its velocity held to how fast it may go across, up and down.
       - An object is put where the host has it: all the way when further than the blend distance, or when it follows the host (an unsteered vehicle, a co-op cutscene). Otherwise it goes halfway, with its drawing smoothed over the move.
       - Its velocities and at-rest flag are written in the object's datum.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_apply_call`):** setting the object's position (`object_set_position`, which moves it among the clusters), smoothing its drawing, and whether co-op and a cutscene are on. The C's own versions make the same calls in the same order.
     - **The check:** the encounters' world steps, generalised. The world context gains an `arguments` pointer: each step's arguments are taken by value, and its result is written back in a compared region. The regions are the object's datum, the arguments and the corrections count. Nested steps (a reconcile within an apply) are part of the outer one.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, every apply and reconcile agrees on both machines, with none left out: 1,024 applies on each, 256 and 128 reconciles. The object correct, for predicted vehicles alone, didn't come up.
       - Planted errors in the halfway blend, the drawing correction's condition and the angular velocity's write were caught on both machines. One in the fall-speed clamp went unseen: no client predicted a fall faster than the host's bound.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all four networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ as with the C: median 3.8 cm against 3.6, largest 14 against 13.
         - With all fifty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] A client's own unit and the host's word on every player's unit in Rust (`halo_map::network_apply`, world steps 6 and 7): network_distributed.c's `distributed_correct_own_unit` and `distributed_handle_unit_state`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Own unit:** the client's own unit is corrected only when the host's echo of its prediction is more than 3 m from where it had it at that tick. It moves by the difference, and the ticks noted since move with it. The round trip is smoothed from the echoes. In co-op it is not moved into a BSP not yet loaded.
       - **Every player's unit:**
         - How the player died is noted. Dead, its copy is killed once the killing blow has had half a second to come.
         - Alive, the host's unit becomes the player's once this machine has it, and an old one is killed.
         - Its seat follows the host's: at once for others, and for a local player once it has disagreed for longer than a round trip and some slack.
         - Its shields and health, powerups and camouflage are the host's.
         - On foot, it moves toward the host's position (its own correction for a local player), onto the teleporter it lands on.
     - **The game's, through calls:**
       - whether a point has a leaf
       - whether the client has an object
       - attaching and detaching a player's unit
       - killing a unit, with statistics or without
       - setting a seat
       - setting the shields' state, which starts their effects
       - finding a teleporter

       The arguments add the netcode's death and seat records and the player. The regions add the player's datum, those records and every local player's noted positions.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 of the joiner's unit states agree, with their own corrections and applies nested in them. The steps before still agree on the host.
       - **Planted errors:**
         - Caught on the joiner: the remote players' tolerance, the seat rule, the round trip's smoothing and the echoed tick.
         - Unseen: the death wait (the host's killing blow arrives before its state says dead), detaching before attaching (a respawning player's old unit is already detached), and the noted positions' shift (no correction past 3 m came back).
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - Over two sessions each, with all networking systems in Rust, the machines' positions differ as with the C: medians 3.9 and 5.0 cm against 6.0 and 5.9.
         - With all fifty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] A client applying the host's object states and inventories in Rust (`halo_map::network_apply`, world steps 8 and 9): network_objects.c's handling of each object state (with `distributed_client_correct_own_vehicle`, `_dead_biped` and `_dead_vehicle`) and `distributed_client_apply_inventory` (with `_note_own_inventory` and `_own_settled`).
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Object states:**
         - A copy the host says is dead is killed: a vehicle at once; a biped no player's once its killing blow has had a while to come.
         - An item made carried that never was here is put in the world.
         - An object in the world is moved toward the host's position unless it is close enough and turned the same way. A vehicle uses a wider blend; an unsteered one follows every move.
         - The vehicle this machine's player drives is corrected against its own prediction come back, its noted ticks moved with it.
         - Its at-rest flag is the host's.
       - **Inventories:**
         - The unit's weapons match the host's slot for slot, once this machine has them.
         - The weapon in hand is the host's (a local player's own choice unless that is gone).
         - The rounds are held to the weapon tag's magazines. A local player's are the host's only once the host has had a round trip since it used the weapon. The weapon's age is the host's.
         - The grenades are the host's: a local player's when fewer, or once the host has seen the last it threw.
         - The local player's own record is noted afresh.
     - **The game's, through calls:** killing silently, the damage update, putting an item back in the world and showing it, dropping, releasing and adding a weapon.
       - The arguments add the noted vehicles, the dead bipeds' entry, the own inventories and the tag table (the weapon tag's magazines).
       - The regions add those, and every weapon the unit carries or is given.
       - New accessors give network_distributed.c's corrections count and round trip.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 8,192 of the joiner's object states and 256 inventories agree, with none left out.
       - **Planted errors:**
         - Caught: the angle tolerance, the at-rest flag, the loaded rounds and the own record's fired time.
         - Unseen: the vehicle blend (none came 2 to 2.5 m off), an item's reconnection (none placed after being made carried), the settle margin, and the grenade rule (no grenade counts changed).
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust, the machines' positions differ as with the C: median 4.9 cm against 6.3.
         - With all fifty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] A client applying the host's object changes in Rust (`halo_map::network_apply`, world step 10): network_objects.c's handling of each change, with `distributed_client_create`, `_change_valid`, `_apply_change`, `_delete` and `_own_object`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Validating a create:** the change must be a networked kind, its definition a tag of the group its type says, of a team the game has (a flag's or ball's by the game type), its colors numbers, its transform one that can be.
       - **Creating:** an object already at the index, the same one, is put where the host has it (unless carried, or this machine's own). Another there is deleted first. A create not at its index is undone and asked for again. A new unit's grenades wait for the host's inventories.
       - **Applying the look:** colors (those drawn held to 0 to 1), the shader's permutation, the regions' permutations the model has. Carried, it leaves the world; a body is killed with nothing to show of it.
       - **Deleting:** an item is let go of, a unit's host weapons dropped, its riders taken out, its player's binding undone, and any player's prompt on it cleared.
     - **The game's, through calls:**
       - making the object: the placement from the change, at the host's index
       - deleting at once
       - a failed create's retry
       - releasing an item
       - getting out of a seat
       - garbage
       - leaving the world

       The tags (object, its type's group, the weapon's flag bit, the model's regions) are read in Rust.
     - **The check:** a made object's datum isn't known when the step begins. So the regions are the objects' headers, the objects' memory pool as far as it is used (allocation only appends; 64 KB of room for one more), the pool itself, the players, the client's entry for the object and the statistics. A new accessor in objects.c gives the pool.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup (90 objects made, 46 deleted), every one of the joiner's object changes agrees, with none left out, alongside the unit states, object states and inventories.
       - Planted errors in a new unit's grenades, the creates' count and the client's entry were caught.
       - One in the regions' permutations went unseen: the host's are those object_new gives here.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust, the machines' positions differ as with the C (median 5.9 cm against 4.8), and the joiner ends with the host's count of items.
         - With all fifty-four systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] The host checking clients' hit reports in Rust (`halo_map::network_damage`, under `network_damage`, world step 11): network_damage.c's `distributed_report_valid` and its helpers, and `network_damage_handle_reports`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **What the player could deal**, found by walking the tags: their recent weapons (melee blows, their own detonations and overheating, their triggers' projectiles at their fastest rate), their unarmed blow, the vehicle they drove, a grenade the host saw them throw. Projectiles' impacts and detonations and their effects' parts are followed four deep, with how far from the firer (a projectile's flight by range, speed, timer and gravity).
       - **Checking the report:** it must be of that machine's player, at one of the host's objects (or destructible scenery), with numbers and indices the game can take, made lately. It must be of the shape the game gives it (at a point, a melee blow from the striker, a collision from the vehicle they drove), no harder than it can be, its origin at the target, a thrown grenade's explosion once, and paid for from the player's hits at the weapon's rate (an explosion's other objects that tick come free). It must come from where the host had the player (their history, their trail since it was fired) and hit the target about where the host had it when the shooter saw it.
       - **Making the damage:** the report becomes the damage the host deals: its flags, an explosion's direction and fall-off as the game gives them, the player's team, and the owner object theirs.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_damage_call`):** whether the machine has the player, its round trip, objects' origins, velocities, maximum vitality and nodes, a point's location, and dealing the damage (`object_cause_damage`, itself Rust's under `damage`). The game's tag walk keeps its cache; Rust walks the tags each time and finds what the cache holds.
     - **Records:** the host's damage records' types moved to network_damage_records.h, so the netcode probe has their layouts. The regions are the report's arguments (the damage made among them), its player's records and the counts.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with a hit every 3 seconds, every report agrees and is dealt (24), with none left out.
       - Planted errors in the damage's direction, its team and a trigger's rate (in the hits it costs) were caught.
       - Two went unseen: the hits' refill (a hit every 3 seconds leaves the bucket full) and the trail's interval slack (the shots were well within reach).
       - Not exercised: explosions, melee blows, vehicle collisions and rejected reports. The scripted hits are a weapon's at a point.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust, every report the joiner sends is dealt, as with the C, and the machines' positions differ as with the C (median 5.7 cm against 5.8).
         - With all fifty-five systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.

   - [x] The rest of the netcode's damage in Rust (`halo_map::network_damage`, under `network_damage`, world steps 12–16): the host's bookkeeping each tick, a client's replay of the host's events, and damage.c's three hooks.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **The host's bookkeeping** (step 12): each player's recent weapons (and their vehicle's, when they drive or gun it), their unarmed blow and when they last drove; where each player's unit and vehicle are each tick, and every few ticks their trail point (position, speed, the size of what they ride); and where each of the tick's damage events goes (a player effect to its player's machine; damage to a unit to its player's, its riders' and its owner's machines; anything else to all).
       - **A client's replay of one event** (step 13): the event's numbers checked and its damage made as this machine has it. Then the local player's screen effect; a unit's flinch and the rest of its damage, but not its death; a biped's killing blow whole, with the host's killer, their score, friendly fire, by vehicle and a telefrag; or destructible scenery's damage.
       - **Whether this machine deals damage** (`network_damage_deals`, step 14): a client deals none unless it carries out the host's word. It shows a weapon's own shake of its own player at once, and reports its own player's hits on the host's objects. The host deals all but a client's player's damage (which their machine reports), unless it is dealing that report or the machine has left. Where damage isn't dealt, what it would show is filled in: the material struck (or the shield's) and how much of it is left, through what the object rides.
       - **The host's events** (steps 15 and 16): a client's player's screen effect (not a weapon's own shake), and an object damaged (destructible scenery's; a unit's, with a killing blow's killer as the host noted it, kept within the last 64 events of the tick).
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_damage_call`, 11 new):** a unit's unarmed blow, a player's machine, whether the client has an object, the replays (player effect, aftermath, kill, static), setting and getting a death, the telefrag notice and a player's score. A kill's replay sets the C's replaying statics around the call.
     - **The check:**
       - One arguments struct (`HookArguments`) serves the five steps: the event or the caller's damage and indices, the hook's numbers, the connection, and the addresses of network_damage.c's records.
       - The regions are the records each step writes: the players' records, history, trails and destinations; the tick's events and their count; the caller's damage and a client's reports; and the count replayed.
       - A replayed kill changes the biped and its player before Rust reads them, so the replay's regions also hold the objects, their memory pool and the players, as the object changes' do.
       - The wire's damage made into the game's is shared with the report check, now from any address.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with shooting, kills and a vehicle, every step agrees with none left out: 2,048 bookkeeping ticks (13,057 calls), 32 player effects, 16 aftermaths, 64 of the host's and 64 of the joiner's deals, and 32 replays (three of them the joiner's deaths).
       - On b30 and a50 the single-player hooks agree too (512 deals, 128 aftermaths).
       - Planted errors were caught in a weapon's unarmed time, a report's host time, a player effect's total, an aftermath's body damage, a replayed death's friendly fire and a shield's material scale.
       - The bookkeeping first differed in write order only: C zeroes a destination's count before it asks for the owner's machine, and so does Rust now.
       - Not exercised: destructible scenery, telefrags, events past the tick's limit and a machine that has left.
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust, the joiner's three deaths are replayed (31 events) and every report is dealt, as with the C. The machines' positions differ as with the C (median 4.3 cm against 4.0).
         - With all fifty-five systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second.
     - **The crash log** now names the module and offset of the faulting address and of the code addresses on the stack, so a crash in halo_sim.dll can be found in its symbols.

   - [x] The host sending its objects each tick in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, system `network_host`, world step 17): network_objects.c's `network_objects_host_tick`, with `distributed_host_update_objects`, `_find_viewers`, `_object_period`, `_send_states`, `_send_inventories` and `_send_damage_animations`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Objects made and deleted:** each networked object the host hasn't told its clients of goes to all of them reliably (what it is, where, how it looks, carried or dead). Each it told of that is gone, or has another in its place, is deleted.
       - **Viewers:** each client machine's players' living units, where they are and the vehicles they drive, and where every player's unit is (for a machine with none).
       - **States:** a moving object goes to each client as often as it is near the client's players (every tick within 25 world units, then every second, third or fourth tick, in the object's turn). The vehicle a client drives goes every third tick with which of the client's ticks the host has it at. One come to rest goes to every client three times, eight ticks apart, and four at rest go each tick, round them all.
       - **Inventories** (every third tick): a change of weapons or grenades, or none sent for a second, goes to every client. A change of ammunition alone goes to the unit's own client at once and to the others as often as its place goes to them.
       - **Damage animations:** the host's flinch and death picks, each in three ticks' messages.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_host_call`):** sending the message (to a machine, or to every client reliably or not) and an object's origin.
       - Every message goes out of one buffer (`objects_host_message`), so the check sees each message's bytes as they are sent.
       - What the step reads of the netcode is taken as the tick begins and passed in its arguments: the client machines and their players, and a co-op cutscene's camera. The C reads the same arguments.
       - The host's records moved to network_objects_records.h, so the netcode probe has their layouts.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments, the message, the told objects and their count, the resting cursor, the moving flags and rest times, the inventories' records, the viewers, the statistics and the damage animations.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 of the host's ticks agree, with none left out (7,795 calls).
       - Planted errors were caught in a change's team, a rest state's spacing and window, the predicted tick of a client's own vehicle, the weapons' checksum and a damage animation's sends.
       - One went unseen: the middle distance's bound (no moving object came 48 to 60 world units from the nearest player).
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ as with the C (median 5.8 cm against 5.8, largest 19.1 against 19.2). Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.
         - With all fifty-six systems in Rust, every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 at 144 frames a second. The exception is one of d20's 31,000 biped updates: the game's record of it has the biped's position as relative to the seat it was just put in.

   - [x] The host taking its clients' word on the vehicles they drive in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world step 18): network_objects.c's `network_objects_apply_vehicle_predictions`, with `network_objects_note_vehicle_ground` and `network_objects_vehicle_below_ceiling`.
     - **What Rust does:** each tick, for each client's player driving a vehicle, the latest place the client says it has the vehicle is taken when all of these hold:
       - The player still drives it, and a co-op client has the host's BSP.
       - It is within 5 world units of the host's copy.
       - It is no higher in the air than a thing thrown up as fast as the vehicle left the ground would be by now (the client's round trip and jitter allowed). Boats, planes, Banshees and turrets are exempt.
       - It is no further from the last one taken than the vehicle goes in the client's ticks since, at twice its tag's top speed or as fast as the host's own ticks sent it lately (less what it took of the client beyond that top speed). The client's clock counts no more than the host's.
       - **Taking it:** the velocities are held to those speeds, and the host's copy is reconciled to the client's word. A copy at rest that the client moved, or says moves, wakes. Which of the client's ticks the host has it at is noted, for the states the host sends back.
     - **The game's, through calls:** whether a machine has the player, whether a co-op player has the BSP, a machine's round trip, the vehicle tag's type and top speeds, and the reconcile (itself Rust's under `network_apply`). The gravity is passed in the arguments. The prediction being taken goes in the arguments for the reconcile to read.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments, the host's predictions records and each predicted vehicle's datum.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a driven vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 ticks agree, with none left out (1,500 calls).
       - Planted errors were caught in the client tick noted, the anchor's span, the rise speed off the ground, the velocity given to the reconcile and the wake from rest.
       - Two went unseen: the thrown-speed allowance (the vehicle never went faster than twice its top speed) and the accept tolerance cut to 1 world unit (the client's word was always that close).
       - **Rust in charge:**
         - With all networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ as with the C (median 6.1 cm against 5.8, largest 18.3 against 19.2). Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.
         - Every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 as before.

   - [x] The host's plan of what each client is sent of each player in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world step 19): network_distributed.c's `distributed_host_plan_players`.
     - **What Rust does:** each tick, for every player:
       - their unit state and input are sampled, and whether their life, unit, ride or seat changed since last sent is noted;
       - for each client, the player goes:
         - every tick when near the client's players (25 world units), then every second, third or fourth tick by distance;
         - every sixth tick when out of their sight (the map's potentially visible set) or dead;
         - at once when they come into sight or change;
         - at least every second tick when a client's player aims near them, every tick through a scope.
       - A client's own players' units go every tick and their input never. A player who has left goes only on a change. A client whose players are dead watches from where they last were alive.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_plan_call`):** the unit state's sampling (`distributed_state_from_player`, Rust's under `distributed_wire`), an origin, the relayed input's (`distributed_relayed_action_from`, Rust's under `distributed_wire`) and whether one cluster sees another. The update the tick ran, how many players' input it has, the map's cluster count and the client machines and their players are in the arguments. The host's records' types moved to network_distributed_records.h.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments, the sends, the sight records, the viewers, the units last sent, the states, the actions, the players' machines and the update number.
     - **Results:**
       - In 90-second sessions on Blood Gulch (scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup) and Prisoner (a small indoor map with shooting and kills), every plan agrees, with none left out (12,913 calls on Prisoner's 2,048 ticks).
       - Planted errors were caught in the send on coming into sight, the input's send on a change and, on Prisoner, where the host's player died, the hidden period.
       - Three went unseen:
         - the middle distance's bound and the aim's cosine (two players stay close or far, never between, and the aim is consulted only past 25 world units);
         - the seat in the change test (a seat changes with the vehicle).
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ as with the C. Over three sessions each the medians were 5.2 to 7.4 cm for the Rust and 3.7 to 5.8 cm for the C, and the largest 17.2 to 20.0 cm against 9.7 to 19.2 cm. Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt. Every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 as before.

   - [x] The host's sending of what it planned in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world step 20): network_distributed.c's `distributed_host_send_players`.
     - **What Rust does:** to each client, the relayed input of each player planned for it (after the tick's update number), then their unit states, its own players' with the tick it predicted them at. Each entry is written by the wire format's writers (`distributed_wire`).
     - **The game's, through calls:** the host's packer (`distributed_packer_begin`, `_add`, `_flush`), which fills each message to the batch's room. The entry being packed is in the step's arguments, so the check compares its bytes.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 sends agree, with none left out (13,048 calls), alongside steps 17 to 19.
       - Planted errors in the own-player flag, the input's send bit and the update number's prefix were all caught.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ by a median of 3.6 cm (largest 10.2), within the C's range. Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] A client's tick of the host's objects in Rust (`halo_map::network_apply`, world step 21): network_objects.c's `network_objects_client_tick`, with `distributed_client_remove_own_objects`, `_ready_picked_up_weapons`, `_note_own_inventories`, `_send_vehicles` and `_carry_unsteered_vehicles`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Asking for the objects:** until the host has told it all its objects, the client asks for them, less often the longer the host takes, saying when one of them failed since it last asked.
       - **Removing its own:** once told all, it deletes the objects of the host's kinds that the host never told of (what its own simulation made: a dying unit's grenades). These are the ones made since the last tick, or all of them when it can't say which.
       - **Picked-up weapons:** a weapon its player picked up (the host said so) is readied once the unit has it, unless the trigger is held.
       - **Own inventories:** what its players carry is noted, and when they threw a grenade or fired or reloaded a weapon.
       - **Its vehicles:** the vehicles its players drive go to the host with this tick, and where they were is noted.
       - **Unsteered vehicles:** in co-op, each vehicle no player drives keeps moving at the velocity the host last sent, turned as physics turns it.
       - **Identity:** who it is goes to the host once it has asked.
     - **The game's, through the applying's calls:** sending the client's message (one buffer the check sees), setting a desired weapon, sending its identity, and the deletes and moves as before. The local players are taken as the tick begins.
       - The client's scalars are one struct (`objects_client_state`), and its records' types are in network_objects_records.h.
       - The own-inventory note gained its comparing form.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments, the client's records (its state, new objects, the host's objects it has, picked-up weapons, own vehicles and inventories), the statistics, the message, and the objects, players and object pool (deletes).
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 of the joiner's ticks agree, with none left out.
       - Planted errors were caught in the grenade comparison, the vehicle's tick and, in a longer session where the client removed two of its own objects, the removal count.
       - Two went unseen: the asking's back-off (the host answered the first asking) and the reload test (a reload changes the rounds, which are compared too). The unsteered vehicles move only in co-op.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust on both machines, the machines' positions differ by a median of 3.7 to 4.4 cm (largest 9.7 to 10.0) over two sessions, within the C's range. Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] The host's answer to a client asking for its objects in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world step 22): network_objects.c's `network_objects_client_asked`.
     - **What Rust does:** a client that has loaded gets every object the host has, players' units first, then vehicles, other units and the rest, and then word that that is all. Every unit's inventory goes again with the next inventories. Asked again, it gets only the word (the objects are on their way), unless it failed to make one, at most once in ten seconds, or another machine has taken its place.
     - **The game's, through the host's calls:** sending to the machine reliably (the send call gained it). The machine's players are taken as the step begins. The step's arguments are the host tick's with the machine asking added, and the machines told are in network_objects_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - Every answer agrees: a client joining at the start, and one joining 40 seconds into the game.
       - Planted errors in the order (vehicles last), the inventories' reset (with the late joiner, whose units carry something) and the word's reliability were caught.
       - The asking again never came up.
       - **Rust in charge:** with a late joiner and all networking systems in Rust, both machines end with the same items, made and deleted as with the C (146 and 101 on the host). The joiner's seconds fall a few ticks off the host's (2 in the C's session, 3 in the Rust's), so their positions compare only roughly: median 24.7 cm against the C's 18.5.

   - [x] The netcode's messages handled in Rust (`halo_map::network_messages`, system `network_messages`, world step 23): network_distributed.c's `network_distributed_handle_message`, the front door every message of the distributed netcode comes through.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - Voice chat's messages go to voice chat (in the lobby too), and none are taken between games.
       - **A batch** (a tick's unreliable messages to one machine, in one datagram) is taken apart, and each message is handled as if it came alone: no batch in a batch, none longer than a datagram.
       - **Each message** must be of a kind there is, with the bytes its count of entries needs (and one entry for the kinds whose handlers read one). It must come the way its kind goes: from a client to the host, or from the host to a client, which notes the host's latest tick. A message of a kind sent every tick that is older than the sender's latest of that kind is dropped (a killing blow sent again reliably is not, unless it came in a batch).
       - The host notes a client's clock by its messages' ticks, and takes no prediction from a client whose game runs fast.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_message_call`):** voice chat, the client's clock (its speed-hack check: notices, logs, kicks) and the handlers, which are mostly Rust's own steps.
       - The kinds' entry sizes, which kinds a client sends, which need an entry and which are dropped when old are tables in the step's arguments, from the game's own switches (now functions).
       - A batch's message is taken apart into a buffer in the arguments, so the check compares it.
       - A message handled while one is (a handler's) is handled as the game's, inside it.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments (with the batch's buffer), the host's latest tick, the received count, the batch flag and the senders' latest ticks.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, every message agrees on both machines, with none left out (13,248 calls on the host, 12,864 on the joiner).
       - Planted errors were caught in the batch flag, the host's latest tick, the entries' size check and the received count.
       - One went unseen: dropping a message as old as the sender's latest (each kind went once a tick to each machine).
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust on both machines (fifty-seven systems in all), the machines' positions differ by a median of 5.6 and 7.1 cm over two sessions (largest 18.4 and 22.4), within the C's range. Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt. Every probe check agrees on the five levels and a50 as before.

   - [x] The host taking its clients' word on their players on foot in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world step 24): network_distributed.c's `distributed_apply_predictions`, with `distributed_on_foot_bound`, `_on_foot_fall_speed`, `_on_foot_ceiling`, `_on_foot_move_valid` and `_take_prediction`.
     - **What Rust does:** each tick, for each client's player on foot, the latest place the client says it has them is taken when all of these hold:
       - It is within 3.5 world units of the host's copy.
       - It is no further from the last one taken than the player goes in the client's ticks since. Across and up that is twice as fast as they run and jump (or as fast as the host's own ticks sent the copy lately, an explosion's throw); down, as fast as a fall from the highest point since they were on the ground.
       - It is no higher above where the host last had them on the ground than a jump (or a throw) takes them, and in the air falling since.
       - **Taking it:** the host's copy is moved there (closed by half when a little off), its velocity held to those speeds, and the anchor noted once a second. A teleport, a new life or a ride's end starts it afresh from where the host has the player.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_predictions_call`):** a player's machine's round trip, a co-op player's BSP, the state's apply (`distributed_apply_state`, Rust's under `network_apply`), and the tags' speeds (the game globals' run speeds and double speed, the biped's jump and heights). The gravity is in the arguments, and the records' types moved to network_distributed_records.h.
     - **A lying client for the tests:** `HALO_NETWORK_TEST_LIE=<seconds>` has a client send a false place every so many seconds for three ticks: a step aside, up, down, or a dash. That puts the host's refusals in the check.
     - **The check:** the regions are the arguments, the predictions, the anchors, the host's noted speeds and each predicted unit's datum.
     - **Results:**
       - In 90-second sessions with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, with an honest client and with one lying every 4 seconds, all 2,048 ticks agree, with none left out.
       - Planted errors were caught in the run speed and the anchor's span (honest client), and in the fall check and the accept tolerance (the lying client).
       - Two went unseen: the rise tolerance (a lie 2.5 world units up is refused either way) and the thrown-speed allowance (nobody was thrown).
       - **Rust in charge:** with the lying client and all networking systems in Rust, the host corrects the client 30 times against the C's 28, and the machines' positions differ by a median of 5.7 cm against 6.1 (largest 19.9 against 18.9). Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] The netcode's input handled in Rust (`halo_map::network_input`, system `network_input`, world steps 25 and 26): network_distributed.c's `distributed_handle_inputs` and `distributed_handle_actions`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **The host** takes a client's players' input (each tick's, with the buttons of the ticks before it): only that machine's players', the sticks and the trigger held to a controller's. In co-op the input counts in the scripts' action tests as a local player's does, its turn the short way round.
       - **The round trip:** once a message the host measures how long a message takes the client and back, by the latest host tick the client had heard of. It is smoothed as TCP smooths its own: an eighth for the average, a quarter for the jitter. Every correction and prediction limit reads it.
       - **A client** takes the host's relayed input of the players it drives: the update, then each player's buttons, facing, sticks, trigger and choices, unpacked from the wire.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_input_call`):** handing the input on to the update server or client, the action tests and the co-op BSP note. The action handed on is in the step's arguments, and the facings' and round trips' types are in network_distributed_records.h.
     - **Their own system:** the handlers run inside the message step (23), where a nested step isn't checked, so they have a system of their own to check apart.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 of the host's inputs and 1,024 of the joiner's relayed actions agree, with none left out.
       - All six planted errors were caught: the throttle's clamp, the round trip's average and jitter, and the relayed throttle, trigger and pitch.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust (fifty-eight systems in all), the machines' positions differ by a median of 4.7 and 3.8 cm over two sessions (largest 18.0 and 10.3), within the C's range. Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] The host's periodic sends in Rust (`halo_map::network_host`, world steps 27 and 28): network_distributed.c's `distributed_send_statistics`, `_send_pings` and `_send_pickups`, as its tick runs them.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Statistics:** every half second (or at once after a kill), each player's statistics that changed since they were last sent (by their checksum). On each second's round, 16 players' go whatever they are, from a cursor round them all, so one lost message is made good.
       - **Pings:** every two seconds, every player's ping, for the scoreboard.
       - **Pickups:** what players on other machines picked up this tick, to each player's machine reliably, once with all of its players'.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_sends_call`):** sending (one buffer the check sees) and a player's ping.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a pickup, all 2,048 ticks of statistics and pings agree, with none left out.
       - All four planted errors were caught: the refreshed players, the cursor, a ping's value and the interval.
       - The pickups agree, but none was made in these sessions: the test's pickup moves the joiner onto a weapon, but it isn't taken in this build.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust, both machines show the same scores. The positions differ by a median of 6.3 cm (largest 21.0), within the C's range, both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] A client's sends each tick in Rust (`halo_map::network_input`, world step 29): network_distributed.c's `distributed_note_own_positions`, `distributed_client_send_inputs` and `distributed_client_send_predictions`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Its own players' positions:** where each local player's unit is this tick is noted, for the host's word on it to be measured against later (not when riding, where it is its seat's).
       - **Input:** its players' input of the tick just run, with the buttons of the ticks before it, the structure BSP it has loaded and the latest host tick it has heard of, to the host.
       - **Predictions:** its players' units on foot, where it has them, to the host. The tests' lie (`HALO_NETWORK_TEST_LIE`) is applied here, its period passed in the arguments.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_client_sends_call`):** the local input (the update client's), the unit state's sampling (Rust's under `distributed_wire`), sending and the packer. The local players, the BSP and the host's latest tick are taken as the tick begins.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second session with scripted play, shooting, kills, a vehicle and a client lying every 4 seconds, all 2,048 of the joiner's ticks agree, with none left out.
       - All four planted errors were caught: the noted positions' slot, the input's host tick, its buttons and the lie's turn.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust, the machines' positions differ by a median of 3.8 cm with an honest client and 6.2 cm with a lying one, within the C's range (the host corrects the liar 28 times, as with the C). Both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] The host's speed check of its clients' clocks in Rust (`halo_map::network_messages`, system `network_clock`, world step 30): network_distributed.c's `distributed_note_client_clock`.
     - **What Rust does:** each client's messages are stamped with its tick, and the host measures them against its own time in two-second windows.
       - A client whose game runs more than a tenth fast, and half a second ahead, has its players' predictions refused.
       - After five such windows it is dropped as a speed hacker, but only when its stream (which no other machine can send as it) says so too.
       - On its datagrams' word alone, which another machine could forge from its address to have it banned, it stays, refused, and is logged once as unverified.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_clock_call`):** the logs, the notice to every client, the cheaters' record and the kick. The time (milliseconds) and whether the message came over the stream are in the arguments, and the clocks' type is in network_distributed_records.h. It runs inside the message step, so it has a system of its own.
     - **A fast client for the tests:** `HALO_NETWORK_TEST_FAST=<seconds>` has a client stamp its messages from then on as if its game ran a quarter fast. The host must flag it, then drop it.
       - **Note:** a drop writes the host's bans.txt and cheaters.txt in the data folder, and the test's ban keeps the joiner out of later sessions until it is deleted.
     - **Results:**
       - In a 90-second honest session all 4,096 clock notes agree with no call. With the fast client they all agree as it is flagged (1.25 times as fast) and dropped ten seconds later.
       - All four planted errors were caught with the fast client: the fast rate, the windows to a drop, the window's length and the stream's word.
       - **Rust in charge:** with all networking systems in Rust (fifty-nine systems in all), the fast client is flagged and kicked as with the C. In an honest session the machines' positions differ by a median of 5.3 cm (largest 25.9), both machines end with the same count of items, and every hit reported is dealt.

   - [x] Co-op's host sending the world's changes in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, new system `network_coop`, world step 31): network_coop.c's `host_send_object_transforms`, `host_send_device_states` and `host_send_object_looks`, the first of co-op.
     - **The test harness:** a co-op session on one machine is the network test's `coop` variant on a campaign level: `HALO_NETWORK_TEST=host:levels\a30\a30:coop` (built with printf in bash, where `\\$map` in double quotes escapes the `$`).
     - **What Rust does:** each tick the host sends every client what changed of the world its scripts and machines move.
       - **Scenery and machines** that moved since last sent (by a hundredth of a world unit, or turned): where they are and how they face.
       - **Devices** whose position or power changed: doors, elevators, a light bridge.
       - **Objects** whose look changed: their regions' permutations (a broken part) or their scale.
       - An object first seen is where and how the map placed it on every machine, so nothing goes for it. With the resent state (a machine joined, or every two seconds), everything that ever changed goes again.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_coop_world_call`):** sending (one buffer the check sees). The records' and entries' types moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100 to 240-second co-op sessions on a10, a30, b30, c40 and d40, every tick agrees, with none left out.
       - The devices were moved by a console command (`HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND="(begin (device_set_position cryo_door_1 1) (device_set_power cryo_door_2 0))"`), since the bots leave none moving in that time.
       - Planted errors were caught in the looks' resend (a30), a transform's up vector (a10, d40), and a device's moving flag and its power (a10, with the command).
       - Two went unseen: the transforms' move and turn thresholds (a10's scenery jumps; none creep).
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a10 and a30, with all networking systems in Rust (sixty systems in all), both machines end with the host's count of items. The positions on a30 differ by a median of 11.3 cm, between two C sessions' 8.0 and 15.9 cm.

   - [x] Co-op's host sending its device groups in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 32): network_coop.c's `host_send_device_groups`, with `device_group_entries` and `device_group_state`.
     - **What Rust does:** the device groups are the values the scripts set that doors, elevators and lights aim at: the scenario's groups, and each device's own. Each tick the host sends every client:
       - each group that changed, three times;
       - the next eight in a rotation through every group;
       - the next eight in a quicker rotation through those moved since the map loaded, which is what a late joiner lacks (and on a join, all of those).
       - A group's value, the flags a client takes and its count of immediate sets go with it. Clients loaded the same map, so the starting state isn't sent.
     - **The game's, through calls:** sending the groups' message. The groups themselves are read from the game's device-groups data array. Its flags' mask and runtime bit come from a new accessor in devices.c (`device_group_network_masks`), and the records' types moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a10 (doors moved by a console command) and a30, all 2,048 ticks agree, with none left out (a message each tick).
       - All four planted errors were caught: the rotation's step, a change's sends, the flags' mask and the moved rotation.
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, with all networking systems in Rust, the positions differ by a median of 7.8 cm (largest 19.1), within the C's 8.0 to 15.9, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] Co-op's host event queue and resends in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 33): network_coop.c's `host_send_attachments`, `host_send_looping_sounds`, `host_send_hud_state`, `host_send_broken_surfaces` and `host_send_events`, with `event_new` and `send_attach`.
     - **What Rust does:** the host queues each thing that happens once (a sound, a title, a HUD change, a nav point, an attach, a broken surface) and sends the queue each tick, each event in three ticks' messages. With the resent state (a machine joined, or every two seconds), it queues again what a late joiner would lack:
       - what the scripts attached, forgetting a child that is gone or has a new parent;
       - the looping sounds they started (music, ambience), and a stop for one that ended without the scripts stopping it;
       - the nav points active, and the objective to a machine that joined;
       - the next eight broken surfaces, round them all.
     - **The game's, through calls:** sending the events, whether a looping sound still plays, whether a surface is broken (and its centroid), and the full queue's log. The event, the queue and the host's records moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Test hooks:**
       - `HALO_SIM_BREAK_SURFACES=<n>` breaks the structure's first n breakable surfaces when the console command runs (a30 has 3; a10 and b30 have none), since no script breaks them.
       - A nav point comes from the command `(activate_team_nav_point_object default player (list_get (players) 0) 0.5)`.
       - `HOST_ONLY_COMMAND=1` in the test script keeps the command off the joiner.
     - **A crash found and fixed (hud_nav_points.c):** with a nav point active, a co-op client halted on custom_render_nav_point's assertion as its player spawned. How a nav point draws is worked out in the tick's HUD update, only for a player with a unit then, and a client's unit comes from the host between ticks. Now a nav point not yet worked out waits a tick to draw.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30, all 2,048 ticks agree, with none left out, with and without the hooks.
       - All nine planted errors were caught. a30's own scripts catch the looping sounds' start and stop, an attach, the objective's resend, the events' sends and their numbers. The hooks catch a nav point's offset, a broken surface's BSP and the surfaces' rotation.
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, with all networking systems in Rust and surfaces broken, the positions differ by a median of 12.4 to 13.3 cm, within the C's 8.0 to 15.9, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] Co-op's host sending its object names and screen effect in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world steps 34 and 35): network_coop.c's `host_send_object_names` and `host_send_screen_effect`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **Object names:** every half second (and on a join), one bit for each scenario object name the host has an object of, with its structure BSP. A client deletes the named objects the host lacks.
       - **Screen effect:** the scripts' full-screen effects (blur, warp, light enhancement, desaturation, video), when they change and with the resent state.
     - **The game's:** the names' objects are read from objects.c's name list, through a new accessor (`object_name_list_port_get`). The rasterizer's screen effect is read before the step, and sending goes through calls. The messages moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30, every tick of both steps agrees, with none left out.

       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, with all networking systems in Rust, the positions differ by a median of 12.3 cm, within the C's 8.0 to 15.9, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] Co-op's host skip vote and presentation in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 36): network_coop.c's `host_count_skip_votes` and `host_presentation`, and sending the presentation.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **The skip vote:** a cutscene is offered for skipping once the script's save at its start is written, and not during the cooldown after a skip. The host counts itself (with a local player) and each client heard showing the cutscene within the last second; a vote counts for a second. It skips when more than half have voted.

     - **The game's:** what the vote and the presentation read of the game is gathered before the step (`host_presentation_inputs`, struct `coop_presentation_inputs`). Skipping (`main_skip_cinematic`), its logs and sending go through calls. The presentation, its message and the skip vote moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 and b30, every tick agrees, with none left out. The test bot's A presses are skip votes during the opening cutscenes.
       - Seven of eight planted errors were caught: the vote's hold time and majority (a30, a10), the fade color, the active cluster, the field of view, the HUD flag (a30), and the camera object's offset (b30's Pelican flies by the camera).
       - One went unseen: waiting for the save before offering the skip (no save happened during a skippable cutscene).

   - [x] Co-op's host resend and players' vitality in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 37): network_coop.c's `host_resend_update` and `players_vitality_keep` (the host's side).
     - **What Rust does:** each tick, whether a client machine appeared since the last (a join, which resends everything a late joiner lacks), and whether to resend this tick (a join, or every two seconds). Then, once the scripts have set the players' maximum vitality, every player's unit not at it is set to it (a respawned one).
     - **The game's:** the client machines and each player's unit with its maximum vitality are gathered before the step (struct `coop_resend_inputs`). Setting a unit's vitality changes the unit, outside the step's records, so the check's replay would otherwise see it already set. Setting the vitality (`object_initialize_vitality`) and the join's log go through calls.
