       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, the positions differ by a median of 11.5 cm, within the C's 8.0 to 15.9, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] Co-op's extra enemies' kept riders in Rust (`halo_map::coop_enemies`, world step 38): coop_enemies.c's `coop_enemies_update`, with `coop_enemies_rider_place`, `coop_enemies_riders_getting_out` and `coop_enemies_forget_vehicle`.
     - **What Rust does:** Server Setup's EXTRA ENEMIES can make a squad a script loads into a dropship larger than its seats; the riders without a seat are kept. Each tick, for each dropship with riders kept:
       - a dropship gone or destroyed forgets its riders, and one with none left is done with;
       - once one of its seated riders is out of it and alive, its kept riders are placed one every four ticks: beside a rider who got out (a ring further about them each round), else at a seat's marker, else the dropship's place; on free ground about it if there is some, else on a ring about it.
     - **The game's, through calls (`coop_enemies_world_call`):** finding free ground (collision tests against the level and its objects, `coop_enemies_spread_position`), a seat's marker, and placing the actor (`actor_place`, `encounter_update_status`). The state moved to coop_enemies_records.h.
     - **The test harness:** no level loads enemies into a dropship within a few minutes, so console commands do. `halo_map names <map>` (new) lists a scenario's object names and encounters with their squads. On a30: `HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND="(begin (object_create lz_cship) (ai_place first_wave) (vehicle_load_magic lz_cship \"\" (ai_actors first_wave)))"` at tick 300, and a new second command, `HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND_LATER="(vehicle_unload lz_cship \"\")"` (at tick 600, or `HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND_LATER_TIME`), with `HALO_NET_COOP_ENEMIES_MODE=multiplier HALO_NET_COOP_ENEMIES_MULTIPLIER=4`.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 with that setup, every tick agrees, with none left out (171 calls: the kept riders placed).
       - Without it, 240-second sessions on a30 and b30 agree too, with no riders kept.
       - All seven planted errors were caught: the release interval, the facing, the ring's spacing, the spread's number, the dead riders, the released count and the rider chosen to stand beside.
       - **Rust in charge:** with that setup, the positions differ by a median of 12.8 cm (16.8 for the C in the same setup) and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] A co-op client applying its host's world changes in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 39): network_coop.c's `network_coop_handle_device_states`, `network_coop_handle_object_transforms` and `network_coop_handle_object_looks`, with `object_find`. The first of the client's side.
     - **What Rust does:** for each entry of the host's message, the client's copy of the object (by its name, else its index, matching the salt or only the absolute index, as long as the tag matches and an unnamed object has no name here), then:
       - **a device** set to the host's power, and moved to its position when off it (still and off it at all, or farther than 3 hundredths);
       - **a scenery or machine** without a parent placed where the host's is, its axes made a rotation;
       - **an object's look:** each region's permutation the model has (or none), and its scale when it differs (between 0 and 100).
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_coop_client_call`):** setting a device's state, placing an object, setting a permutation, scaling. The model's regions are read through the loaded tags. Since the calls change the objects the step reads (a device's position, a scale), the objects' headers and memory pool are the step's records, as for the network objects' changes.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a10 (doors moved on the host only, `HOST_ONLY_COMMAND=1`), a30 and d40, every message agrees on the joining machine, with none left out.
       - Three of six planted errors were caught: the device tolerance, the snap's moving test and the axes made valid. The three unseen check against a hostile or mismatched host (a permutation past the model's, a salt that differs, a named object found by index).
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, the positions differ by a median of 12.4 cm, within the C's 8.0 to 15.9, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] A co-op client applying its host's device groups in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 40): network_coop.c's `network_coop_handle_device_groups`, with `device_group_find`.
     - **What Rust does:** for each of the host's groups, the client's own: a scenario's by its index (one not made at run time), a device's own by the device (`object_find`) and its role. The group is set to the host's value (held to 0 to 1) and flags, jumping there when the host did (its count of immediate sets differs from the last seen) or when a rotation's entry disagrees (a change missed, a late join); a change's entry animates, as on the host.
     - **The game's, through calls:** setting a group (`device_group_network_set`). The snap counts seen moved to network_coop_records.h; the game's device groups are the step's records, since setting one changes what the next entries read.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a10 (doors moved on the host, a 40-second late join) and a30, every message agrees on the joining machine.
       - Three of six planted errors were caught: the changed entry's test, the device's role and the groups seen. Three went unseen: the snap count (an immediate set reaches the client as a changed entry anyway), the flags' comparison (no script changes them) and a scenario group's runtime test (a hostile host's).

   - [x] A co-op client matching its host's named objects in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 41): network_coop.c's `network_coop_handle_object_names`, with `object_holds_player`.
     - **What Rust does:** for each scenario object name of a type the object netcode doesn't sync (scenery, devices), whether the host has its object and the client doesn't, or the other way. A difference seen twice in a row on the same BSP creates the object, or deletes the client's (not one holding a player). A message of another BSP or count of names forgets the differences.
     - **The game's, through calls:** creating by name (`object_new_by_name`) and deleting (`object_delete`). The names' objects, the objects' headers and memory pool are the step's records.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 with the host alone destroying and later recreating `intro_tree` (`HOST_ONLY_COMMAND=1`), every message agrees on the joining machine.
       - Two of four planted errors were caught: the second sighting and the networked types. Two went unseen: forgetting a difference that resolved itself, and keeping an object that holds a player.
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30 with those commands, the positions differ by a median of 12.0 to 12.9 cm (the C's 12.9), and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] A co-op client applying its host's events in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 42): network_coop.c's `network_coop_handle_events` and `client_apply_event`, with the sound, HUD, player effect, nav point, animation, effect, attach and script sounds' appliers.
     - **What Rust does:** each event once and in order (its number past the last applied, wrapping), none with a real that isn't a number, then by its kind, checking it against what this machine has:
       - **sounds:** a dialogue sound (remembered, sixteen at a time, for a skipped cutscene to stop), a looping one started (unless already playing) or stopped, from the object if the client has it;
       - **a chapter title**, the **HUD** (help text, objective, flashing, messages cleared, checkpoint), the scripts' **camera shake** (held to what an observer accepts), a **nav point** (its arrow, flag, team or unit checked);
       - **a unit's animation** (an animation graph's animation, the unit the client's), **a scenery's** (found as the host named it), **an effect** at a cutscene flag or on an object's model marker;
       - **an attach or detach** (not one already made, nor onto its own child), the **revert's** stopping of the dialogue, a **broken surface** of this BSP, a **unit opened** or closed.
     - **The game's, through calls (`network_coop_client_call`):** each engine function the event ends in, by numbers (a marker's or animation's index, the game finding its name). Since starting a looping sound changes its tag, whether each entry's was playing is gathered before the step, and Rust follows its own starts and stops within it; the objects (an attach) are the step's records. The script sounds moved to network_coop_records.h; network_objects.c gained an accessor of the objects a client has.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 (with a nav point on the host's player and broken surfaces, host only), a10 and b30, every message agrees on the joining machine, with none left out.
       - Four of eight planted errors were caught: the numbers' comparison, a sound's scale, a nav point's offset and a broken surface's BSP. Four went unseen: an objective past the HUD's messages (a hostile host's), a resent attach (a30's reach the client once), the dialogue ring's size (fewer than eight sounds) and a looping sound started twice in one message.
       - **Rust in charge:** in co-op on a30, the positions differ by a median of 8.5 to 12.4 cm (the C's 8.3 to 12.5), and both machines end with the host's count of items. Two of about ten such sessions began late on the joining machine (71 seconds in common rather than 91) with medians of 21 to 22 cm; no C session has yet, and the cause isn't known.

   - [x] A co-op client's presentation messages in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 43): network_coop.c's `network_coop_handle_presentation` and `network_coop_handle_screen_effect`.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **The host's presentation of a tick** is kept to be played in its turn: when heard, unless already played past, in its slot of eight by the host's tick, with the newest tick heard.
       - **The scripts' screen effect** as this machine can draw it: none with a real that isn't a number; its convolution type, extra passes and overbright mode held to what the rasterizer has; video with no convolution; split screen without the blur.
     - **The game's, through calls:** setting the screen effect, from the state the step left. The presentations kept moved to network_coop_records.h.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 with a screen effect started on the host (`(begin (cinematic_screen_effect_start true) (cinematic_screen_effect_set_convolution 1 2 0 4 2))`, host only), every message agrees on the joining machine, with none left out.
       - Three of six planted errors were caught: the slot, and the convolution type's and extra passes' limits. Three went unseen: a presentation already played past and the newest tick's test (loopback delivers in order), and one with a higher pass limit (the script's passes are within it).

   - [x] A co-op client's tick in Rust (`halo_map::network_coop`, world step 44): network_coop.c's `network_coop_client_tick`, `client_play_presentation` and `client_presentation_apply`, with `client_watch_host_from_behind`, `client_cinematic_end`, `client_host_camera_set` and the client's `players_vitality_keep`. With it, all of network_coop.c runs in Rust.
     - **What Rust does:** each tick, on a co-op client:
       - **the host's presentation** played one a tick, two ticks behind the newest heard (two a tick when further behind; restarting when far off): the players' input, the scripts' vitality and allegiances, the camera shake ended with the host's, the cinematic started or ended, the host's camera used while its scripts control it (or with nobody to watch), the letterbox, the active cluster;
       - **the camera:** the host's, placed by this machine's copy of the moving vehicle it films, or (with nobody to watch) eased behind and above the host's eyes, its up rebuilt when the look is too steep (`observer_up_from_forward`); its field of view held to an observer's;
       - **each new fade** started, backdated to when the host began it; the **skip vote's** tally; the **HUD** state;
       - every player's unit kept at the vitality the host's scripts set; the cinematic ended, the host's camera let go and input given back when the host is quiet for two seconds; the skip vote sent while a skip is offered.
     - **The game's, through calls:** each engine function the tick ends in (input, allegiances, the shake, the cinematic, the director's camera, the letterbox, the active cluster, the scripted camera, the fade, the HUD state, sending the vote). What it reads of the game (the cinematic in progress, the spectating, the shake) is gathered before the step (struct `coop_client_tick_inputs`). The real-math helpers it uses (`normalize3d`, `points_interpolate`, `vectors_interpolate`, `valid_real_vector3d_axes2`) are ported with it.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 (with the players' vitality set on the host and respawns) and b30 and a30 with a 40-second late join, every tick agrees on the joining machine, with none left out.
       - Five of eight planted errors were caught: the presentation delay, the catching up, the fade's backdating, the vitality kept and the active cluster. Three went unseen: the view behind the host's easing and its rebuilt up (no session left a player with nobody to watch), and the letterbox's flag (a30's cinematic always shows it).
       - **Rust in charge**, with the whole of co-op in Rust: on a30 the positions differ by a median of 12.2 to 12.5 cm (the C's 8.3 to 12.9), on b30 with a late join 4.0 cm, and both machines end with the host's count of items.

   - [x] AI sync in Rust (`halo_map::network_actors`, world steps 45 to 49, system `network_actors`): all of network_actors.c, how a client shows the host's AI.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **the host noting** (step 45) the control each actor gave its unit, and what it started once (an animation impulse, a melee attack, a leap, speech, a custom animation outside co-op), each numbered from one counter and kept for the next three entries;
       - **the host's tick** (step 46): each unit driven this tick (or fleeing, which moves without its actor) packed into an entry (control, a held burst's trigger, angles as yaw and pitch, seat, shields and health, camouflage, team, running blindly, position and axes); sent to each client as often as it is near its players, or to everyone when it starts something; units no actor drove dropped; the damage of units taking some (and once more when it ends) to every client;
       - **a client handling the damage** (step 47) and **the states** (step 48): each entry checked (an object it has from the host, a living AI unit, the control's ranges, the speech a sound, the animation in its graph, the position and axes), a feigning Flood form got back up, the control kept, what it starts kept if not played, the seat, shields and health, camouflage, team and running blindly set, and a unit off the host's position by more than 5 cm moved toward it;
       - **a client driving its units** (step 49), where the host runs the AI: each with the last control the host sent, playing what it started once; one the host let go of, or quiet two seconds, let go.
     - **The game's, through calls:** the send period (`network_objects_send_period`), sending, getting back up, seating, the damage state, moving a unit (`network_objects_reconcile`, its arguments in the client's table), counting a correction, `unit_set_actively_controlled`, `unit_control`, the actions, speech at the head and custom animations. The tables, the sends and the clients' machines (gathered before the host's tick) moved to network_actors_records.h.
     - **The steps' regions** hold only what they write and what their calls change that they read: the host's tick and a client's driving leave out the objects, and a client's states take only the units the entries name. Copied for each of their many calls, the whole object pool slowed a checking joiner to half speed, and the packets it dropped hid the damage messages.
     - **Results:**
       - In 100-second co-op sessions on a30 and b30 (with `(ai_place beach_lz) (ai_place beach_lz_marine)` on the host for a fight on the beach), every step agrees on both machines, with none left out: on b30, 131,072 notes, 2,048 host ticks (141,410 calls), 2,048 damage messages, 8,192 state messages and 2,048 drives (131,055 calls).
       - Twelve of thirteen planted errors were caught: the repeat count, the noted flag, the sent control flags, the yaw, the damage's end, the period's turn, the damage's fields, the trigger, the correction tolerance, how long a control is held, the played speech and the impulse's alignment. One went unseen: the throttle's rounding (the AI's throttle was 0 in every entry; bipeds steer by facing, and no AI drove a vehicle).
       - **Rust in charge**, with all of networking in Rust: on a30 the positions differ by a median of 10.4 and 8.1 cm (the C's 8.3 to 12.9); on b30 with the fight 2.9 cm with 1,059 corrections of the joiner's copies (the C's 3.0 cm and 1,056), and both machines end with the host's count of items.
     - **Left of the networking:** vote-kick (network_votekick.c) and voice (network_voice.c), below.
   - [x] The players' vote to kick a player in Rust (`halo_map::network_votekick`, world steps 50 to 53, system `network_votekick`): the decisions of network_votekick.c.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **the host taking a request** (step 50), a client's (from its own stream, at most one a second) or its own machine's: refused for votes turned off, no such player, the host's players, one's own players or machine, an address not known, another vote running, playing too short a time, the gap after a vote, a starter cooling down or a target protected, each with its notice to the asker alone; else a new vote, or a vote for the one running, recounted (a person once by address and hardware id, more than half of those who may vote and at least two), its notices as the count changes, passed (the machine kicked, kept out) or its count sent to each client;
       - **the host's tick** (step 51): each client's time played; the vote passed when its target leaves; each second recounted, passed, failed at 45 seconds (its starter cooling down, its target protected) or its count sent;
       - **a client hearing the count** (step 52), for the scoreboard;
       - **the host's server's questions** (step 53): a machine joined (its time played starts over), one joining kept out (kicked by a vote, or the one a vote runs against), and a new game ending the vote.
     - **The game's, through calls:** a machine's players' names, notices to everyone and to one machine, the kick, the count sent and the log's line. What the steps read of the game (the time, the settings, the machines in the game and the clients, each machine's real address and hardware id, the player named) is gathered before them (struct `votekick_inputs`). What the scoreboard and the console ask (the count as shown, a player's name, the host's kick and ban, sending a request) stays the game's.
     - **Testing it** takes three machines (a vote needs two votes besides its target's machine): a script runs a host and two joiners on loopback, the host announcing to both (`network.broadcast` takes a list), each with its own console commands at given ticks. `HALO_NETWORK_TEST_HARDWARE_ID` (new, p2p.c) gives each its own hardware id; on one computer they otherwise share one and count as one person.
     - **Results:**
       - In sessions on bloodgulch with `votekick_minutes` at 0 (a vote that passes, Player 3 kicked by the host's and B's votes; one that fails, then the refusals after it and a second vote) and at 5 (the time-played refusals), every step agrees on all three machines, with none left out.
       - All twelve planted errors were caught: the gap, the votes' minimum, the seconds left, the may-vote flag, the request's time, the remembered time, the time left told, the time played, a notice's text, the time a client heard, a machine's generation and the kept-out answer.
       - **Rust in charge**, with all of networking in Rust: both sessions' notices, kick and refusals are the C's, and in the failing one the positions differ by a median of 3.9 cm and the machines end with the host's count of items.
     - **Left of the networking:** voice (network_voice.c), below.
   - [x] Voice chat in Rust (`halo_map::network_voice`, world steps 54 to 56, system `network_voice`): the decisions of network_voice.c. With it, all of the netcode's decisions run in Rust.
     - **What Rust does:**
       - **each frame's update** (step 54): the voices' volume; outside a network game the session ended; the host's settings read as they change and told to each client with its key (again as they change, and every ten seconds); a client's settings forgotten unheard for 25 seconds; mutes of a machine whose players changed forgotten; a client's hello every two seconds; the microphone wanted (open mic, or push to talk from its first press) and, while talking (open mic: over the level, and 400 ms after), each frame encoded and sent, or relayed by the host;
       - **the messages** (step 55): a client's frame to the host, taken with its key, within its allowance (50 a second, ten at once) and the quality's size, from no more than eight talkers, and relayed to each machine that hears it (by the mode: the lobby's, teammates and enemies near or anywhere) and has said it hears voices, or played on the host; a frame relayed to a client, with its key, played (heard for being near: quieter further, from the speaker's side by this machine's camera); the host's settings and this machine's key, heard;
       - **a machine joining the host** (step 56): a new key.
     - **The game's, through calls:** sending (the messages' headers filled there), the microphone, its level, Opus's encoding, playing, forgetting the voices, the volume, push to talk and a new random key. What the steps read of the game (the time, the settings, the network game and its lobby, the players' teams, machines and living units' origins, the camera, the machines' names) is gathered before them (struct `voice_inputs`). Opus and the sound stay the platform's (voice_audio.c); what the menus and the scoreboard ask (who is speaking, the mutes, the icon) stays the game's.
     - **Results:**
       - In 50- to 70-second sessions with open mic and the test tone (`HALO_VOICE_CHAT=open_mic HALO_VOICE_TEST=1`) on bloodgulch (the default mode, and with voice off in games), and co-op a30 with `team_proximity`, every step agrees on both machines, with none left out, and both hear each other as the C's do (heard nearer and further, from either side).
       - All twelve planted errors were caught: the hello's interval, the settings' repeat, the farthest gain, the pan, the right vector's normalizing, the allowance, the settings' range, the route of enemies near, the speaker's player, the sequence, the new key and open mic's hold. Four needed a session where the players were heard from further than three units (proximity raised to 100, or co-op a30): where the bots stayed apart or together, nothing was heard by proximity.
       - **Rust in charge**, with all of networking in Rust: both machines hear each other (66 and 88 frames logged, gains 0.2 to 1.0); on bloodgulch the positions differ by a median of 6.4 cm and on a30 8.7 cm (the C's 8.3 to 12.9), and the machines end with the host's count of items.

- [x] **One executable** (`tools/package.py` → `dist\halo_rt.exe`, the `bundle` feature): the viewer carries OpenCE's halo.exe and SDL3.dll, the 32-bit halo_sim.dll and the DLSS runtime, unpacks them into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\halo_rt\runtime-<hash>`, and starts the game hidden with `game.rust_simulation = "all"` (new: every one of the 63 systems in Rust), at its menus or on a level named. Checked from an empty folder: a30 played path traced with DLSS Ray Reconstruction, all 63 systems in Rust, the game's HUD over it; the menus with no level named. Five levels (b30, a30, c20, d20, a50) ran a minute each with every system in Rust and the probe firing, with no panic and every probe check agreeing. The game still runs as a hidden 32-bit process under the viewer.

When nothing is left in the 32-bit process, the bridge goes away.

## Effects in the viewer (sub-roadmap)  ← *in progress*

Phase 5 had the game draw everything but the opaque world into an overlay: a flat picture over the traced one. That was cheap and faithful, but what's in it is never lit by the traced lights, never seen in reflections or through glass and water, comes after DLSS and the denoiser, and its multiplying blends meet a world the game no longer has (the smoky corridors and the flickering on a30's crashed Pelican). This moves what has a place in the world into the viewer, one kind at a time. Each step has a switch that leaves it to the game (`--game-…`), for comparison against the game's own on the same frame. The overlay ends up as only the HUD and menus.

1. **Fog**
   - [x] Atmospheric and planar fog (OpenCE `bevy-bridge-collision`, protocol 32). Each frame shares the fog the first player's window was drawn with, as `rasterizer_window_set_fog` leaves it (`struct bevy_bridge_fog`). With `game.bevy_bridge_viewer_fog`, the game leaves out its environment fog pass. The viewer fogs its tonemapped picture against the traced depth (`src/fog.rs`, `shaders/halo_fog.wesl`) with the game's equations. These were decoded from `_rasterizer_environment_fog_begin`'s register combiners and its two density textures: a ramp, and a smoothstep in distance times one in depth. They blend in the display's encoding, as the game's do. `--game-fog` turns it off.
     - Checked on b30 (atmospheric plus the sea's water fog) and c10 (atmosphere-dominant swamp fog), against `--game-fog` at the spawn: level surfaces within 1 to 3 levels of 255. Objects (trees, boulders, Marines) are now fogged as well, as the game's own model shaders fog them. The overlay never could, so in play they had stood out unfogged.
   - [x] Fog screens (protocol 33): the textured, wind-blown fog layers, such as c10's `fog_noise`. Under the overlay the game had skipped them altogether. Their motion (wind, strafing with the camera, rotation, zoom, random reseeding, the frames' animation) is the game's own state. So the game still moves them on each frame without drawing them: pass 0's work, now `rasterizer_environment_fog_screen_update`, shared by `rasterizer_environment_fog_screen_share`. It shares each layer's texture transform, frame weights and bitmap frame. The viewer draws them as `_rasterizer_environment_fog_screen_end` combines them: added in the screen's colour, times the surface's density from the first pass, or blended over all when the near and far densities are equal.
     - Every level's fog tags (a temporary probe): screens on a30's valley, b30's sea, b40's and c40's snow and pit fog, c10's swamp, d20's coolant and d40's dirty water. The water ones are dense (0.5 to 0.9) and show under water.
     - Checked on c10: the swamp's screen comes through, faint at the spawn just above the fog (layer weights 0.002 to 0.05). Still to check by eye: under b30's sea, and inside c10's swamp fog.
   - [x] The multiply's order: the game multiplies (decals) before its fog. With the viewer's fog, the overlay's multiply is now done in the fog pass, before the fog, and its node over the picture is gone. Before, distant darkening decals also darkened the fog over them. The pass now runs on every bridged frame, at zero density where the level has no fog, so the multiply still happens.
   - [x] Fog seen by the rays: reflections showed the surfaces they hit unfogged (b30's sea mirrored the distant structure dark and sharp beside its pale, fogged self). Solarik's `SolarikRayFog` (the same five fog values, from `follow_fog`) fogs a glossy path's first hit for the way beyond the surface it leaves, composed so that the fog pass over the picture, by the surface's depth, then leaves the hit fogged for the whole way (camera to surface to hit); paths to the sky stay unfogged, as the game's fog covers surfaces only. The fog's colour is taken as radiance at the exposure, before tonemapping. `HALO_RT_NO_RAY_FOG=1` leaves it out. Through water the fog pass already fogged what the water lets through (the plane's case); glass shows what lies behind at its own depth.
   - [x] Lit fog, optional (`--lit-fog <strength>[,sun][,only]`, or `HALO_RT_LIT_FOG`; Solarik's `SolarikRayFog::lit`, `realtime/lit_fog.wesl`): single scattering of the scene's lights along each pixel's view ray, through the game's own fog (its coverage taken as one minus the transmittance, the fog's hue as the medium's albedo). Each pixel lights one point, picked by the coverage's rise along the ray, with one light resampled from sixteen of the light tiles' candidates and shadow traced; the sun only with `,sun` (the game's fog colour already stands for daylight). DLSS-RR took the first version's light as the surfaces' and textured it with their albedo (c10's rock came out patterned), so the light now goes to a texture of its own, filtered by depth and accumulated over up to 24 frames by the motion vectors, and is added after the denoiser, before tonemapping. Over a surface it is divided by what the viewer's fog, drawn after, lets through. Off by default; 0.75 ms on c10 at 1280x720. Checked on c10's spawn: the swamp's haze glows around the level's emissive surfaces (at strength 10 strongly, at 3 clearly); `,only` shows the term alone. Not yet seen: muzzle flashes and plasma in a firefight, the strength that suits play (1 is physical), and how the 24-frame accumulation smears a moving light.
2. **Decals**
   - [x] Share the decals (protocol 34, `game.bevy_bridge_viewer_decals`). In `_rasterizer_decals_draw` the game now records each decal it would have drawn instead of drawing it: its quads from the decal vertex cache (read through the buffer's `Lock`, since the port keeps a physical address in `Data`), bitmap, layer, framebuffer blend, colour and fading intensity. Each frame carries those of the first player's rendered clusters, up to 2048 decals and 10,240 vertices (the game's own cache size). `--game-decals` turns it off.
   - [x] Draw them (`src/decals.rs`, `shaders/halo_decal.wesl`): a mesh per decal, kept from frame to frame by its datum index. They're drawn unlit in the transparent pass over the lit, traced picture, before DLSS and the fog, with depth bias, and each blend function is its own pipeline.
     - The colours are the Xbox combiners' (decoded from `_rasterizer_decals_draw`), in display encoding. Multiply and double multiply (blood, bullet holes, the levels' placed markings) become linear factors on the light. Add, max and reverse subtract (plasma burn glows, glowing symbols, some blood) become radiance at the camera's exposure.
     - An alpha blend multiplies toward its colour, since it would need the light under it. Only one decal uses it, an a10 floor marking.
     - Survey of every level's decal tags (a temporary probe): multiply 41 (blood and placed floor and wall markings), double multiply 19 (bullet holes), add 23 (burn glows, symbols), max 6 (blood), alpha blend 1.
     - Checked on a10's cryo bay: 15 decals, 1,164 vertices, at their places (bullet holes, blood and the bay's floor markings). With the depth test off (`HALO_RT_DECAL_DEBUG=2`) they show in magenta where they are, behind the wall the camera faces, and with it on they're correctly hidden. Still to check by eye in play: their look against the game's (blood, bullet holes, burn glows).
     - Not in the ray-traced scene, so reflections don't show them. Drawing them into the traced surfaces' colour is the next step for that.
   - [x] (Done, 21 October, protocol 44.) Retire the multiply layer (protocol 31) once nothing else uses it. 19 October's play sessions used it, for the game's transparent surfaces; the viewer draws those now (Transparent surfaces, below).
3. **Particles and effects**
   - [x] Sprites (protocol 35, `game.bevy_bridge_viewer_effects`): everything `build_sprites_end` draws, which is particles, particle systems, weather, glows and antennas. The game records each batch instead of drawing it. That's its quads (taken out of the camera's space), the effect shader's blend, flags and maps, the bitmap frame, and the centroid the game sorts it by: up to 512 batches and 32,768 vertices a frame. `--game-effects` turns it off.
   - [x] Draw them (`src/effects.rs`, `shaders/halo_sprite.wesl`). A pool of meshes holds each frame's batches, placed at the game's sort centroids so the transparent pass keeps the game's order. They're unlit, over the lit, traced picture, before DLSS and the fog.
     - The colour is the effect shader's combiners (decoded from `rasterizer_xbox_transparent_geometry.c`): the bitmap times the vertex colour, or the nonlinear tint (1 - v)t^4 + vt, faded by the vertex alpha toward the blend's neutral. The game's dumped effect vertex shader (`HALO_GPU_DUMP_SHADERS`) confirms the vertex colour is `oD1` and `oD0.a` is the fog's transmittance.
     - Checked on b30's landing against the game's own sprites in the same frame, with the game drawing them as well (`HALO_BEVY_BRIDGE_EFFECTS_ALSO_DRAWN`), its overlay's coverage in red (`HALO_RT_OVERLAY_DEBUG=1`) and these in cyan by their coverage (`HALO_RT_SPRITE_DEBUG=1`). The beach's blowing dust (`scenery\emitters\dust`, about 200 large sprites around the camera), the impacts, and the burning crash on the right cover the same places.
     - Found on the way: Bevy's materials apply the camera's exposure in their own shaders, so a colour written past them is already what shows. The decals' and sprites' added colours had been scaled by 1/exposure, about 1,000 times too bright.
   - [x] Contrails and lightning: their strips of world-space vertex pairs (`render_contrail`, `lightning_render`) are shared as sprite batches, each segment a quad, through `build_sprites_share_strip`, with the same effect shader fields. Seen in the log on b30's beach: the assault rifle's tracers and the needler's and plasma rifle's trails. Still to check by eye in play.
   - [x] Lens flares (protocol 36: a batch's `kind` and `tint`). The game still tests each flare's occlusion against its own depth. It then shares each reflection it would have drawn (`rasterizer_lens_flares_draw`) as the quad `rasterizer_widget_draw_sprite3d` would draw, facing the camera at the flare's depth in the world. (The view-to-world matrix's `forward` and `left` are the view's x and y: the screen's right and up.) Only when the viewer draws the fog too: the game adds its flares after everything, in the display's encoding, so they're added up in a texture of their own (`src/lens_flares.rs`, `shaders/halo_lens_flare.wesl`) and added in the fog pass, after the fog screen. The colour is the internal sprite's combiners: (1 - v) k t^6 + v t, added by the vertex's alpha.
     - Checked on b30's opening flyby against `--game-effects`: the Pelicans' engine glows match. Drawn as sprites into the HDR picture before tonemapping, they had come out far fainter over the sky.
     - Left out: the sun glow (`rasterizer_sun_glow_draw`), which blurs the framebuffer around the sun (under the overlay there's nothing there to blur), and hiding a flare behind the first-person weapon (the stencil).
   - [ ] Still the game's: the weather's own sheets, if any.
   - [x] Effects' own fog: the game fogs each effect by its own depth (the combiners' last stage), after its fog. Here the fog pass fogs a pixel by the surface behind the effect, so an effect in front of a distant fogged wall was fogged as much as the wall, and one against the sky not at all. The sprites now take the camera's fog (`HaloFog::layers`) and the scene's depth (the depth prepass): each fades toward its blend's neutral by its own fog's transmittance, and its colour is raised by what the fog pass will take from the surface behind (1 over 1 - that coverage, up to 4 times; none over the sky). An alpha-blended sprite over a fogged surface still lets a little more fog colour through than the game's would. Checked with `HALO_RT_SPRITE_DEBUG=3` (red its own coverage, green the raise) on b30's landing: the dust by the Pelican's door, in front of the far cliff, had been dimmed by about half and isn't now. `HALO_RT_NO_SPRITE_FOG=1` leaves it to the fog pass.
   - [x] Z-sprites (protocol 42: a batch's `secondary_radius`, the shader's secondary map radius times its zsprite radius scale). Every secondary map seen in play (b30 and a50 with the bot firing: `flash c generic muzzle`, `flash h shield impact`, `flash h pistol detonate`, `dirt burst`) is a z-sprite's z-map (`effects\zmaps\zmap_noisy_*`), none with the primary or in screen space. As the game's zsprite constants and texture stages (decoded from `rasterizer_xbox_transparent_geometry.c` and its dumped vertex shader), each pixel's depth is the view depth less the radius (no nearer than 1 cm past the near plane) times the z-map's green over blue, the z-map spanning each quad (the game's corner stream: (0,0), (0,1), (1,1), (1,0)); so a flash or burst bulges toward the camera instead of being cut flat by the surface it meets. The first-person weapon's are left flat, as the game leaves them. `HALO_RT_NO_ZSPRITES=1` turns them off. Still to check by eye in play: impacts on walls up close.
   - [ ] Secondary maps with the primary or in screen space (multiplying colour and alpha, with their own texture animation): none seen in play yet.
   - [x] The bright ones (plasma, explosions, muzzle flashes) light the scene: their tags attach the game's dynamic lights, which the bridge shares and Solarik traces.
   - [ ] Optional: particles lighting the scene by their own brightness, as emissive sprites (effects without a light of their own, and the glow's shape rather than a point). Mind the light samples they would take from the scene's lights.
   - [ ] What's left in the overlay is the HUD, the menus and text.
4. **Transparent surfaces**
   - [x] Survey (`halo_map transparent <map>`, every chicago, generic, meter and plasma shader): over the ten campaign maps, the levels' own 121 (103 generic, 18 chicago), the objects' and effects' about 174, and 7 meters and 4 plasma. Additive the most (226), then alpha blend (58). Map animation by time: 21 level, 84 object; generic stages' colour animation 31 and 82; map animation by an object function 39; fades by the view's angle 26; numeric counters 3; screen-space first maps 9; first maps scaled with distance 4; extra layers 2; cube map first maps 4.
   - [x] Drawn as the game draws them (`HaloTransparentMaterial`, `halo_transparent.wesl`): each map's transform from `shader_texture_animation_evaluate` (ported: `MapAnimation::evaluate`) and each generic stage's colour 0 between its two colours by its function (or the object's function A, times a change colour), evaluated each frame (`animate_transparent`); each framebuffer blend its own blend state (subtract, min, max, double multiply and alpha multiply add exact now); one-sided unless two-sided; the alpha test; the fade by the view's angle (perpendicular, parallel), its source and the camouflage; screen-space and distance-scaled first maps; numeric counters (the rifle's ammo: each digit's frame from the object's function, its base the frame count); fogged by their own depth as the sprites. An object whose functions drive a shader gets its own copy of its material; the bridge shares each object's function values (protocol 43, the first-person weapon's too).
   - [x] In play the viewer draws them (`game.bevy_bridge_viewer_transparent`: the game leaves chicago and generic out of its overlay; `--game-transparent` leaves them to it). Checked on b30: the shore's waves and the Pelican's engine beams as the game's, the rifle's counter reading the same (23).
   - [x] Meters (the plasma weapons' heat, the Warthog's, the flamethrower's): `_shader_type_transparent_meter`'s combiner program (`combiner::meter`) with its constants from the object's functions each frame (`MeterInfo::constants`: brightness, flash, value, gradient, extension), the map's zero alpha killed; its blend's constant colour (the tint) applied in the shader, tint mode 1's colour on what's there taken as its mean. Checked on a plasma rifle placed on b30's beach (`HALO_RT_PLACE` takes weapons now): the gauge's gradient and flash as drawn with every function 1. The game leaves meters to the viewer too.
   - [x] Plasma (the shields' flare when hit: Chief's, the Elites', the Monitor's, the Sentinels'): an object definition's modifier shader (`objects::modifier_shader`), drawn over the object's skinned parts every frame as the game draws it, invisible until its function (the shield's hit) lights it. rasterizer_xbox_plasma_energy.c's combiner program (`combiner::plasma`) over its two 3D noise maps (3D bitmaps decoded now: `BitmapInfo::decode_3d_rgba8`, the port's volume swizzle), sampled at the model's rest position (the game's units and axes, carried in a copy of each part's mesh as its colour) times their scale and moved along their direction with time; v1 its colour and alpha from parallel to perpendicular by the view's angle (Chief's: blue edge on, orange head on), its intensity the function to its exponent; added by its alpha. Checked on b30 (`HALO_RT_PLASMA_DEBUG=1` at full strength): Chief's orange veins, blue at his outline; none when unhit. The game leaves plasma to the viewer too, so with the HUD, menus and text its overlay holds nothing else.
   - [x] (Retired, protocol 44: the game leaves out any multiplying draw that would reach the overlay.) The multiply layer: none of 20 October's runs used it (the game logs `the overlay's multiplying blends shared` when anything does). Retire it once a play session confirms.
   - [x] Cube map first maps: reflection, object centred and viewer centred, sampled by the direction their type takes (68c0f47; a10's command displays).
   - [ ] Extra layers, the numeric countdown timer.
   - [ ] Animation time: the viewer's own clock, not the game's (phases differ from the game's).
4. **Found in play (hands-on, 2026-10-15 and 16; a10, a30, a50, b30, c10)**
   - Good: impact decals; smoke, dust and fog, including under b30's sea and in c10's swamp fog.
   - [x] (Fine now, 20 October, with the conical muzzle light.) Muzzle flashes too bright, with a strong falloff. That's both the flash sprites (`effects\particles\flash\...`, added, first person; probably the missing secondary map) and the weapon's dynamic light.
   - [x] The muzzle flash's light is a sphere centred on the player, not at the weapon. Muzzle flash lights shine every way (180 degree cutoff) from the weapon, at the camera in first person. A light shining every way within a world unit of the camera (the player's weapon's) is now a spot light 1 m out along the aim, full to 26 degrees and none at 69, at 60% of a point light's brightness. Protocol 38 shares each light's tag, the tag's flags, its object and whether that is the player's (first person). Muzzle flashes are now told by their tag (flag bit 5, or the name), not by distance: the player's spot lights along the camera's aim, others' 0.3 m out along their weapon's (in b30's beach fight the Covenant's and the Marines' point at each other). Explosions, grenades and bolts stay round. Checked in play: they look right.
   - [x] (Fine now, 20 October.) The plasma pistol's light is too bright and lingers. Its light tag's own animation (duration, intensity and colour over its life) should fade it, as if the plasma were cooling on the weapon.
   - [x] (Fine now, 20 October.) a30's crashed Pelican (the drop pod at the start) still flickers, with decals and effects in the viewer. So it isn't the overlay's multiply. Next suspects: its smoke sprites' sort order, or the wreck's own surfaces (z-fighting, or emissive or transparent parts).
   - [x] c10's flat panels (captured with F9) weren't sprites. They were its radiosity light cards (`c10light ...`, `c10_light_fungal_green`): transparent shaders with an invisible bitmap added, which only lit the lightmaps. The viewer had drawn every emitting shader as a solid surface. Now a transparent emitter lights the rays only, unseen by the camera.
   - [x] The ray-traced swamp water still reflected those cards, as pale shapes on the ground. No Solarik change needed: their ray material is now masked out everywhere (alpha 0, `AlphaMode::Mask`). Every ray passes through them (no reflections, no shadows from them), and Solarik's light sampling, which scales only blended emitters by their alpha, still takes their full emission. The lighting at a fixed c10 view is unchanged.
   - [ ] c10's hanging vines float against the sky. Either we see more than the game shows (its fog, its sky), or they're misplaced. Compare with the game's own drawing.
   - [x] Black patches on the blunt-tank Grunts (captured on a10): not their shine but the wrong image. The Grunt's bitmaps hold two images, the pointy variant's layout (0) and the blunt one's (1), and the model's shader entries say which (their permutation; the game takes `permutation % image count`). The viewer always took image 0, so the blunt backpack sampled the pointy layout's empty black space. Now each model shader's permutation picks its images, bakes included. Still to do: an object's forced permutation (actor variants), which the bridge doesn't share.
   - [x] a50's "plane" across the top of the view, moving with the player: its sky's fog band, a ring only ~3200 units across. At a metre per sky unit, it hid the level beyond it. The sky is now drawn at 100 m per unit (`HALO_RT_SKY_SCALE` overrides that).
   - [x] a30's view off the cliff (22 October): pale sheets with hard edges below the horizon, and the sky stretched there (in the menus too). The game draws the sky model round the camera at its world position (render_sky.c: the model at 1/1024 scale, at the camera's position times 0.999), so the model is seen from the camera's place in it, in world units; here it was seen from its origin. a30's sea, far land and river are finite sheets laid out about the level, and from the origin their edges came into view. The sky dome now moves the camera to its world position in the model (`follow_camera`); off a30's cliff the land, river and sea are one view. The camera's skybox, behind the drawn model, was the environment light's cubemap, whose directions below the horizon take the horizon's colour (`HORIZON`, so that rays out through the sea don't see black): it showed through the translucent layers streaked downwards (the menus' stars). The skybox is now its own composite of the layers in every direction (`SkyModelCubemaps::background`, +0.3 s at load). Left: the layers draw over their own composite (the game clears to the fog's colour), and a30 shows a thin black sliver where two of its land sheets meet; the environment light's cubemap is still composited from the model's origin. b40 has no sky model.
   - [x] a10's opening starfield smoothed away by DLSS-RR (22 October): RR denoises the whole picture, and took the sky model's stars, a pixel each, for noise (in a still of the cutscene's first shot, 165 star pixels against 521 without DLSS). Solarik's sky guides were Solari's (diffuse albedo 0, specular 0.5: a mirror); they are now the integration guide's (diffuse 0.5, specular, normal and roughness 0), which alone gave 181. The sky now goes to RR's transparency overlay, upscaled without denoising (`src/sky_overlay.rs`: where the prepass's and the main pass's depths are both clear, the picture at an alpha of 1, which takes in the transparent surfaces the game draws over the sky): 355, the starfield as without DLSS but upscaled. Bevy's DLSS node passed no overlay; bevy_solarik now vendors bevy_anti_alias (`ViewDlssRayReconstructionOverlay`). Silhouettes against the sky are a little harder-edged. `HALO_RT_NO_SKY_OVERLAY=1` leaves the sky to the denoiser. Not yet seen in motion by the user.
   - [x] a30's sky grey in play, clear in the level viewer (22 October): the sky's layers are transparent combiner materials, which fog themselves by their own depth (the effects' own fog), and drawn ~100 km off they took the full fog of the game's (bridged) cluster: clouds, ring and the sky's sea lifted toward grey. The game draws its sky unfogged (its own picture, captured from its window with `--show-game --no-overlay` and the player moved to the spot, `HALO_SIM_TELEPORT`). The sky's layers now read an empty fog buffer (`fog::no_fog_layers`). Left: the sky's sea (`water dusk`) is flat white where the game's reflects the clouds and ring (its first stage is a cube map reflection, which isn't drawn yet).
   - [x] The sky's sea reflects (22 October): `water dusk` is a `shader_transparent_water`, which the sky's layers had no material for (a plain white one). On a sky model the game draws water as its reflection cube map alone, opaque (rasterizer_xbox_water.c: no blending for the sky, and a model's group has no plane, so its tint is white), looked up along the view's reflection, bent by its ripples. `SkyWaterMaterial` (`shaders/sky_water.wesl`) does that: the cube map in Halo's axes (checked against the game's own picture: the ring's pink and the bright streak fall where they do in the game), and the ripples as rasterizer_water_build_bumpmap sums them (up to four bump maps, each tiled, drifting and offset, by their contributions; the sum tiled at the ripple scale and drifting), each mip level faded toward flat by its level over the last times the fade factor, with the game's level-of-detail bias, so that the far sea stays a mirror with a fine shimmer. halo_map reads the water's reflection map, flags, ripple maps, ripples and their mip settings (offsets from OpenCE's struct, added to the layout probe; regenerating the layout from OpenCE-bevy drops other constants, so they were added by hand). The game's sea is a little brighter, as is the rest of its picture. Water in the level itself is unchanged (ray traced).
   - [x] The level's water ripples (22 October; the user: b30's sea "sort of flat"). The sea is a thin pane Solarik ray traces; it reflected about its flat geometric normal. Solarik's new `WaterRippleMaterials` (`water_ripples.rs`, by `StandardMaterial`) gives a pane the water shader's ripples, carried in each material's surface detail (a storage binding of their own is one more than the primary glass pass may have); every ray that meets the pane sees its normal bent by them (`water_ripple_normal`, as rasterizer_water_build_bumpmap sums them: each map tiled, drifting by the scene's time, weighted; the sum tiled at the ripple scale), each map sampled at the level of detail of the ray's width and the sum faded toward flat by its level, so the far sea settles back toward a mirror. The camera's pane pass now always carries a ray cone for it, and reflects about the rippled normal (about the flat one where that would send the ray into the water). Compared with the game's own picture of b30's beach (its window, the player moved there): fine waves across the sea, as the game's; our sea is bluer (its tint), and the foam is the game's transparent layers. `HALO_RT_NO_WATER_RIPPLES=1` leaves it flat. (The primary glass pipeline takes over a minute to compile after a Solarik shader change, longer than any other; until then the panes are drawn raster, without reflections or ripples: scripted captures of glass need `--screenshot-after` of ~9000.)
   - [x] b30's sea, a flat teal plane since the fog moved into the viewer: the planar fog under its fog plane (the sea's surface, full at 0.15 units deep, teal) covered the ray-traced water, its reflection with it. The game fogs the seabed and draws its water's reflection over that. Where the viewer draws the water, the planar fog seen through the plane from above now covers only the light that came through (1 - Fresnel), and the atmospheric fog is to the surface.
   - [x] The shoreline juddering behind the camera in b30's flyby (the waves are the game's overlay): almost every overlay picture missed its frame (1106 in the opening). The game shares frame N and takes its picture after, but had often shared N + 1 by the time the viewer read, and the reader took only the latest, so N was never read, and the viewer kept an older camera under the picture. The reader now takes the frame before the latest first while it is there: no misses (`HALO_RT_SYNC_LOG` logs any).
   - [x] The needler's needles hidden above the horizon (b30): they're glass, which Solarik composites before the transparent pass, and the sky's layers, drawn in it, drew over every pane with only the sky behind it. Each pane now writes a depth-only sky mask, far beyond the level but in front of the sky (`glass_sky_mask.wesl`), so the level's own transparent surfaces behind it still show. Drawn first in the transparent pass (in the opaque pass it also kept the skybox from behind the panes, and they were composited over black: dark, ragged needles against the sky). The first-person weapon's masks write the pane's own depth instead (the game draws that weapon over everything), and the fog reads the main pass's depth, so the needles no longer take the fog of the sea floor behind them. Checked in play on b30.
   - [x] The Warthog's headlights, a glaring sphere on the Warthog: their light's cutoff is exactly 90 degrees, and only narrower cones became spot lights. Now up to 90 degrees is a spot light, the tag's falloff angle is the cone's inner angle (protocol 37 shares it), and a spot light's brightness grows with its reach linearly (from the flashlight's), not by its square. Then, in play, still high, too far back, too bright and too wide: the game's one light sits on a marker atop the windscreen and lit the hood. Others' spot lights now shine from 1.6 m further ahead and 0.6 m down (about the bumper), with 60% of the tag's cone (full to 36 degrees, none past 54) and half the brightness.
   - [x] The flashlight's hard edge, and its short reach: its full cone was 75% of the cutoff (34 to 45 degrees). Now full to the falloff angle (20) and fading to the cutoff (45), and lights reach 1.75 times their radius (Bevy's range window fades them out by then).
   - [x] The game halted throwing grenades on b30 ("object index #989 is unused", data.c), twice; the viewer sat on its last frame, looking frozen. Protocol 38's first-person flag walked a light's object's parents with `object_get`, and a grenade's light outlives the grenade. Now `object_try_and_get`. Checked in play: no halt. The viewer now shows a banner when the game stops (its process exited, or no frame for 15 seconds), with the game's last error from its debug.txt and where its logs are. Checked by suspending and killing the game.
   - [x] Marines never got into the player's Warthog (b30, after the beach), with the simulation in Rust; in C they did. `actor_dispatch_call` sent every call kind from the handlers' first (0x1041) up to the handlers, so the vehicle choice's (17 << 8) went unanswered when Rust asked: the vehicle's origin read as zero, about 60 units off, and no marine was ever close enough. Check mode missed it, as the game calls the vehicle choice directly. Found by bisecting the 63 systems (to `actor_dispatch`) on a scripted scene: `(ai_kill beach_lz)` at 2000, the player into `jeep` at 4500. Now the gunner and passenger fill, in the bundle too.
   - [x] Characters cast no ray-traced shadows (a marine in full sun on b30's beach had none; the Warthog did), so none from the headlights either. Solarik takes a mesh into its scene a frame after an entity is given it, and a character's bending parts got a new skin every frame they moved, so they were never in it (the path tracer showed them missing; skinned once, or every fourth frame, they appeared). Each bending part now has two entities taking turns: the new skin goes to the one not shown, while the other, skinned a frame before, is; when the nodes stop, the older leaves. Checked: the path tracer shows moving Covenant, and a marine's shadow falls on a tree in daylight.
   - [x] Then, in play: phantom shapes in the water (b30's opening Pelicans, and a hand by the rifle), and marines and Covenant flashing black where they bend while moving. The rays' skin is a frame behind the camera's. Skinned in world space, a fast Pelican's bending parts trailed the rest of it, and a moving marine's trailed its own surface, so rays leaving that surface hit it and it shadowed itself. The skins now follow the model's root node at once and are skinned relative to it, so only the bending lags. They sit further inside by how far each vertex moved (1.25 times, up to 5 cm), and are skinned once more when the nodes stop. Checked: no phantoms in the opening, running marines lit normally on the beach.
   - [x] A faint outline round the first-person weapon over the sea (there before the skins). The weapon's rigid pieces were in the rays' scene where the game poses them for the camera, and the sea, a pane Solarik traces from the camera, showed them round the raster weapon's edge; the fog made it stand out. The first-person arms and weapon are now the camera's alone (Halo doesn't shadow with them either), but their panes, which stay traced (the needler's needles). Found with a depth view in the fog pass: the dark outline was in the picture before the fog, gone with the weapon out of the rays.
   - [x] Blocky, unstable sun shadow edges outdoors (b30's beach after landing: a staircase that shrank with distance). With Ray Reconstruction off, the sunlit sand was a sparse scatter of sun samples. Solarik chose lights by estimated power, and weighed the sun as its illuminance over 1 m²: beside the level's emissive surfaces it was chosen under 1% of the time, so few pixels had a sun sample and the denoiser rebuilt the edges from them. Directional lights now take half of light selection together (Solarik `halo-rt` branch, `DIRECTIONAL_LIGHT_SHARE`; `SOLARIK_DIRECTIONAL_SHARE` overrides it, `SOLARIK_LIGHT_FLUX_LOG` logs the shares). Unbiased: each sample is still divided by its probability. Checked: crisp, smooth edges, RR on and off.
   - [x] Hidden models still in the rays' scene: Solarik's extraction ignored visibility, so the first-person player's own body (hidden, round the camera) shadowed the rifle, which flickered with leaking sun samples once the sun was sampled properly, and every object the game hides still shadowed and reflected. Entities whose inherited visibility is hidden are left out now. The rifle's worst flicker (95th percentile of frame-to-frame change, still camera) fell from 8.7 to 3.4.
   - [x] Black patches on running marines' armour (thigh and knee plates, boot soles), only while animating. Solarik built every new mesh's acceleration structure opaque, then found its material alpha-tested and rebuilt it a frame later, so a character's skin (a new mesh each frame) reached the rays a frame late. The viewer made up for it with two copies taking turns and an inset that grew with motion (up to 5 cm), which pushed thin armour shells' inner faces out through their outer ones, enclosing the drawn surface. Solarik now builds a new mesh non-opaque at once when an instance already gives it an alpha-tested material (`halo-rt` branch): on b30's landing, about 210 instances a frame were left out of the TLAS for a missing BLAS, now none. So one copy per bending part, in the pose the camera draws, 5 mm in; the running marines shade normally and cast their shadows.
   - [x] Grass washed out grey looking toward the sun (a30's fields): surfaces Halo gives no reflection had the usual 4% reflectance, and the sky's sheen on them greyed them at grazing angles. Now 1% (`MATTE_REFLECTANCE`), and Solarik's Fresnel lowers grazing reflectance below 2% as Bevy's rasterizer does (half for these, where it rose to a mirror at any f0): the ground in a sunward view of a30 is 15% darker and half again as saturated. (`shader_shine <map> [name]` in halo_map's examples prints shaders' reflection settings.)
   - [x] Covenant crates dusty: a model shader's specular mask faded roughness to matte (0.9) where it was dim, while it stayed partly metallic, which read as dust over most of a crate. Halo's mask dims a reflection, never blurs it: a model's unmasked parts are now semi-glossy (`MODEL_UNMASKED_ROUGHNESS`, 0.45), like plastic. (`shader_maps <map> [name] [dir]` in halo_map's examples prints a shader's maps' channel averages and dumps them; the crates' random change colours were already right.)
   - [x] A notch in Sgt Johnson's cap bill: triangles wound against their vertex normals were all turned to face them, but the four at the bill's root are a seam whose normals bend round its edge, and the game draws them facing down; turned up, they left a hole seen from below. Only triangles whose three normals all disagree are turned now; seams are drawn from both sides. (`model_obj` exports a geometry as OBJ; `model_lods` with `MODEL_LODS_TRIANGLES` prints the reversed triangles.)
   - [x] Master Chief armour in every a10 cryo tube: the viewer drew each model's finest level of detail, and the tube's (at 3000 pixels, never reached in play: the game gives the finest only to a cinematic's movie stars) holds the armour. Models now draw the finest whose cutoff is at most 960 pixels (`CINEMATIC_CUTOFF_PIXELS`); only the tube has one beyond, in every map.
   - [x] a10's alarm lights flooded rooms red: they shine every way through a gel (a cube map turned with the light) of two opposite discs, which the spinning lamp sweeps round; the viewer drew the light unmasked. The bridge shares each light's up (protocol 39), `halo_map::light_gel` reduces a gel to its discs (beam axis, falloff and cutoff from its profile), and a light shining every way through narrow discs is a spot light along each (`GEL_WIDEST`, at half its brightness, `GEL_LIGHT_SCALE`). The holograms' and the Monitor's gels spill everywhere and stay point lights. (The cube maps decode now: `Bitmap::decode_cube_faces_rgba8`; `halo_map light` prints a light's gel and beams.)
   - [x] No shadow of the player in first person: the game leaves the first-person unit out of its view, and the viewer left it out of the rays too (drawn, its body closed round the camera and shaded the first-person arms and weapon, which sit inside it). The bridge now marks the first-person unit and what it holds as its body (protocol 40, `OBJECT_FIRST_PERSON_BODY`); the viewer keeps it hidden from the camera but in the rays' scene with Solarik's new instance mask (`RaytracingMask::AWAY`), and rays leaving within 1 m of the camera (`SolarikNearRays`, `NEAR_RAYS_RADIUS`: the first-person models and the camera's own rays) skip it. Checked on b30's beach with the player teleported into the sun (`HALO_SIM_HS_COMMAND`, `beach_lz_banshee_flag`) and a detached camera: the Chief's shadow, rifle and all, on the sand; the first-person rifle unchanged and steady. (`HALO_RT_CAMERA_LOG` logs the game's camera in `--camera`'s form.)
   - [x] Camouflaged enemies a milky white shell: a raster-only blend lit by the sky probe (bright indoors too). They are now liquid glass in the rays' scene: Solarik's new refracting panes (`ALPHA_BLEND` with a `thickness`, `MATERIAL_FLAG_REFRACT`) composite for the camera the picture behind them bent through 0.6 m of glass by their rippling normals (`liquid_normal`, read from a copy of the lit picture), reflect what is round them by Fresnel, and, as glass, cast only a faint edge shadow. The first-person arms and weapon keep the old shell. Checked on b30's beach: sand, sea line and rocks bend through the body. Not yet seen indoors: actor teleports into shaft A's BSP failed.
   - [x] Dim rooms smoky in motion: Solarik dropped all of its lighting history (DI and GI reservoirs, the world cache) whenever the scene changed, and in a game it never stops: the local lights Bevy extracts change as they leave and enter the view (b30's dark room: 3, 5, 6 lights as the camera moves a metre and a half), the flashlight and the game's lights move and flash, emissive and occluding objects move. Rooms lit by bounces alone were left with a frame or two of history, sparse speckle that DLSS-RR smeared to smoke. Solarik's new `SolarikLighting::dynamic_scene` keeps the history through scene changes (its reservoirs translate light ids and age out, cache cells are traced again), as upstream Solari does; only camera cuts and level changes reset it. Checked without DLSS (unavailable in the session) on a scripted dolly in b30's shaft A (`--dolly`): the world cache stays filled and the lighting dense where it went black and speckled after each reset. `HALO_RT_SCENE_RESETS=1` restores the old behaviour; `HALO_RT_NO_DLSS=1` leaves DLSS-RR off; Solarik's `SOLARIK_SHADER_DEFS` adds shader definitions (`VISUALIZE_WORLD_CACHE`).
   - [x] Lights off screen lit nothing: Bevy frustum culls a light by the sphere of its range, and only the lights it keeps reach Solarik, so a light behind the camera lit neither what it shines on in view nor, through bounces, the room. The fixture lights and the game's pooled lights are never frustum culled now (`NoFrustumCulling`): b30's dark room keeps all 42 fixture lights where it had 3 to 6 as the camera moved; it is 6% brighter, a fixture behind the camera lights the alcove's back wall, and its noise is unchanged (the same frame-to-frame difference, still).
   - [x] Glowing projectiles light what they pass. The plasma rifle's and pistol's bolts, the Ghost's, Banshee's and Shade turret's carry lights in their tags without the dynamic flag: in the game only lens flares, lighting nothing, and left out of the shared lights. The game now shares a projectile's such light too, at its tag's colour and reach (`lights_port_list`), and the viewer lights it like any other. Rockets, needles and the Wraith's mortar carry no light at all; the viewer gives them their own (`PROJECTILE_GLOWS`: an orange exhaust, pink, blue plasma), round from the projectile's middle. Fuel rods, charged bolts and grenades already had dynamic lights. On b30's beach, 152 plasma pistol bolts, 204 plasma rifle bolts and 18 needles were lit in a minute and a half of fighting; a bolt passing the camera tints the player's rifle. Rockets and mortars are untested in play. Sentinel beams glow orange too (modelless, like the bolts, so the game shares them hidden: the glows take hidden projectiles); Scorpion shells are left dark by choice. Untested in play: no Sentinel fight could be staged by script. In play they gave no light: the beam's projectile crosses 40 world units a tick with a range of 30, so it never outlives the tick that fired it and no snapshot holds it; what is seen is its contrail. The game now shares each contrail's line after the lights (protocol 41, `BEVY_BRIDGE_LIGHT_CONTRAIL`: its middle, direction, half length, contrail tag) and the viewer lights the Sentinels' along its length (`CONTRAIL_GLOWS`, a light every 3 world units, at most 4). Still untested in play.

Seen in a10's hangar (Scorpions, Warthogs, marines matte where they shone) and on b30's beach (Chief's armour no longer chrome).

   - [x] Lights dimmed by tag (`LIGHT_DIMMING`): Guilty Spark's (`monitor dynamic`, reaching 5.5 world units, and brightness grows with the square) to 0.15; the grenades' glow to 0.7 and their explosions' (reaching 10) to 0.6. The flashlight's cone is 0.7 of its tag's and its brightness 0.8 (`FLASHLIGHT_CONE`, `FLASHLIGHT_SCALE`). Not yet seen in play.
